Delete higher carries the deleted levels' layers down to the level it keeps, after its own: each vertex there takes half the displacement of the vertex it became above plus half the mean of that vertex's neighbours', level by level, the same way the levels pass sculpting down, so setting a carried layer to 0 takes off what it put on the kept level (its detail finer than that level goes with the levels above, as their other sculpting does). Past 32 layers on the level the rest are baked, and the note counts them. They are saved with the scene (every level's, with names, strengths and visibility; files from before layers open without any). Not in HD windows or with dynamic topology; turning dynamic topology on bakes them. Rebuilds keep them, as Nomad's layers survive a remesh: Remesh, Decimate, Mirror and weld, Slice (both parts) and Make boolean (the base) give the new mesh the layers of the level they rebuilt from, with their names, strengths, visibility and recording, and each new vertex takes each layer's displacement at the nearest point of the old surface (blended over that triangle's corners), so the shape is unchanged and moving a slider afterwards moves the new mesh as it moved the old one. Mirror and weld gives the mirrored half the kept half's displacement mirrored, and keeps the seam on the plane. A new vertex takes the layers fully within two edge lengths (of the coarser mesh) of the old surface, less the farther it is beyond that, and none beyond four (a boolean's other objects, a slice's cap), so a slider leaves no step at the seam. The layers of the object's other levels go with them, and the note says how many the rebuilt object kept and how many went. Only new vertices near what a layer displaced are searched for on the old surface, so a layer over part of the surface costs little: one over a small patch adds 36 ms to a 1M remesh (0.3 s) and 0.3 s to a 10M one (4 s), and one over the whole surface 0.4 s and 6.6 s, on the 4-core VM. Meanwhile the old level stays in memory beside the new mesh, with the search's tree and 21 bytes a new vertex: roughly 1 to 1.5 GB more at 10M while it runs (an estimate, not measured), only when there are layers. 10M vertices (`build/bench --brushes`, VM): a Draw frame while recording is the same as without (1.2 against 1.3 ms); the stroke's end adds it to the layer in 2.3 ms, and a slider change on that stroke's layer takes 4.6 ms plus 3.5 ms for normals. A script adds one with `new_layer` and sets one with `layer 2 0.5` (or `layer 2 0.5 hidden`). Exported to glTF, the layers become shape keys (see Export).

Under the Layers section's More options, on the layer the select picks: Duplicate adds a copy just above it, hidden so the surface stays as it is (its eye shows it, doubling that work), named with " copy". Invert turns the layer's work the other way at the same strength. Merge down puts what the layer and the one under it show (each displacement times its strength, so a hidden layer adds nothing) into the one under it, at full strength and shown, and drops the layer, without moving the surface. Move up and Move down change the order (an undo step, since older steps name layers by their place). Bake all keeps every layer's work as plain sculpting, as one undo step. Wide strength (remembered) lets every strength slider go from -5 to 5, ZBrush's multiplier; a strength set past -1 or 2 shows on the wide range anyway. Layers hold sculpting only, not polypaint, so there is no split of sculpt and paint. Script: `layer_edit duplicate` (or `invert`, `merge_down`, `move_up`, `move_down`, `bake_all`) on the selected layer, `set layer_wide on`.

## Brushes, masking and symmetry

Seventeen brushes, picked on the shelf, in the brush picker (All at the shelf's end) or the brush pie, the first ten with keys 1 to 9 and 0 in this order:

| Key | Brush | What it does (Ctrl inverts) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Draw | Pushes the surface out along the average normal under the brush (in). |
| 2 | Smooth | Moves each vertex toward the average of its neighbours. Shift smooths with any brush. Its strength is a number of passes, as Blender's: a quarter of strength is one whole pass, so 0.5 (the default) takes two and 1 four, a part of one coming last (`sculpt.smooth_passes`). On an open border (a hole's edge) a vertex moves toward the middle of its two neighbours along the border, so the edge evens out along itself rather than shrinking into the surface. Its Options choose how it evens out: Smooth; Relax, which moves vertices only across the surface (along it, as Nomad's Relax), evening out their spacing and keeping the shape; or Polish, which takes the roughness off and keeps the volume (the HC Laplacian with Blender's Surface Smooth weights, 0.5 and 0.5, pulling each step back toward where the dab began: on a rough sphere smoothed all over twice at full strength Smooth loses 2.8% of the volume and Polish 0.01%); Alt held while smoothing (the Smooth brush or Shift with any) polishes, as ZBrush's alternate smooth. Polish takes about twice Smooth's time. Under More, Valleys or Peaks even out only the dents or the bumps, and Stable (`smooth_stable`) gives a mesh denser than eight edges a radius more passes and a coarser one fewer, as the square of the edges across the brush, up to eight, so a dab evens out about the same share of the brush at any density; presets and `.strokes` files (version 24) keep both (`smooth_mode`, `smooth_stable`). The library's Smooth stronger is strength 1: four passes. |
| 3 | Clay | Builds up toward a plane just above the surface, moving only what lies below it, so dips fill first and strokes leave flat-topped layers (digs). |
| 4 | Flatten | Pulls toward the plane through the area under the brush (pushes away from it). Options: Plane height and Plane depth (0 to 100% of the radius, both 100% by default, as Blender's Plane brush) say how far above and below its plane it reaches: height 0 only fills the dips (the library's Fill preset), depth 0 only scrapes the bumps (Scrape). |
| 5 | Inflate | Pushes each vertex along its own normal, so shapes swell evenly (shrinks). |
| 6 | Pinch | Pulls the surface together toward the brush's centre, along the surface (pushes apart). |
| 7 | Crease | Cuts a sharp groove: draws in with a narrow falloff and pinches toward the stroke (a sharp ridge). |
| 8 | Grab | ZBrush's Move: picks up what is under the brush where the stroke starts and drags it with the pointer, in the plane facing the camera, each vertex by its falloff weight. With Past the outline on, a press just past the model's edge takes hold of the nearest surface within the ring, so an outline can be pulled out from outside it (see Grab modes below). With dynamic topology on, what it stretched splits down to the detail size when it lets go. |
| 9 | Mask | Paints the mask (erases it). |
| 0 | Layer | Raises a flat-topped layer to one height per stroke (0.15 radii at full strength) along each vertex's normal: going over the same place again in the stroke adds nothing, and a new stroke adds one more layer, as ZBrush's and Blender's Layer brushes do (lowers). |
| | Snake hook | Pulls the surface out after the pointer for horns, tentacles and fingers: the point the stroke has reached follows the pointer in the plane facing the camera, and each step of the way, at most a quarter radius, drags what is under the brush along by its falloff, at the pen's pressure when it pressed (easing off as the pen lifts leaves the tip as thick). Nothing is raycast, so it leaves the surface; with dynamic topology on, the stretched triangles split as it goes, and at the stroke's end what its last pulls stretched splits too. Its Options, as Blender's snake hook: Pinch (-100% to 100%, 0 by default) draws what it drags in toward the line it is pulled along, by a share of each step over the radius and less near that line, so a horn thins to a point without collapsing; below 0 it magnifies instead, fattening the tip. Under More, Rake (0 to 100%) turns what it drags by how far the hook turned since its last step, about the axis it turned on, so a horn drawn round a bend twists with it rather than shearing flat; Elastic tip drags as the Elastic brush does, the surround stretching after the tip out to three times the ring, by Elastic's Volume. Settings `hook_pinch`, `hook_rake`, `hook_elastic`; presets and `.strokes` files (version 20) keep them. |
| | Elastic | Blender's Elastic Deform: drags what is under the brush as a block of rubber pulled at that point would move (regularized Kelvinlets, three scales as Blender's), so the part pulled keeps its shape and the surround stretches after it smoothly, out to three times the brush ring (faded out from twice it) with no edge to the falloff; the point pressed follows the pointer exactly. Volume (Options, 0 to 100%, default 80%) is how much of its volume the rubber keeps: Poisson's ratio from 0 to its physical limit of a half (Blender's slider goes on to 0.9, past which the pull turns against itself), so at 100% the sides thin as it stretches. It is a Grab otherwise: one undo step, symmetry, Shift and Alt mid-drag, Past the outline; it has no falloff curve, and Connected only, All depth, Same side, Silhouette and Topological do not apply. |
| | Morph | Brings the surface back toward the stored morph target, half the way a dab at full strength, so later work can be painted away in places (ZBrush's Morph brush). Without a target it does nothing. |
| | Paint | Polypaint: colours the surface with the Polypaint colour, moving nothing (takes the paint off). See Polypaint and polygroups below. |
| | Cloth | For the length of the stroke, the surface within 1.6 brush radii of where it began behaves like cloth (see Cloth brush below). |
| | Clay strips | ZBrush's ClayBuildup, from Blender's Clay Strips: lays flat-topped strips with square ends that turn along the stroke. The tip is a square laid on the area's plane with one side along the way the stroke goes (from the last dab to this one), its corners rounded by Tip roundness (0 square to 100% a disc, 20% by default) and its length along the stroke set by Tip scale (25 to 100%); the brush's falloff, focal shift and hardness are measured in it. Each vertex under a plane just above the surface rises toward it, most from the middle of its depth (Blender's z(1 − z) build-up), so strips fill the dips first and keep flat tops (digs). It starts on its own pen response, size p^1.5 never under 0.3 (Blender's). |
| | Trim dynamic | ZBrush's TrimDynamic, from Blender's Multiplane Scrape: cuts what is under it into two flat planes meeting along the stroke, each side pulled onto its own plane where it stands above it, for hard edges. Dynamic angle (on by default) reads the angle between the planes from the two sides' normals each dab, easing a fifth of the way to a new reading so a long stroke keeps an even edge, and reads 0 on a hollow (a flat scrape); off, Angle (0 to 160°, 60 by default) sets it. Ctrl turns the planes the other way, cutting a valley. Clay strips and Trim dynamic are not voxel brushes. |
| | History | A local undo, as ZBrush's HistoryRecall brush: brings the surface back toward how it was before an earlier step of the History, half the way a dab at full strength, only where it is brushed (see History brush below). |

History brush: by default it paints back to how the shown level (or HD window) was before the oldest step its History still holds, so in a fresh session it paints back toward what was opened. The Brush palette's History brush section, shown while the brush is held, names that step ("Before: Draw stroke, 4 steps back (the oldest held)"); Earlier and Later move it one step at a time (also in the palette, as History brush: earlier, later and use the oldest), Use the oldest goes back to the default, and a right-click or long press on a step of the History panel's bar chooses that step (see History timeline and time-lapse). A chosen step stays until it is undone or the History drops it; the next History stroke then says so and paints back to the oldest instead. The target is built when Earlier, Later or Use the oldest moves the point, or else at the first History press that finds none (a copy of the positions with the steps' saved positions laid over it, newest first: 12 bytes a vertex; on a 10M-vertex sphere about 0.1 s with four threads, the copy shared between them) and kept while its step is in the History, so the brush's own strokes, ordinary undo steps, do not move it. It reaches back neither through dynamic topology strokes (with dynamic topology on the brush does nothing and says why) nor past a change carried to this level from another (the levels bring each other up to date when the current level changes, and no step of this level holds that change): with nothing in reach it does nothing and says so. One target per level, not saved with the scene.

The Object palette's Morph target: Store keeps the selected object's surface as it is now, Delete drops it. There is one per subdivision level, kept in memory only (12 bytes a vertex: 120 MB at 10M), not saved with the scene, and none while dynamic topology is on, whose strokes add and remove vertices (turning it on drops the target). A Morph dab costs a little less than a Draw dab (10M: 0.22 against 0.31 ms in the same run). Script: `morph_store`.

Symmetry: X (the X key, or a tap on the view rail's Symmetry), Y and Z mirror every dab across the object's own planes, through its origin. Local symmetry (Local in the popover a long press on Symmetry opens, or the Symmetry card) puts those planes, and the radial axis, through the middle of the object's box on the level being sculpted instead, as ZBrush's Local Symmetry does; the middle is measured again only after work that changes the object. Radial symmetry (Radial in that popover) lays Copies (2 to 32, 8 at first) of every dab evenly round the X, Y or Z axis, and with mirror axes on too each copy is mirrored as well, up to 256 dabs a dab; the Symmetry button shows lit while radial is on with no mirror axis. Grab carries each copy with the pointer turned and mirrored the way that copy is, Lock plane turns its plane, and Clip along a line clips every image. Every brush, voxel clay and Clip use it; the mirror-only images give the very same results as before. Feather (in the same popover, on by default), as Blender's, weakens a dab and its images where they overlap near a plane or the radial axis, each by how much they overlap (an image whose centre is within two radii adds the share it is within), so a stroke along the middle builds the height it builds elsewhere rather than twice it; Grab and voxel clay, whose images never pile up, are not feathered. Settings: `symmetry`, `symmetry_local`, `radial`, `radial_count`, `radial_axis`, `symmetry_feather`; the exit stats' `symmetry=` reads like `x+local+radial8y`, or `none`. Each image costs about one dab.

## Polypaint and polygroups

**Polypaint** works as ZBrush's: each vertex has a colour, painted with the Paint brush in the colour picked in the Brush palette's Colour section or the Object palette's Polypaint section (Surface page), at full mesh detail (no textures or UVs). A colour has a coverage too: unpainted vertices show the material's clay, the brush lays paint over it as far as its strength and falloff reach (paint over bare clay is the paint colour from the first touch, so stroke edges fade into the clay without a dark fringe), and Ctrl takes coverage off again (Shift blurs it: see Blur and Erase below). The mask, hiding and Front faces only hold it back as they do any brush. Fill with colour paints everything that is not masked or hidden; Fill gradient paints it from the main colour at the object's top, as seen, to the secondary colour at its bottom (ZBrush's FillObject Grad); Clear paint takes it off the same part. Strokes and the three commands are undo steps (4 bytes a vertex of what they reach). Each subdivision level keeps its own colours: changing level carries what was painted since the level became current to the level you go to (a vertex continuing one below takes its colour, an edge vertex the average of the edge's ends, and going down a vertex takes the one above it), so a look at a coarser level and back leaves fine paint as it was, and a new level takes the colours of the one below. Colours are saved in scene files (for the current level) and exported: PLY as `red`, `green` and `blue` vertex properties, OBJ as three more numbers on each `v` line, both as the paint over white as far as it covers. On the GPU each vertex has 8 more bytes (colour and group) in a storage buffer the mesh shader reads, made only once an object has paint or groups. 10M vertices (bench, VM): a Paint stroke frame takes 1.0 ms against Draw's 1.3. Not in HD windows (whose detail leaves when they close) or with dynamic topology on (turning it on drops colours and groups; it is an undo step).

**Sculpt and paint channels**, as the reference app's sculpt and colour switches: the Colour section's Sculpt, Both and Paint (also the command Sculpt and paint, which a bar can hold) say what every sculpting brush changes. Sculpt moves the surface, as before; Both moves it and paints the main colour under the same falloff, alpha and symmetry in the same stroke and undo step (an inverted brush digs and still paints); Paint lays the colour and moves nothing, a Paint brush with that brush's falloff. Paint, Mask, Grab and Cloth keep to their own channel, and the wheel's Mask only masks. **Blur and Erase**: while painting (the Paint brush, or a sculpting brush on the Paint channel), Shift (or the wheel's Smooth, which then reads Blur) blurs the paint instead of smoothing the surface, each vertex's colour mixing toward its neighbours' at the Smooth brush's strength, and Ctrl (or the wheel's Invert, which then reads Erase) takes paint off at the brush's own strength and falloff instead of digging; neither moves the surface, and the History names them Blur and Erase. Both keeps Smooth and Invert as they were, since it sculpts too, and so does voxel clay. The Colour section's Smooth blurs, Invert erases switch (`paint_dial`, on at first) turns it off, so Shift smooths the surface while painting, as before; the exit stats print `paint_dial=` and `last_step=` (the newest undo step's name), and stroke files keep it (version 14; older files replay with it off, as they were drawn). Paint needs a level's own vertices, so in an HD window or with dynamic topology on Both only sculpts and a Paint stroke is refused, and voxel clay, which has no polypaint, refuses Paint strokes too. The **secondary colour** (under the Colour section's More options) is swapped with the main one by Swap colours: V, or the Colour section's button. Alt, which turns the view here, does not paint with it. The channel and the secondary colour are remembered (`paint_channel`, `paint_color2`); the exit stats print `paint_channel=`, both colours, `brush_subtract=` and `painted_vertices=`.

**The Colors card** (`src/app/kit_colors.odin`; design and sources in the project notes, `notes/colors/colors-card-design.md`) is where colours are picked, laid out and named as Procreate's Colors panel with ZBrush for iPad's Color card's strengths. A tap on the brush bar's Colour (or the Colors command, or Open Colors in the Colour section) opens it beside the brush bar; it stays open while you paint, its dots drag it, and holding them puts it back in the tray's Colour section. Panel options (the dots at its title's right) docks it on the left or the right as any panel (see Panels and columns): it asks its column for its whole height and takes its share, and the column's header then holds its Close. Its header holds the main and secondary colours (a tap picks which one the pickers set, ringed in the accent; a long press swaps them), Swap colours, Fill with colour, Fill gradient, the Eyedropper and Close. Tabs along its bottom pick how to pick, as Procreate's: **Disc** (a hue ring round a disc of every saturation, across, and brightness, up), **Classic** (a saturation and brightness square, then Hue, Saturation and Brightness sliders), **Harmony** (Complementary, Split Complementary, Analogous, Triadic or Tetradic from the list at its top; a disc of hue round it and saturation out from its middle with a reticle for each colour of the scheme, which a drag on the main one moves together, the Brightness slider, and the scheme's colours as swatches; a tap on another colour's reticle or swatch makes it the colour), **Value** (HSB or RGB sliders with their values in degrees, percent or 0 to 255, and the Hexadecimal code to read or type: three or six digits) and **Palettes**. Each slider's track shows the colours it runs through and its knob the colour it sets. Under the first four are the **history**, the last seven colours picked (a pick joins it when the finger or the pen lets go, a code is typed, a swatch is tapped or the eyedropper picks; a colour picked again moves to the front; Clear empties it), and the default palette. Palettes lists the built-in palettes (Skin, Creature, Clay and stone, Greys, Vivid) and up to eight of your own (28 swatches each); + makes a new one, which becomes the default; the circle at a palette's left makes it the default, its menu duplicates it or deletes one of yours, your palette's name is a text field, a tap on its empty slot saves the current colour there, and a long press on one of its swatches sets it to the current colour or deletes it. The list is as tall as the Disc and scrolls inside the card, so the card keeps its size and its tabs their place when Palettes is picked, as Procreate's panel does (the colour popover's list too). A swatch tap anywhere makes that the colour. Every swatch is a whole touch target (48 points), so a row holds seven; the card's body scrolls when the window, or the card's share of a column it is docked in, is too short for it. The **Eyedropper** (Shift+C, ZBrush's C) picks the colour of the surface under the pointer at once when the pointer is over the viewport: the polypaint over the material's colour, as it is rather than as lit, which is the colour to paint with. From its button (or the palette) it waits for the next press on the viewport instead: holding and dragging shows a loupe over the finger, its top half the colour under it and its bottom half the colour now, as Procreate's, and letting go picks; a release over nothing picks nothing, and Escape stops it waiting. Voxel clay and HD windows give the material's colour. The tab, the harmony, HSB or RGB, the default palette, the history and your palettes are remembered (`colors_tab`, `colors_harmony`, `colors_value_rgb`, `color_default`, `color_history`, `color_palette` lines); the exit stats print `colors_tab=`, `color_history=`, `color_palettes=`, `eyedropper=`, `color_popover=` and `window_colors=`. Every colour field elsewhere (the brush's main and secondary colours, Object > Polypaint, the material's and a group's colour, a light's, the background's) is a well of its colour with its hex code and Red, Green and Blue sliders under it: a tap on the well opens the **colour popover** beside it, toward the middle of the window, as Procreate's colour well does: the card's tabs along its top, the picker, the history and the default palette, setting that field's colour (a material's or a light's through the sRGB curve, as the field shows it), with a history pick when the finger or pen lets go. It stays open while you pick, over a section opened from the brush bar too; a second tap on the well, a press elsewhere or Escape puts it away, and it goes with its panel.

**Polygroups** split a mesh into parts, as ZBrush's do: a group number per triangle, shown in flat colours with View > Polygroups (Shift+W). Ctrl+W groups the masked part as one new group and clears the mask, or with nothing masked makes what is shown one new group (with nothing hidden, the whole object becomes one group), as in ZBrush. The Object palette's Polygroups section (Select page) has the same as buttons (Group visible, Group masked), and, under More options, Groups by creases (a group for each part of the surface between edges sharper than the Angle, 35 degrees by default: a cube's six faces, a hard-surface import's panels), Groups by normals (each group keeps within the Angle of the way its first triangle faces, so even a smooth curve is cut into facets), Groups by pieces (a group for each piece joined by edges, as ZBrush's Auto groups), Groups by UVs (a group for each UV chart of a retopologized object, on any of its levels: a triangle above the base takes its chart from the one it was cut from), Merge similar (groups of as many triangles whose areas are within 1% become the lowest numbered of them: a row of copies of one shape becomes one group) and Clear groups. Each groups only what is shown; hidden triangles keep their groups. On the test VM (release build, 4 threads) at 10M vertices Groups by pieces and by normals take about 2 s each and Merge similar 0.4 s. A Ctrl + Shift click on a group shows only that group; a second Ctrl + Shift click on it when it is all that shows hides it and shows everything else; with Alt as well, the click masks the group instead. A Ctrl + Shift click on empty space, or on an object without groups, still shows everything. Every change is an undo step, groups follow level changes as colours do (a triangle going up takes the group of the triangle it was cut from, going down the group of the middle one of its four), and they are saved with the scene. Drawing them costs no triangle data: each vertex carries one group to the GPU and each triangle is drawn starting from a corner that carries its own group, whose value a flat shader input takes, so borders are sharp; a triangle none of whose corners can carry its group shows a neighbour's colour (none in the tests; at most one in a thousand is allowed). While the view moves, far parts drawn from simplified copies may show ragged group borders. Script: `group creases` (or `visible`, `masked`, `normals`, `pieces`, `uv_charts`, `merge_similar`, `clear`), `polygroups on`, `set paint_color 0.2 0.4 0.9` (0 to 1), `paint fill` and `brush paint`.

Masking works as in ZBrush: each vertex has a mask from 0 to 255, and every brush moves it only by what the mask leaves (a fully masked vertex never moves). Masked areas are drawn darker. In the Object palette's Mask section, Clear (Alt+M), Invert (Ctrl+I) and Mask all change the whole mask at once; mask strokes and these commands are undo steps like any stroke. Blur, Sharpen, Grow and Shrink move the mask's edge, as ZBrush's BlurMask, SharpenMask, GrowMask and ShrinkMask and Blender's mask filters do, a step at a time: each vertex takes from the corners of its triangles their average (Blur), their highest value (Grow) or their lowest (Shrink), so a step reaches one ring of triangles further, and Sharpen pushes each partly masked vertex toward masked or unmasked against half a Blur step, which narrows a soft edge to a sharp one that stays smooth along the triangles. Mask cavities masks the creases and dents, as ZBrush's Mask By Cavity does: each vertex is masked by how far it sits below the average of its neighbours along its normal, measured against their distance, so grooves and pores go dark and flat or bulging parts stay free; Blur after it softens the result. Mask hidden (under More options, and Mask it on the action bar while something is hidden) masks every corner of a hidden triangle fully and unmasks everything else, the one mask command that writes hidden vertices: hide a part, Mask hidden, then Show all, and the mask is that part. Steps sets how many steps a press takes (3 by default): since a step is a ring of triangles, a denser mesh needs more for the same distance. The section shows what the last command did and how long it took. The mask is carried when the object changes subdivision level (a vertex that continues one of the level below takes its mask, a new edge vertex the average of the edge's ends), so it holds on every level. An HD window starts unmasked and its mask is dropped when it closes. The mask costs no GPU memory or upload: it rides in the low 4 bits of each of the two 16-bit halves of the vertex's packed normal, which still places the normal within 0.1 degrees.

Mask by, Mask adjust and Mask region (under the Mask section's More options) are ZBrush's Masking palette's automatic masks. Mask by masks the whole selected object, replacing its mask, by one of: Peaks (the crests, more the higher they stand above what is around them), Curvature (crests and dips alike), Smoothness (the flat and gently curved parts), Depth (the nearest part of it from the view fully and the farthest not at all), Facing away (what faces away from the view; edge-on parts half), Colour intensity, Colour hue (the polypaint near the main brush colour's hue) and Colour saturation (from the polypaint), Open borders (the edges of holes and of the shown part), Group borders (where polygroups meet) and Creases (edges sharper than the Polygroups section's Angle). Strength (for the first three) sets how much the shape counts, a curve takes what the kind measures to the mask, as the falloff editor does, and Blur (0 to 5 steps) softens the result in the same undo step. Mask adjust reshapes the mask as it is through its own curve, then blurs it: a steeper curve hardens a soft edge, one above the diagonal strengthens a partial mask. Mask region fills a closed outline of mask: on each piece of the object that has any mask, every unmasked part the outline cuts off is masked fully but the largest (by area), so a ring of mask fills on its smaller side; an outline with a gap fills nothing. Each is one undo step, leaves hidden vertices' mask alone (a Blur too), and says "nothing changed" where there was nothing to go by (no polypaint, a grey main colour for hue, no polygroups, no closed outline). The colour kinds and Group borders are refused in an HD window and with dynamic topology on, as polypaint is. On the test VM (release build, 4 threads) at 10M vertices Mask by takes 60 ms (Depth) to 240 ms (Peaks), 400 ms with 5 Blur steps, 0.5 s to 0.7 s for the edge kinds, Mask adjust 60 ms and Mask region 0.8 s. They are in the command palette and the gesture list (Mask region, Mask by facing away and curvature, Mask adjust), and the settings file and scripts keep the section as `mask_by_kind`, `mask_by_strength`, `mask_by_blur`, `mask_by_curve` (its two control points, `x y x y`), `mask_adjust_blur` and `mask_adjust_curve`. Script: `mask_by peaks`, `mask_adjust`, `mask region`.

Selection sets keep masks to bring back, as ZBrush's Selection Sets do (Object palette > Selection sets). Store mask as set (also in the command palette) keeps the selected object's mask under a name, "Set 1" and on, which its row's text field changes; Restore on a row makes that set the mask again, and for the set the list picks, Add to mask takes the more masked of set and mask at each vertex, Subtract takes the set off the mask, and under More options Store over replaces the set with the mask and Delete removes it. A restore is one undo step, like a mask command, and leaves hidden parts' mask as it is; storing, renaming and deleting are not undo steps, as in ZBrush. A set is kept by the level it was stored on and restores on any level, carried there as the mask is when the level changes; deleting levels carries the sets of the deleted ones to the current level. An object keeps up to 16. A remesh or any other rebuild (Decimate, Mirror and weld, Slice, Make boolean) drops them, and its note says how many went (Undo brings them back with the old mesh); turning dynamic topology on drops them too and says so, and they cannot be stored or restored with it on or in an HD window. The palette's Restore selection set 1 to 9 can be given keys (Settings > Keys). Sets are saved in scene files (version 10, which older versions of the app do not open), as runs of equal values where that is smaller, so a mask costs at most a byte a vertex.

Mask shapes work as ZBrush's: with the Brush tool, Ctrl and a left drag that starts on empty space (not on an object) draws a rectangle, or a lasso when the Mask section says so, and letting go masks everything of the selected object inside it, through to the back side; with Alt held too it unmasks. Each shape is an undo step. The polygon is filled into a 1024 x 1024 coverage grid first, so a lasso of any length costs a lookup per vertex: at 10M vertices a shape takes about 25 ms on the test VM, 2 ms at 1M (`build/bench --masks` has the rows). A drag that starts on an object is a stroke as before, and Alt + left drag without Ctrl still orbits.

Ctrl + Shift and a left drag with the Brush tool runs the gesture tool chosen in the Object palette's "Hide, clip and slice" section (remembered between runs): Hide rectangle (the default, as ZBrush's SelectRect), Hide lasso, Clip, Slice or Knife; the brush browser's Clip and hide tiles choose it too (see Brush presets). A Ctrl + Shift click without a drag shows everything again, whichever is chosen, or on a polygroup isolates it (see Polypaint and polygroups).

Shapes wait for Apply when drawn with a finger or the pen (Settings > Touch > Shapes, "Mouse at once" by default): a mask rectangle or lasso (Ctrl, or the rail's Mask, from empty space), a hide rectangle or lasso, and a Clip, Slice or Knife line stay on screen when the drag lets go, with a bar under them: Apply (Enter), Flip (Unmask, Hide inside, Other side; not for Slice) and Cancel (Escape or Back, once no menu or popover is open). White handles sit on a rectangle's corners and a line's ends, and a ringed one in the middle moves the whole shape; each takes a press within half a touch target, by a finger, the pen or the mouse, and two fingers landing on one still pinch or turn the view, leaving the shape where it was. A press anywhere else does what it always does, so the view can be turned, panned or zoomed under the shape before applying, and Apply uses the view as it is then; a new shape replaces it, and leaving the Brush tool lets it go. A shape drawn with the mouse applies as it lets go, as before. "Always at once" and "Always after Apply" make every pointer the same. Script: `set shape_apply auto` (or `now`, `confirm`).

Hiding works as ZBrush's: the hide tools draw a rectangle or a lasso (green), and letting go keeps only the triangles whose middle is inside it shown, through to the back; hold Alt as you let go to hide what is inside instead (red). Ctrl+H (or Show all) shows everything and Ctrl+Shift+H (or Invert) swaps hidden and shown; it never hides every triangle. Hide masked hides the triangles masked at least half at every corner, and Hide unmasked the others, to work on the masked part alone; the two are exact complements, so from a fully shown object Invert turns one into the other, and what is hidden already stays hidden. Both are in the Hide, clip and slice section and the command palette, with no keys until one is assigned. Hidden triangles are not drawn and no brush, mask, mask command (but Mask hidden) or clip moves a vertex all of whose triangles are hidden, and the brush and picking look through them; vertices on the edge of a hidden part still move. Each hiding change is one undo step. Each subdivision level keeps its own hidden part. Hidden parts are not saved in scene files, and hiding is refused in an HD window or with dynamic topology on (turning dynamic topology on shows everything); an HD window does not open while anything is hidden. On the test VM (release build) hiding half of a 10M-vertex sphere takes about 0.3 s. Script: `hide y` hides the + side of the selected object's middle across Y, keeping the - side shown (`hide -y` the other way; also x or z).

The Hide, clip and slice section's More options change what is shown a step at a time, as ZBrush's Visibility palette does. The edge of the shown part is every vertex with a shown triangle and either a hidden one or an open border among the shown (the edge of a hole). Grow shows each hidden triangle with a corner on a shown one, a ring more, and Shrink hides each shown triangle with a corner on the edge, a ring less (Grow then Shrink never hides what was shown first). Grow all shows the whole of each piece with anything shown (triangles sharing a vertex are one piece, as for Split to parts); Grow to groups the whole of each polygroup with anything shown. Outer ring keeps only the shown triangles with a corner on the edge. Show masked shows the hidden triangles masked at least half at every corner, so it undoes Hide masked. Grow and Shrink are also on the action bar's Hidden group and in the gesture list. Each is one undo step, refused like the rest of hiding when it would hide everything. On the test VM at 10M vertices (half hidden) Grow takes 20 ms, Shrink 0.24 s, Outer ring 0.32 s, Grow all 1 s and Grow to groups 60 ms. Script: `grow_visible`, `shrink_visible`, `grow_all_visible`, `outer_ring`, `grow_to_groups`, `show_masked`.

Slicing works as ZBrush's SliceCurve but makes separate objects: the Slice tool draws a line (cyan) across the viewport, and letting go cuts the selected object in two along the plane through the line and the view direction, through to the back (`geom.mesh_slice`, `src/geom/slice.odin`). Each part is closed with a cap of evenly sized triangles (as long as the edges the cut crossed, up to 100,000 triangles a cap), so both stay closed and can be sculpted, remeshed or exported; holes and several separate cuts (a torus cut through) are filled correctly. The part on the right of the drag becomes a new object beside it; the other stays selected. It works on the level the object is on and rebuilds it like a remesh: levels, HD detail, mask and hiding go, and symmetry is not used. One Ctrl+Z undoes both parts. On the test VM a 1M-vertex sphere slices in about 0.4 s and a 10M one (20M triangles) in about 6 s plus 2.4 s to set the two objects up. Script: `slice x` slices the selected object across the middle of its bounds along x (or y, z).

The Knife works as ZBrush's KnifeCurve: it draws a line as Clip does, shading the side that goes (the right of the drag; Alt, or Flip on a finger's line, the other side), and letting go cuts the selected object along the plane through the line and the view direction, through to the back, throws that side away and closes the cut with a cap as Slice does (`object_knife`), so what is left stays closed. Unlike Clip, which flattens and keeps every vertex, it removes the geometry; unlike Slice, nothing becomes a new object. It rebuilds the object like a remesh, as one undo step: levels, HD detail, mask and hiding go, the level's sculpt layers come across, symmetry is not used, and dynamic topology's mesh is compacted first. A line that misses the object, or would take all of it, changes nothing. Script: `knife x` cuts away the + side of the middle along x (`knife -x` the - side; y and z too).

Clipping works as ZBrush's Clip Curve: with the Clip tool chosen, Ctrl + Shift and a left drag draws a line across the viewport, shading the side to the right of the drag (hold Alt to take the other side instead; what is held when you let go counts). Letting go flattens every vertex of the selected object on the shaded side onto the plane through the line and the view direction, through to the back, so the part past the line becomes a flat cut face. Nothing is deleted: the triangles stay, lying in the plane (Remesh selected afterwards to tidy the cut). It mirrors with the brush's symmetry, leaves masked and hidden areas (partly masked ones move part of the way), and is one undo step. Not with dynamic topology on. On the test VM a clip takes 35 ms at 10M vertices for a small cap and about 150 to 250 ms when it cuts away half the mesh, most of it saving 60 MB of positions for undo; the Object palette shows the last one's time.

Stroke (the Brush palette; Dots, Rectangle, Spray, Roll, Drag dot, not shown for Grab, Snake hook and Cloth, which drag what they pick up): Dots lays dabs along the path, as above. Rectangle (Drag rectangle), as ZBrush's DragRect, lays one stamp where the drag starts and redoes it as the pointer moves: its radius is how far the pointer is from the start and, with an alpha, the image's top faces the pointer, so an alpha can be placed, sized and turned in one drag. Each redo first puts back what the stroke did so far from the undo copies it already keeps (`sculptor_stroke_restore`), so it stays one undo step and costs one dab a frame. It works with every brush but Grab and Snake hook, and not with dynamic topology, whose strokes change the triangles (those use Dots). Spray, as ZBrush's Spray, lays a dab every half radius scattered up to the Spread slider's share of the radius from the path on screen (0 to 1.5, default 0.6), each 75 to 100% of the size and 50 to 100% of the strength and, with an alpha, turned its own way, for texture without a visible repeat. Its random numbers come from the stroke's seed and the dab's step (SplitMix64), and the seed comes from a counter each stroke takes the next of, kept by the stroke recorder, so a replayed Spray stroke lands exactly the same dabs. Roll, as ZBrush's Roll, lays a dab every diameter, the first a radius from the press, with the alpha's top along the stroke whatever the alpha's mode, so an alpha tiles like a tape rolled out (stitches, zips, chains); without an alpha it is Dots at that spacing. Drag dot, as ZBrush's DragDot, lays one stamp at the brush's size where the press is and slides it with the pointer until let go: each move puts back what the stroke did (`sculptor_stroke_restore`) and lays the stamp where the pointer meets the surface as it was, so it stays one undo step and costs one dab a frame; off the model the stamp stays where it was. Not with dynamic topology nor on voxel clay (those use Dots). Spacing (Stroke > More, with Dots; `stroke_spacing` in settings.txt, 5 to 200% of the radius, default 20%) sets how far apart Dots lays its dabs, as ZBrush's LazyStep: wider for a dotted line of stamps, narrower for a smoother one at more dabs a frame. Even strength (on by default, `stroke_even`), as Blender's Adjust strength for spacing, scales each dab's strength by how much a row of dabs piles up at 25% (the spacing every brush was tuned at) over how much it piles up at the spacing set, both measured with the brush's falloff (`sculpt.dab_overlap`), so a stroke builds the same height at any spacing (within 2% from 10 to 50%, a test checks) and the closer default lays smoother strokes as high as before; it never takes a dab past full strength, and voxel clay, whose dabs do not pile up, keeps its own. A settings file from before version 4 holds 25%, the old default, and the new one takes its place once. Scene spacing (`stroke_scene_spacing`) measures the gap along the surface rather than on screen, as Blender's Scene spacing, so where the model turns away the dabs come closer on screen and stay as far apart on the model (down to a quarter, past which the surface is nearly edge-on). No back and forth (Stroke > More, with Dots, `stroke_one_way`; ZBrush's) lays dabs only while the stroke goes the way it first went on screen, so a zigzag lays parallel hatching (the library's Hatch preset, a Crease, has it on), and Directional (`stroke_directional`) only while it moves away from where it was pressed; the spacing walks on either way, so the dabs fall in step again when the stroke turns. Size fixed in the scene (Stroke > More, `size_world`, every stroke mode; ZBrush's Dynamic size, Nomad's Constant) keeps the brush the size on the model it had on screen when it was turned on: each pixel of the Size slider then stands for that many scene units (`size_ref`), so zooming in makes the ring bigger on screen and the stroke the same on the model, and dab spacing, the detail of dynamic topology and the ring off the model follow the ring's size there. Presets keep the two ways as the named values `one_way=1` and `directional=1`. Colour spray (with Spray, 0 to 100%, `color_spray`) makes each Spray dab's paint colour that share lighter or darker in each channel, drawn from the stroke's seed as the scatter is, for mottled polypaint that replays the same. Shift's Smooth and the rail's Smooth and Mask always stroke as Dots. Presets keep the stroke mode and the spread; settings.txt keeps them as `stroke` and `stroke_spread`. Script: `set stroke spray` (or `dots`, `drag_rect`, `roll`, `drag_dot`).

Front faces only (a switch in the Brush palette's Options, as ZBrush's BackfaceMask) makes every brush but Grab, and the mask shapes, leave the vertices whose normal faces away from the viewer, with a short fade across the ones seen edge-on, so a stroke on a thin part such as an ear, a fin or a sheet leaves its far side alone and a mask rectangle stops at the visible side. It is per vertex, not a visibility test: a front-facing part hidden behind another is still reached. With symmetry, each mirror image faces the mirrored way. Script: `front_only on`.

Lock plane (a switch in the same Options section, as ZBrush's Original plane and Nomad's lock) keeps the area the stroke's first dab found, its normal and middle, for the whole stroke, so a long Flatten or Clay stroke across changing ground flattens to one plane instead of following it, and a Draw stroke pushes one way throughout; a stroke joining one (Join strokes) keeps its plane, so a long Flatten laid in pieces stays on one. It applies to Draw, Clay, Flatten, Pinch and Crease, and to any brush with an alpha (which lies on that plane); Clay's offset and a leaning pen still apply on top, each mirror image takes the plane mirrored, and the switch greys out on the other brushes. Connected only (as ZBrush's topological auto masking) makes a dab work only on what joins the place it presses (the triangle under its centre) through the shown surface without leaving the brush, so a finger pressed against the next one, the far lip of a thin slit or a separate shell inside the brush stays untouched however near it is in space, and hidden parts join nothing; it holds for every brush but Cloth (masking and painting included, and Grab, which then picks up only the part joined to where it was pressed; Topological, below, also measures its falloff along the surface), and greys out for Cloth. Both are commands too (Lock plane, Connected only and Front faces only in the palette, for a key or a pie), presets keep them, and `.strokes` files store them. Lock plane costs nothing; Connected only finds what is joined on the worker threads, a BVH leaf each, and costs about as much again as the dab: a Clay stroke at 10M vertices takes 1.8 ms of brush time a frame with it against 0.7 without (release, `--bench-stroke=120`, 4-thread VM), and 8 bytes a vertex once first used. Script: `lock_plane on`, `connected on`. The same section has All depth for Grab (ZBrush's Move Infinite Depth): Grab then picks up everything within the brush's radius of the line from the eye through the press, however deep, so a grab on the front of a head moves the back of it too (All depth wins over Connected only, which greys out for Grab while it is on). And for Smooth, Smooth only: All, Valleys (only the vertices lower than their neighbours along their normal: dents fill, ridges stay) or Peaks (only those higher: bumps come down, dents stay); Shift smoothing with any brush smooths the same way, and voxel objects smooth everything. Settings `grab_depth`, `smooth_only`; presets and `.strokes` files keep both (a preset with either set is a line a build from before them skips, as one with a focal shift is for builds before that). A plain Grab with Connected only on (it is one switch for every brush) now moves only the joined part; `.strokes` files from before keep their Grab strokes as drawn.

**Grab modes.** Grab's Options section picks how the drag moves what it picked up, as ZBrush's Move, Blender's Grab and Nomad's Move between them do: Free (the default: as the pointer goes, on the plane facing the camera), Along normal (out along the surface's normal where it was pressed, as far as the pointer went toward where that normal points on screen, back the other way; with the normal facing the camera, up on screen is out) and Along surface (across the surface only, the normal part of the drag taken out, as Blender's Nudge). Holding Shift mid-grab takes Along normal and Alt Along surface for as long as they are held, as ZBrush's Move does, from where the grab started, so the move jumps to the new mode rather than adding to it. Under More: Normal weight (0 to 1, Free only, Blender's) blends a Free drag toward Along normal; Same side (Nomad's) leaves the vertices whose normal faces away from the press's, so a grab on a thin ear, lip or wing moves only the side it was pressed on; Silhouette (Blender's Grab silhouette) keeps whatever faces away from the way the drag goes where it was, so pulling an edge out stretches the outline instead of dragging the whole part. Only the pointer itself over the model starts a stroke, for every brush: a press off the model turns the view however much of the brush ring overlaps it, as in ZBrush. Past the outline (under More, off by default; Elastic has it too) lets a press up to the brush ring's radius past the model's outline take hold of the nearest surface point and drag it from there, as Nomad's Move does, so the edge of a shape can be pulled out from outside it. Grab's strength and Snake hook's keep the pen's pressure at the press for the whole drag. Topological (a switch under More, and the library's Move topological preset; ZBrush's Move Topological, Blender's geodesic falloff) measures the falloff along the surface from the press instead of in a straight line: a grab on one finger leaves the next one however close it lies, a grab on a lip leaves the other lip, and the falloff follows the surface round a fold rather than cutting across it. The press finds the distances once (Dijkstra over the edges of the shown triangles within the brush's reach, on one thread; the edges are gathered on all of them), which costs a few milliseconds on a large brush at 10M vertices (see the line under the brush table); the moves after it cost what a plain Grab's do. All depth wins over it, as over Connected only, which it implies. Settings `grab_mode` (free, normal or surface), `grab_normal_weight`, `grab_same_side`, `grab_silhouette`, `grab_topological`, `grab_outline` (Past the outline; settings and `.strokes` files keep it, presets do not), and Elastic's `elastic_volume`; presets and `.strokes` files keep them (version 17, which also keeps Alt in each frame, 18 Topological and 19 Volume).

Symmetry (X, Y and Z on the toolbar at the top; the X key toggles X) mirrors every dab across those planes through the object's origin, up to 8 images with all three; Grab drags each image the mirrored way.

Each brush costs about what Draw does (`build/bench --brushes`, 4 threads, this repo's cloud VM, the large brush; a frame is 4 dabs plus the normal update, and Grab is one move a frame):

| Brush | 1M: verts/dab | 1M: dab ms | 1M: frame ms | 10M: verts/dab | 10M: dab ms | 10M: frame ms | 10M: frame ms max |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Draw | 3875 | 0.031 | 0.20 | 38810 | 0.262 | 1.67 | 2.39 |
| Draw, spacing 0.2 | 3873 | 0.046 | 0.31 | 38790 | 0.319 | 2.35 | 4.09 |
| Smooth | 3936 | 0.047 | 0.27 | 39403 | 0.500 | 2.62 | 3.82 |
| Smooth, polish | 3936 | 0.337 | 1.44 | 39403 | 4.208 | 17.61 | 18.71 |
| Smooth, stable | 3937 | 0.500 | 2.10 | 39403 | 4.581 | 19.41 | 23.98 |
| Clay | 3914 | 0.030 | 0.19 | 39204 | 0.271 | 1.78 | 2.04 |
| Flatten | 3937 | 0.030 | 0.18 | 39414 | 0.287 | 1.80 | 2.69 |
| Clay strips | 4973 | 0.072 | 0.40 | 49822 | 0.659 | 3.66 | 6.66 |
| Trim dynamic | 2198 | 0.050 | 0.27 | 22018 | 0.356 | 2.04 | 2.52 |
| Inflate | 3871 | 0.029 | 0.18 | 38766 | 0.301 | 1.88 | 5.44 |
| Pinch | 4018 | 0.032 | 0.20 | 40240 | 0.319 | 1.92 | 2.39 |
| Crease | 4023 | 0.040 | 0.26 | 40294 | 0.332 | 1.94 | 3.65 |
| Grab | 3724 | 0.013 | 0.05 | 37162 | 0.117 | 0.44 | 1.27 |
| Grab, topological | 3064 | 0.017 | 0.06 | 30663 | 0.148 | 0.47 | 17.13 |
| Elastic | 36526 | 0.137 | 0.46 | 365837 | 1.593 | 4.93 | 94.07 |
| Mask | 3178 | 0.013 | 0.06 | 31831 | 0.107 | 0.55 | 4.59 |
| Layer | 3908 | 0.038 | 0.26 | 39148 | 0.741 | 3.47 | 7.23 |
| Layer, second stroke | 3908 | 0.037 | 0.24 | 39148 | 0.310 | 1.81 | 2.06 |
| Snake hook | 3813 | 0.021 | 0.15 | 38182 | 0.210 | 1.38 | 1.69 |
| Draw, X symmetry | 7734 | 0.083 | 0.56 | 77453 | 0.507 | 3.16 | 5.51 |
| Draw, half masked | 1931 | 0.039 | 0.26 | 19376 | 0.202 | 1.42 | 1.76 |
| Draw, Noise alpha | 3966 | 0.055 | 0.35 | 39727 | 0.371 | 2.07 | 2.56 |

The rows for Draw at spacing 0.2, Smooth polish and stable, Clay strips, Trim dynamic, Grab topological and Elastic come from a later run (2026-10-29), in which Draw took 0.29 ms a frame at 1M and 1.90 ms at 10M, so compare them with that rather than the Draw row above. Smooth's full strength is now four passes, and Polish and Stable cost more again (Stable up to eight passes on a dense mesh); Clay strips reaches its square's corners, √2 radii out; Grab topological's max is its press, which walks the surface (16.6 ms at 10M), and Elastic's its first move, which weighs every vertex within three radii. For Grab, verts/dab is what it holds and dab ms is one move. Layer keeps 8 bytes per vertex for the stroke's heights (80 MB at 10M), made the first time it is used; the first stroke pays for the memory being touched for the first time, later ones cost about what Draw does. Smooth costs more because it reads every neighbour of each vertex, symmetry costs a dab per image, and fully masked vertices cost almost nothing. Mask strokes skip the normal update and only send the painted vertices to the GPU again.

A vertex whose triangles' corners all share its value, fully masked or unmasked, stays as it is under every mask command, so after one quick check of every node a step works only on the nodes around the mask's edge, and the step after it only rechecks the nodes next to what moved. A press costs about one pass over the mask whatever the steps, then a little per step for the edge (`build/bench --masks`, 4 threads, this repo's cloud VM, a sphere masked on one side):

| Command | Steps | 1M: changed | 1M: ms | 10M: changed | 10M: ms | 10M: undo MB |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Clear | 1 | 499k | 1.0 | 5.00M | 14.5 | 6.0 |
| Invert | 1 | 998k | 1.4 | 10.00M | 20.8 | 11.9 |
| Blur | 1 | 3k | 1.8 | 11k | 10.8 | 0.2 |
| Blur | 10 | 20k | 11.6 | 64k | 61.4 | 0.4 |
| Sharpen | 1 | 3k | 1.8 | 11k | 10.5 | 0.2 |
| Grow | 1 | 1k | 1.5 | 5k | 10.3 | 0.2 |
| Grow | 10 | 18k | 7.6 | 59k | 43.6 | 0.4 |
| Shrink | 1 | 1k | 1.5 | 6k | 10.1 | 0.2 |

Before working only near the edge, a step at 10M took 85 to 107 ms and ten steps 750 ms. The undo holds only the nodes that changed, and the upload after a command takes under 0.3 ms unless it changes the whole mask.

## Voxel sculpting

The `voxel` package sculpts a volume rather than a surface, the way 3D-Coat's voxel room does: clay can be added anywhere, holes punched through, pieces merged, with no topology to stretch or tear. The field is a signed distance (negative inside, in voxels) kept only in a band of 3 voxels either side of the surface, in bricks of 8³ 16-bit values (4096 units a voxel, clamped at ±4 voxels); bricks that are all one clamp are tiles (a code in the chunk's table: Empty or Inside), so memory follows the surface's area, not the volume: a sphere 512 voxels across is 29k bricks and 36 MB, 1024 across is 119k bricks and 146 MB. The domain is 4096³ voxels in 128³ chunks, each made on first use with a table of its bricks and of its mesh nodes.

Brushes change the field a brick a job. Add and Subtract are a smooth union or difference with a stamp sphere whose cap protrudes a tenth of the radius out of (or into) the surface, cut to the brush's footprint: the stamp's own distances fill the band it moves into, so a dab can add more than a voxel of clay. Smooth moves each value toward the mean of its 27 neighbours, read from a window gathered before anything is written; Flatten clips to (or, inverted, fills up to) the plane under the pointer; Inflate offsets by at most a voxel a dab. A dab's serial pre-pass picks the bricks within reach, makes bricks of the tiles clay can arrive in and saves each brick once a stroke for undo; the jobs write their own bricks; the post-pass turns bricks that are all one clamp back into tiles and makes a brick of any tile beside a face the band has reached, so the surface never runs into a tile. Every new value depends only on old values, so one thread and many agree bit for bit. At a stroke's end the touched bricks (and the blends between stamps) get true distances back: voxels beside a sign change are kept, the rest solved by Godunov sweeps, one per corner-to-corner direction, each visiting the bricks in wavefronts (the bricks whose coordinates sum to the same level share no face, so a wavefront runs in parallel and reads its neighbours live, Gauss-Seidel across bricks as inside them); a brick a sweep left as it was is skipped until a neighbour changes, and the repair stops at the first sweep that moves nothing by more than a 256th of a voxel, usually the eighth.

The surface is meshed by surface nets a node (16³ voxels) at a time from a 20³ window: one vertex per loop of edge crossings in each crossed cell (the crossed edges joined through the faces they share), at the mean of the loop's crossings, its normal the field's gradient blended from the cell's corners, and a quad per crossed edge the node owns joining the four cells' vertices for that edge. The field keeps no saddle face, a 2×2 face of voxels with its two inside corners on a diagonal, which the surface would cross twice: after a field is built or read, and at a stroke's end over the bricks it touched, the inside corner nearer the surface is moved a hair outside, and again for any saddle that flip makes, so every cell face is crossed twice or not at all, every edge of the surface is in exactly two triangles and every vertex's triangles form one fan: the surface is closed and manifold, which `--check-mesh` reports. A sheet or neck thinner than a voxel loses a voxel to the flips, and a sheet one voxel thick on a diagonal dissolves. Between a stroke's dabs a saddle may stand for a few frames, with an edge in four triangles there, until the stroke ends. Each node owns a slot of vertices and a slot of indices in one mesh with a quarter headroom; a remeshed node that still fits rewrites its slot and the frame uploads only those slots; one that outgrew it moves to a hole or the end. Unused index slots are zeros (triangles on a dummy vertex), so the whole buffer can be drawn; a tenth of the slots is kept in reserve so the arrays grow (a new GPU copy) at a stroke's end rather than in the middle, and they are compacted when holes take over half. A brick dirties only the nodes that can see a change (a changed value within 3 voxels of the surface, and a neighbour node only when the change is within 2 voxels of its side). Border vertices are duplicated between nodes and agree bit for bit, so no seam shows. Each node's chunk carries the normal cone of its triangles, worked out from their face normals when the node is meshed, so the renderer skips the nodes facing away from the camera (`--no-cull` draws them all) as it does a mesh's chunks. Each vertex carries a code naming the cell it stands for as the node that owns the cell indexes it, its loop, and which neighbour of that owner made this copy; the conversion to a plain mesh (Convert to mesh, Export) welds the copies to their owners' vertices by those codes, each node in parallel, with no position sort, and gives the same mesh on one thread and many.

The mask lives in the volume too, as 3D-Coat's Freeze does: a brick may carry a mask brick of a byte a voxel (255 fully masked), and a masked voxel takes only the unmasked share of any dab's new value, so a masked patch holds still under every brush. The Mask brush (Ctrl, the rail's Mask, or the brush itself) paints the mask with the brush's falloff and strength (inverted, it erases), the mask rectangle and lasso mask every voxel of the band whose centre projects inside the shape (Front only reads the field's gradient), and Clear, Invert and Fill work on the whole clay; Cavity, Hidden, Blur, Sharpen, Grow and Shrink need a mesh and say so. Each surface vertex samples the mask field where it stands and the value rides up with the normal, so the mask shows as on a mesh. A brick that collapses to a tile drops its mask, a brick made from a tile starts unmasked, and undo saves the touched bricks' masks with their values. Convert to mesh hands the vertex masks to the mesh's sculptor, as Nomad keeps masks through a remesh; Res+ and Res− and a boolean drop the mask. Undo saves the bricks a stroke touched, 1 KiB each, as they were (or the tile they were) and swaps them with the live ones, so a step is its own inverse; 512 MB of steps are kept an object. The scene file holds the field chunk by chunk (tag `SVOX`, version 3: tile codes, then each brick packed after its byte count, a masked brick's mask packed after it; versions 1 and 2, whose bricks are raw, still read). A brick packs as one chain of its values in memory order, each sent as the change of its difference from the one before (a distance field is smooth, so most of these fit a byte) and equal values as one run (the clamp either side of the band), to 46 % of the raw bytes on a 256-across sphere, plain or sculpted (zlib at level 6 gives 48 %); a mask packs as runs; a brick or mask that would not shrink is written raw; the surface is meshed again on reading, and a damaged section is refused.

`build/bench --voxel` on this repo's cloud VM (4 cores, software everything; the mesh bench's spheres have about as many vertices as the surfaces here: 347k at 256 across, 1.39M at 512, 5.57M at 1024):

| Voxels across | Bricks | Field MB | First mesh ms | Vertices | Add dab ms | Flush ms (max) | Upload KB/frame | Mesh MB | Stroke end ms | Sweeps | Undo ms | Smooth dab ms | To mesh ms | From mesh ms | Res− ms | Res+ ms | Union ms |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 256 | 7k | 9.4 | 89 | 347k | 0.25 | 1.34 (2.07) | 545 | 25 | 39 | 8 | 11.1 | 0.26 | 23 | 236 | 73 | 74 | 335 |
| 512 | 29k | 36.0 | 265 | 1.39M | 0.78 | 3.77 (11.02) | 1666 | 99 | 127 | 8 | 31.4 | 0.65 | 78 | 922 | 273 | 323 | 1235 |
| 1024 | 119k | 145.5 | 1089 | 5.58M | 4.35 | 15.48 (52.09) | 26885 | 453 | 788 | 8 | 168.8 | 1.82 | 401 | 4755 | 1420 | 1676 | 6174 |

The stroke's brush is a tenth of the sphere's radius (13, 26 and 51 voxels), so the frame cost is the brush's footprint in nodes, not the clay's size; the 1024 row's upload and its worst flush are the one frame where the arrays grew. The rows are one run after the field booleans came in (the VM's timings wander by a fifth between runs; an A/B of the bench before and after the weld by codes, back to back at 512, put the codes' cost to the first meshing and the flush at about 5 %). A dab is 0.2 to 4 ms and the remesh of what three dabs changed 1.5 to 20 ms on four threads (3 to 4 times that on one), against the mesh sculptor's 0.3 to 1.1 ms frames at 1M to 5M vertices. The stroke's end is the band repair over every brick the stroke touched, then the remesh of those bricks' nodes: at 1024 the repair is 0.5 s of it (24k bricks, eight sweeps of 512 voxels each; before the wavefronts it took 0.9 s and had not settled after its eight sweeps), the saddle pass 0.06 s and the remesh 0.15 s; other runs read 772 and 837 ms. The conversion to a plain mesh was a sort of every vertex on one thread, 7 s at 1024; by codes it is 0.35 s. From mesh converts that mesh back to clay at the same voxels across, as Convert to voxels does: the ray casts along the three axes, the seeds and the band repair over the band. Res− halves the sculpted clay's voxels along each side (its band repair grows into the tiles in rounds) and Res+ doubles them again, a read of the old field with no repair; neither counts the first meshing of the new surface, which is the First mesh column. Union is a boolean on the field with a second sphere of the same size 1.2 radii over (Booleans below): every brick of the base under the other's band is read again at each of its voxels, trilinear in the other's field, then the band is solved again over them; the preview runs on a copy at most 256 across, the first row. None of it has been tried on Windows or a real GPU.

In the app a voxel object is a kind of object beside the mesh ones: Add object with As voxels on (Scene palette), the Objects card's Add > Voxel sphere, or `--voxels=N`, adds a sphere of clay of radius 1, N voxels across (the Scene palette's size picks N so the surface has about that many vertices: 500k is 304 across, 1M is 432, 5M and up 1024, the most). It is picked, orbited around, moved, turned and scaled, listed, copied, duplicated, deleted, hidden, ghosted and saved as any object (scene file version 13, which older versions of the app do not open: the clay's `SVOX` section stands where a mesh's levels go). Draw, Clay and Layer add clay (Ctrl, or Invert, takes it away), Smooth, Flatten and Inflate do what they do on a mesh, with pressure, symmetry, Spray and Line strokes and the brush ring on the surface; the Mask brush, the mask shapes, Ctrl+I and Alt+M mask the clay (above); booleans work on the clay itself (see Booleans below); the other brushes, hiding, levels, dynamic topology, HD, the rebuild tools, polypaint and layers need a mesh, and say so in a note rather than run. Each stroke is one undo step in the History with the mesh strokes (512 MB of steps an object, the oldest dropped past that). The Object palette's Voxels section (Build page; a voxel object shows only Transform, Voxels and Booleans) gives the clay's size and Convert to mesh, which rebuilds the object as a mesh of its surface with the node borders welded, as one undo step, for every mesh tool; Export writes that surface. For a mesh object the same section has Voxels across (32 to 1024, whole nodes of 16; setting `voxel_across`, 0 for the resolution the object's vertex count maps to) and Convert to voxels (also in the Objects card's more menu and the command palette), which rebuilds the object as clay of its shape: the mesh is laid on the voxel lattice, rays along every voxel row find where they cross it with the exact tests the voxel remesh uses, the winding number along x says which voxels are inside (overlapping parts merge, as a remesh does), a voxel beside a crossing gets the distance a plane through the crossings on its axes would have, and the band repair solves the rest, so the surface lies within a voxel of the mesh (a unit sphere round-trips within one voxel; a torus keeps its hole). An open mesh is filled where that winding is positive, as Remesh fills it. Levels, HD detail, mask, layers, paint and the polygroups' materials go, as one undo step; a mesh without volume is refused with a note. For a voxel object the section also has Res+ and Res− (also in the command palette), 3D-Coat's names: Res+ doubles the voxels along each side (up to 1024 across) with the surface kept where it is, the finer band read from the old field between its voxel centres, and Res− halves them (down to 32), the coarser band read at the old centres and solved again by the band repair, so detail finer than the coarser voxel goes; each is one undo step that drops the clay's own stroke steps, as a rebuild does. The lattice's domain is centred on the object and halves with Res+, so clay more than a quarter of the domain from the middle (sixteen times a unit sphere's radius at 256 across, eight at 512) is refused with a note rather than cut off. The exit stats add `voxel_resolution` (the largest in the scene). The surface is closed and manifold once a stroke ends (see the field's saddle faces above), so Convert to mesh gives a mesh every mesh tool takes; `--check-mesh` checks the welded surface after finishing any stroke a `--bench-stroke` run leaves open. Exit stats: `voxel_objects`, `voxel_bricks`, `voxel_field_mb`, `voxel_surface_mb`, `voxel_undo_mb`; `--check-mesh` checks the welded surface. The dabs, remesh and upload are the numbers above: on the VM a 128-across sphere (86k vertices) takes a Draw stroke at 2.4 ms a frame of brush time and 200 KB a frame of upload in the debug build. The Objects card's picture of a voxel object is its clay's surface, drawn again once it has settled, as a mesh's is. Not yet: the bench stroke's level steps.

## Model mode

Model mode builds and edits cages, polygon models with their edges shown, as ZBrush's ZModeler, Blender's edit mode and Nomad's primitives do; the cage itself is the `poly` package's half-edge mesh (Layout). The shelf's Model page, the shelf of the Model space (Interface), holds Extrude, Inset, Move, Delete, Insert loop, Remove loop, Bridge, Close hole and Weld, the cube and cylinder, and Apply to sculpt (the other actions and primitives are in the palette and the All picker); the Model tool's palette in the tray picks the action and its target, and a primitive and its segments (Segments for a cube, plane or quad sphere; Around and Along for a cylinder; Around and Tube for a torus; Around for a cone) and adds it. Add cage puts a cube, plane, cylinder, quad sphere, torus or cone where the next object goes, sized to the unit sphere, as one undo step; scripts: `add_cage cube` (or `plane`, `cylinder`, `sphere`, `torus`, `cone`), with the palette's segments.

A cage object is drawn as its polygons cut into triangles, shaded flat, with the cage's own edges as dark lines over it (not the triangles' diagonals): a line list drawn by the mesh shaders, pulled toward the eye so the edges win over the faces they border while the faces in front still hide the edges behind, which works on every device, also where the triangle wireframe cannot be drawn. Its polygroups are its faces'. It is picked, moved, listed, copied, hidden, exported and saved as any object (scene file version 26: the cage stands where a mesh's levels go, and its triangles are made again on opening; from version 27 with its selected faces).

The Model tool works as ZModeler's pen grammar does: an action says what a press does and a target says which elements it reaches from the one under the pen. Each action takes faces, edges or points, and the hover picks that kind: the face under the pen, the edge of it nearest the pen, or its corner nearest the pen. Over a cage, what the press would reach is lit before anything happens (faces teal, edges amber, points blue; the parts behind the surface faintly), with "Action · Target" beside the pen (a finger or a pen that does not hover sees the same once it presses); a press on a cage also selects it, and a press on empty space turns the view. The actions: **Extrude** (a drag pulls the faces out along their normal on a ring of new side faces, the faces keeping their distance from where they were where they meet at an angle; Ctrl gives the sides a polygroup of their own), **Inset** (a drag shrinks the faces inside their outline by an even width, stopping short of the middle; Ctrl insets each face on its own), **Move** (a drag moves the faces along their normal, the rest of the cage stretching to follow), **Delete** (a tap removes them, leaving a hole; Ctrl closes the hole with one polygon instead, for faces whose outline is one loop), **Polygroup** (a tap gives them the palette's polygroup; Ctrl a new one) and **Flip** (a tap turns them over; turning over part of a piece is refused, so its target starts as Island). The targets: Single (the face), Face loop (the loop through the face across the edge nearest the pen), Island (the whole piece), Flat island (the faces joined to it within 15°), Group island and Group all (its polygroup, joined or everywhere), Similar (every face with as many corners and an area within a tenth of its, Blender's Select similar) and Selected (the cage's selected faces wherever the press lands on it, or the face under the pen when none are). Edge actions: **Insert loop** (a tap on an edge puts a loop across every quad of its ring where the pen is, the share along the edge carried straight across; a drag slides it along the edges), **Remove loop** (joins the faces either side of the target's edges, Blender's Dissolve edges, dropping the points left between two edges), **Collapse** (shrinks the target's edges to points), **Spin** (turns an edge one step round its two faces), **Close hole** (one face over the border loop under the pen), **Extrude edge** (a drag grows a strip of quads out of border edges, in the surface's plane; Ctrl along its normal) and **Bridge** (press on one border and lift over another of as many edges: a band of quads joins them, turned to be least twisted). Point actions: **Weld** (press on a point and lift over another: the first merges into the second), **Connect** (press on a corner and lift over another corner of the same face: the face splits between them) and **Dissolve point** (a tap joins the faces round a point into one). **Select** (a tap, or a drag across faces, selects what the target reaches; Ctrl deselects) keeps a selection of faces on the cage, shown orange; the palette's Selection row (and the command palette; scripts `select_cage all`, `none`, `invert`, `grow`, `shrink`) selects all or none, inverts it, grows it by the faces beside it or shrinks it by its edge, each one undo step. Edits keep it on the faces that come from selected ones (an extruded face stays selected, its new sides do not), and the scene file and undo keep it. Edge targets: Single, Edge loop, Edge ring and Border loop, each action offering those that fit it (Insert loop always works on the ring; Remove loop starts on the loop). Each action remembers its own target. Symmetry (the Symmetry panel's X, Y and Z) mirrors every action across those planes through the cage's origin, as ZModeler's and Blender's mirror editing do: the hover lights the target's mirror images too, and the action works on both sides as one undo step. Each image is the element whose points lie at the mirrored places, so a cage built symmetric (every primitive, and whatever symmetric actions make of one) finds them all; an element that is its own image (a face across the plane) is worked once. Insert loop puts in the mirror loop and the two slide as mirror images, stopping short of meeting on the plane (a loop put in on the plane is its own image and stays there); Weld of a point onto its own image meets it on the plane; Bridge, Connect and Close hole leave an image an earlier step already joined. Tidy cage (palette, command palette; scripts `tidy_cage dissolve_flat`, `tidy_cage merge_close`, `tidy_cage apply_mirror`) cleans the selected cage up as one undo step: Dissolve flat joins faces within 5° of each other inside one polygroup (Blender's Limited dissolve), Merge close joins points closer than a thousandth of the cage's size, and Apply mirror makes the live mirror's image part of the cage (see the live stack). A drag starts past the slop and follows the pen along the faces' normal (Inset: as far as the pen has gone), the label by the pen saying how far it has gone; Shift snaps it to quarters of the faces' mean edge. A tap with a drag action (Extrude, Inset, Move, Extrude edge) repeats how far that action went last, as ZModeler's does, so a row of faces can be pulled out alike one tap each. After a drag, the action bar's Adjust (and Adjust last operation) sets how far it went by dragging the Amount sideways, in place on its own undo step, as Blender's Adjust last extrude does, until anything else changes the scene. Ctrl and Shift are the wheel's Invert and Smooth on a tablet. Each action is one undo step; one the cage cannot take (a cage left touching itself at a point, a hole one face cannot close) is refused with a note saying why, the cage as it was. Scripts: `model_action extrude` (or `inset`, `move`, `delete`, `polygroup`, `flip`, `insert_loop`, `remove_loop`, `collapse`, `spin`, `close_hole`, `extrude_edge`, `bridge`, `weld`, `connect`, `dissolve_point`, `select`, `crease`, `crease_point`, `bevel`) and `model_target face_loop` (or `single`, `island`, `flat_island`, `group_island`, `group_all`, `similar`, `selection`, `edge_loop`, `edge_ring`, `border_loop`).

The live surface: the palette's Surface row (and the command palette; scripts `cage_smooth 0` to `cage_smooth 4`, and `cage_flat`) draws the selected cage smoothed by Catmull-Clark subdivision, 1 to 4 steps (each four times the faces), as ZBrush's Dynamic Subdivision and Blender's Subdivision Surface do: the cage's edges are drawn along the smoothed surface, the hover lights faces, edges and points where they show on it, and every action still works on the cage, the surface following each drag live. Borders stay sharp and a border's corner stays put. Flat splits the faces without rounding, for hard surfaces that want more faces and no curves. Smooth and Flat are each one undo step and are kept with the cage in the scene file (from version 28). Creases keep edges sharp when smoothed, with the semi-sharp rules of OpenSubdiv and Blender: a crease of n keeps an edge sharp for n steps and then lets it round, which reads as a softer edge, and fully sharp never rounds. **Crease** (edges; it starts on the edge loop, Edge ring and Single offered too) creases what the target reaches fully sharp with a tap, or as sharp as its last drag set; a drag up or down sets how sharp, from none to fully sharp in five steps of 40 points (Shift: whole steps), the label by the pen saying how sharp; Ctrl takes the crease away. **Crease point** does the same to points, which then stay put as corners. Creased edges and corners are drawn violet over the cage while the Model tool is in hand, softer ones fainter. The palette's Creases row (scripts `crease_cage by_angle`, `crease_cage group_borders`, `crease_cage clear`) creases every edge whose faces bend more than 30°, every edge between two polygroups, or takes every crease and corner away, each one undo step. **Bevel** (edges; Edge loop, Edge ring or Single) turns the target's edges into rounded strips, as ZModeler's Bevel and Blender's do: press on an edge and drag up to set the width, sideways for more or fewer segments (one per 30 points, from 3 or the last round bevel's count, up to 12), and below where the pen pressed for a flat chamfer of one segment; the label by the pen says the width and segments, Shift snaps the width to quarters of the edges' mean length. Where several bevelled edges meet, each face between two of them gets one point a width in from both (Blender's miter) or the edges between slide, three or more close with a corner face, and a bevelled edge that ends at a point slides its sides along the edges either side; the rounded strips follow circular arcs tangent to the faces beside them. The width stops before any point would pass the middle of an edge it slides along. Edges on a border, or meeting a point on one, are refused with a note. A tap with Bevel repeats the last bevel's width and segments, and Adjust last sets its width.
