Mesh check (palette; the command palette's Cage check; script `cage_check`) colours what would trouble smoothing, printing or a game engine on the shown cages while the Model tool is in hand: open edges and polygons of five or more corners red, triangles yellow, faces whose corners leave their plane by more than a hundredth of their size pink, and poles (inner points where three, or five or more, edges meet) green and magenta. The line under the palette counts the selected cage's creased edges, corners, poles and open edges too.

Cutting and shaping loops: **Knife** (edges) cuts the cage along a stroke, as Blender's Knife drawn with a pen: draw across the cage, starting beside it if you like (with Knife picked a press on empty space draws rather than turning the view), and each face the stroke crosses in sight is split along it, a new point where it crossed each edge, as one undo step. Taps cut a path instead, as Maya's Multi-Cut does: a tap on an edge marks a point (drawn with a line to the pen), the next tap on an edge cuts from it to there through the faces between, and each tap after cuts on from the last until a tap off the cage. Under symmetry the cut is mirrored, and where a cut meets its mirror image inside a face they join at a new point. Creased edges a cut splits keep their crease in both pieces. **Circle loop**, **Space loop** and **Flatten loop** (edges; Edge loop or Border loop) shape the loop under the pen, as Blender's To Circle and Space evenly and ZModeler's Align do, moving its points without changing the topology: Circle rounds a closed loop into a circle in its own plane (through its points by their mean distance from its middle; Ctrl fits it inside, by the nearest), Space spreads the points evenly along the loop's path (an open loop keeps its ends), and Flatten moves them onto the loop's plane. A loop that cannot take the shape (Circle on a loop that stops at a pole) is refused with a note. Scripts: `model_action knife`, `circle_loop`, `space_loop`, `flatten_loop`.

The live stack: the palette's Mirror, Thickness and Bevel creases sections change how the selected cage is drawn without changing the cage, as Blender's Mirror, Solidify and Bevel modifiers and ZBrush's QGrid do, and every action still works on the cage under them, the stack following each drag live. **Mirror** (X, Y and Z toggles; command Cage mirror) comes first: it draws the cage's image across the plane through the cage's middle square to each axis that is on (X and Z make four copies), as Blender's Mirror modifier with Clipping and Merge does. A point within a thousandth of the cage's size of the plane is on the seam: it is drawn on the plane and both halves share it, so a half modelled up to the plane shows as one closed whole, and every extrude, move or loop on the half shows on the image as it is made. The image has no edges drawn and takes no actions. A face lying across the plane would make the halves overlap, so the mirror is left out with a note until a loop cuts it there. **Apply mirror** (under the toggles, or Tidy cage) makes the image part of the cage, the seam's points shared, and turns the mirror off, as one undo step. **Thickness** (a slider from Off to 20% of the cage's size) draws the cage as a shell that thick: the cage is one side and a copy the other, Out along its normals, In, or Centre (half each way), the copy kept an even distance away at bends, and each border is closed by a rim of 1 to 6 faces across whose edges are creased, so an open plane becomes a closed plate that smooths with crisp rims. **Bevel creases** (a Width slider from Off to 20% of the cage's size, 1 to 6 segments) draws every creased edge as a strip that wide, with a patch where several meet, as the Bevel action would, so Smooth rounds it to a tight fillet; under it a crease's sharpness only says which edges are bevelled. The bevel comes after Thickness, so a plate's rims are bevelled too. A creased edge that reaches a point where only two edges meet cannot be rounded, and the bevel is left out with a note. A drag of either slider is one undo step, and so is each segments or side tab. The hover lights the cage's faces, edges and points where they show on the stack (a bevel's strips belong to the face beside them, and its corner patches to none), and Apply to sculpt takes the stack as it is drawn. The stack is kept with the cage in the scene file (from version 31; the mirror from version 32). Scripts: `cage_mirror x` (or `y`, `z`; each turns that axis on or off), `tidy_cage apply_mirror`, `cage_thickness 0` to `cage_thickness 20` (percent of the cage's size), `cage_thickness_segments 1` to `6`, `cage_thickness_side out` (or `in`, `centre`), `cage_bevel 0` to `cage_bevel 20`, `cage_bevel_segments 1` to `6`; the exit stats are `cage_live_faces`, the cages' faces as their stacks draw them (0 when they are off), `cage_live_broken`, the stacks failing the poly mesh check (always 0), and `cage_mirrored`, the cages drawn with a mirror.

Parts: **Align parts** (faces; it takes the whole island) joins one part to another, as Shapr3D's Align does: press on a face of a part and lift the pen over a face of another, and the first part moves so its face lies on the other, centre on centre and facing it, turned about the join so its edges run along the other face's nearest edges, as one undo step. On another part of the same cage that part's island moves; on another cage the pressed cage moves whole (its place and turn, as the move gizmo would set them). A tap on the part just joined turns it one edge further round the join, and a Ctrl tap flips it to face the same way, passing into the other; any other change ends that. Symmetry is not used. **Spine** (faces; Single and the other face targets) grows the target along a path, as 3D-Coat's Spine and Blender's extrude along a curve do: press on a face and draw the path out of it, and on lifting the faces are extruded step by step along it, each step about half as long as their outline's edges, turning with the path without twisting, as one undo step. The path lies on the plane through the faces' middle along their normal that faces the view most squarely, so turn the view to see the faces from the side; a view straight onto them is refused with a note. A pen's pressure sets the size along the way: the faces keep their size while the pressure first rises, then follow it as a share of the stroke's middling pressure (from a twentieth up to twice), so pressing harder fattens the arm and lifting off tapers it; a mouse keeps the size. With Ctrl the path is an arc from the faces, leaving along their normal, to where the pen lifted. Under symmetry the faces' mirror images grow along the mirrored path. Scripts: `model_action align`, `model_action spine`; the exit stats `selected_x`, `selected_y` and `selected_z` say where the selected object sits.

Retopology by hand: the **Topo pen** (Model palette's action list; script `model_action topo`) draws a new cage over a sculpt, as Maya's Quad Draw, Modo's Topology Pen and ZBrush's Retopo brush do, each gesture one press, drag and lift. The sculpt is whatever shown object under the pen is not a cage (a sculpt, a voxel object, an imported mesh), so nothing ties a cage to it and a saved scene carries on as it was. A tap on the sculpt drops a dot there (blue); a drag from a point (a dot or a cage point) over two or more others makes a face of them in the order the pen met them (3 to 8 corners), turned to face out of the sculpt, and its dots become the face's points. The first face makes a new cage, "Retopo of" the sculpt, in its own teal colour and in the sculpt's place, as an added object; later faces go into the cage their points are on, or the selected cage, or the one made last. A drag from a point that meets no other point moves it over the sculpt, and lifting over another point welds them (the point it would weld to is ringed in pink while dragging; two points of one cage merge where the second is). A drag from a cage's border edge grows a quad from it out to the pen, its new corners on the sculpt under where the edge's ends moved to on screen (a corner landing on a point of the cage takes that point); Ctrl and the drag grow the whole border that far. Shift and a drag relaxes the points within the ring round the pen, each toward its neighbours and back onto the sculpt: started on a border point the border slides along itself with the inside pinned, started elsewhere the inside evens out with the border pinned; the drag is one undo step. A drag that starts on the sculpt, away from every point and edge, lays whole stretches of cage at once, as ZBrush's Topology brush and 3D-Coat's strokes do: an open stroke draws a strip of quads along it as wide as the brush's radius on screen (at least 12 pixels), one quad per radius; a stroke that comes back to where it started fills its outline with a grid of quads about a radius across, its sides from the outline's sharpest turn; and Ctrl and a drag stamps a grid patch over the rectangle the drag spans, its cells a radius across (up to 32 a side). Every point of a strip or patch lands on the sculpt or the stroke is refused with a note, and each is one undo step, into a new cage or the one the faces would go into. A Ctrl tap on a dot takes it away, and on a face deletes the face. Points sit a five-hundredth of the sculpt's size above it, and while the Topo pen is in hand every shown cage is drawn over the sculpt, faces a see-through teal, where all their corners are in sight, since a flat face between points on a curved sculpt dips under it. Each face, move, weld, grow, relax and delete is one undo step; dots are not, and are not saved. Exit stats: `topo_pen_dots`, `topo_pen_faces`, `topo_pen_welds`, `topo_pen_extends`, `topo_pen_relaxes`, `topo_pen_sheets` (strips and patches laid).

A cage is not sculpted: brushes, masks, painting, hiding, levels, HD, dynamic topology, layers and deformers say so in a note, or grey out, rather than change the triangles the cage is drawn with. Apply to sculpt (shelf, palette, command palette; script `apply_cage`) turns the selected cage into an ordinary mesh object, as one undo step, which every brush then sculpts. A closed cage drawn Smooth or Flat becomes subdivision levels, as ZBrush's Apply of Dynamic Subdivision does: its base is the cage's polygons cut into triangles, and above it are as many levels as the cage had Smooth steps, each one Catmull-Clark step of the one below with the cage's creases and corners (Flat only splits), so the top level shows what the cage showed and the levels below are there for big changes of form. Each polygon keeps its polygroup, and an all-quad cage keeps its quads for export and UVs. Model mode draws a smoothed cage's quads cut on the same diagonals the levels use, so nothing moves on Apply. An open cage becomes the one level it was drawn with (and says so); a closed one at Smooth 0 becomes one level too, but keeps its polygons, creases and quads, so its Subdivide makes Catmull-Clark levels. Such an object's Subdivide adds Catmull-Clark levels too, and its levels sync up and down as Loop levels do (see Subdivision levels); HD detail above its top level is still Loop's, and dynamic topology turns it back into an ordinary Loop object. The exit stat `levels_rule` says how the selected object's levels above its base are made (`Loop`, `Smooth` or `Flat`). Tools that rebuild an object from its surface (remesh, decimate, mirror and weld, slice, knife, repair, booleans, Convert to voxels, a Blender send that comes back changed) do the same, as part of their own undo step. Exit stats: `cages`, `cage_points`, `cage_edges`, `cage_faces`, `cage_ngons`, `cage_broken` (cages failing the poly mesh check, always 0), `cage_selected` (selected faces), `cage_smooth` (the most Smooth steps of any cage), `cage_creases` (creased edges), `cage_corners` (creased points), `tool`, `model_action`, `model_target`, `model_done` (actions made) and `model_refused`. `scripts/touch_check.py 'model_*'` runs the extrude and undo, delete tap, insert loop tap and slide, weld, symmetric extrude, symmetric insert loop, select drag, Ctrl deselect, tap to repeat, adjust last extrude, extrude on a smoothed cage, crease tap, drag, Ctrl tap and point, bevel round, chamfer and segments, mesh check, knife stroke, symmetric knife, knife taps, loop shapes, apply to Catmull-Clark levels, Edit cage, refused-stroke, live stack, align parts, spine, topo pen, topo strokes and live mirror scenarios.

## Voxel remesh (DynaMesh)

Rebuild (Object palette, Shape page) replaces the selected object's topology by what it is for, picked on its tabs: Even (this remesh), Fewer (Decimate, below) or Quads (Retopologize, below), each at the count its Density slider sets (1k to 25M vertices, or 100 to 1M quads), and its button runs it. While Density is held, and for 1.5 s after, or while a mouse is over it, the selected object shows a checkerboard of the cells that density gives, as Nomad's resolution preview does: for Even the very lattice the remesh will build on (each cell becomes about one vertex; the line under the slider gives the cells' width), for Quads squares of the mean quad's size. Fewer has none, since decimation is dense where the detail is. Cells smaller than about three pixels fade to an even tint rather than shimmer. The preview measures the object's area once per change of its mesh, spread over frames at about 1 ms a frame (a 10M sphere takes about 90 ms in all on this repo's 4-core VM, so its cells show after a few dozen frames); drawing it is a few instructions in the mesh shader, and it is never in a studio render or a time-lapse. `--density-preview=N` shows it held on, for an even remesh of N vertices.

Remesh (Rebuild's Even, or Ctrl+R at the object's own vertex count) rebuilds the selected object as an even surface of about that many vertices, as ZBrush's DynaMesh does: stretched triangles from Grab or long strokes become even again, overlapping or self-intersecting parts merge into one closed surface, and anything thinner than the lattice spacing goes. It keeps the shape of the level the object is on; its levels, HD detail and mask go, and Ctrl+Z brings them back (HD detail apart).

How it works (`src/geom/remesh.odin`): the object is laid on a lattice whose spacing gives about the asked-for vertex count (at most 4,096 lattice points along any axis, DynaMesh's top resolution). A ray along each lattice row finds where it crosses the mesh and which way, using exact fixed-point tests so a ray through a shared edge or vertex counts it once, and a lattice point is inside where the winding number is positive. Rays along y and z find where the surface crosses those lattice edges. Marching cubes then joins the crossings, with one rule on ambiguous cube faces that makes the result closed and manifold (every edge between exactly two triangles), and three rounds of relaxation along the surface even out the triangles. Rays, crossings and cells are stored only where the surface is, so memory grows with the surface's area rather than the lattice's volume.

`build/bench --remesh` (4 threads, this repo's cloud VM, `--prefault=3000`). Sculpt-ready is the same preparation as any new mesh of that size (BVH, chunks, normals). Edge spread is the 99th percentile and the longest edge over the median edge, before and after:

| Vertices | Remeshed vertices | Remesh ms | Sculpt-ready ms | Edge p99 / median before | after | Max / median before | after |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 500k | 504k | 160 | 67 | 3.38 | 1.59 | 4.5 | 1.7 |
| 1.00M | 1.00M | 298 | 154 | 3.38 | 1.59 | 4.5 | 1.7 |
| 5.00M | 5.03M | 1519 | 686 | 3.37 | 1.59 | 4.5 | 1.7 |
| 7.00M | 7.05M | 2434 | 919 | 3.37 | 1.59 | 4.5 | 1.7 |
| 10.00M | 10.06M | 4310 | 1315 | 3.37 | 1.59 | 4.5 | 1.7 |

The remesh itself runs on every thread; on this VM a large share of it is first touches of new memory, which are much cheaper on a desktop. Peak memory at 10M was 2.3 GB, the old and new meshes included.

## Project detail

**Project detail** (Object palette), after ZBrush's ProjectAll: the selected object's shown level takes the shape of the object chosen in Project from, and its polypaint too with Polypaint too. Each vertex looks along its normal both ways, as far as Reach (a share of the selected object's size, 0.03 by default), for the other surface, and moves to the nearer hit by Strength; the mask holds vertices back as it holds a brush. Vertices that find nothing stay put. It is one undo step.

By default each object is read in its own space, as baking does, so a copy made by Duplicate or Retopologize, which is put beside the original, projects as if it lay on top of it; As placed uses where the two sit in the scene instead. The usual round trip: duplicate a sculpt, Remesh or Retopologize the copy (the detail goes), subdivide it a level at a time and Project detail at each level from the lowest up, so every level holds what it can of the original. A 100k-vertex retopology projected from a 1M sphere takes about 0.3 s on the VM, the source's BVH included. Not under dynamic topology on the selected object. Script: `project 1` projects from the first object onto the selected one (any object's number, counting from 1, down the Scene palette's list).

## Curve and insert tools

**Curve** (C, or Curve on the toolbar; its settings fill the tray's first palette), after ZBrush's curve brushes: drag over any surface and, on release, a new object follows the path. It is made one of three ways. **Tube** sweeps a circle of Sides sides along it, capped at both ends; Lift sets how far its middle sits out from the surface, in radii, so 0 half buries it and 1 rests it on top. **Stitches** sets leaning bars across the path. **Zipper** sets interlocking teeth from alternate sides. Width is a share of the brush's diameter where the path began, so S resizes it. The path is resampled at even steps and smoothed three times, which takes out the hand's wobble. Each part is turned to the path's frame there (along the path, out of the surface and across it), so the result follows the surface's curvature. The result is an ordinary closed object: remesh it to sculpt it, or mark it Union for the booleans.

**Insert** (I), after ZBrush's insert mesh brushes: click a surface to put a shape there at the brush's size, standing on the surface's normal and turned with the view; drag before letting go to size it instead. The shape is any primitive or starter at Vertices (a starter is never under 15,000), or a copy of any object in the scene (Copy of ... in its list), fitted to that size. Sink pushes it into the surface by that share of its half size. **Mark it as** sets its boolean mark as it is made, so a cylinder marked Subtract, with Live boolean on, cuts a hole at once.

Each curve or insert is one undo step. The geometry is in `src/geom/curve_mesh.odin`. Script: `draw_curve tube` (or `stitches`, `zipper`: a wavy path across the middle of the view) and `insert_middle cube` (or another shape, at the middle of the view).

## Booleans and Extract

**Booleans** (Object palette), after ZBrush's Live Boolean: set "The selected object is" to Union, Subtract or Intersect on any object, and the marked objects apply, in scene order, to the base, the first object left unmarked. With **Live boolean** on, the result is drawn in their place at about Live vertices (200,000 by default) and made again whenever one of them is moved, turned, sculpted or remarked, once the mouse is let go; while you drag or sculpt the selected one it shows as it is. Subtract and Intersect objects are not drawn on their own, so select them in the Scene palette's list to move them. **Make boolean mesh** replaces the base with the result at about Result vertices (1M by default) and deletes the marked objects, all as one undo step. Marks are saved in .sculpt files.

**With a voxel base** (the first unmarked object is clay) the booleans run on the field, the way 3D-Coat's voxel room and OpenVDB's CSG do (`src/voxel/combine.odin`): each marked object is read at the base's voxel centres through the two objects' placings (a mesh mark is voxelised in the base's space at the base's voxel size first, as Convert to voxels does), union keeps the smaller distance, subtract the larger of the base's and the mark's negation, intersect the larger of the two, and **Blend** (0 to 16 of the base's voxels, 0 by default) rounds the seam with the Add brush's smooth minimum, as Houdini's SDF blend does; the band is then solved again where anything changed, so the result is clay at the base's resolution, closed and manifold, with no mesh intersection that can fail and nothing to retopologise. Intersect drops every brick outside the marks' box too. Live boolean previews it on a copy of the base brought down to at most 256 voxels across by Res− steps (kept while the base is unchanged), as Nomad previews on a coarser lattice; Make boolean mesh swaps the result into the base's clay as one undo step and deletes the marks; Live and Result vertices do not apply then. A voxel mark on a mesh base takes part as its welded surface. Script: `--voxels=96 --script="duplicate_object; boolean subtract; move 0.9 0 0; select 1; make_boolean"` cuts one sphere of clay out of another. The `build/bench --voxel` Union column is a union of two spheres of the row's size on the field.

How it works for meshes (`src/geom/boolean.odin`): both meshes go through the voxel remesh together, with B's crossings counted 4,096 times A's, so one winding number says whether each lattice point is inside A, B, both or neither, and the operation picks which count as inside. Any two closed meshes therefore combine, overlapping or not, into one closed manifold surface. The voxels round edges off at their spacing, so each vertex is then put back onto the nearer input surface along its normal when one is within a voxel, which brings back the inputs' detail and flat faces; only the seam where they meet stays at voxel resolution. Two 1M-vertex spheres: about 0.35 s for a 1M union on the VM; the live preview at 200k takes about a tenth of that.

**Extract masked** makes a new object from the selected object's masked part (mask over half), as ZBrush's Extract does: a shell Thickness thick (a share of the object's size, 0.02 by default) along the normals, its open edge smoothed so it does not follow the mask's jagged outline, closed where the masked part was one piece. Script: with `--spheres=2`, `select 2; boolean union; move 0.9 0 0; select 1; make_boolean` marks the second sphere Union, moves it onto the first and makes the mesh (`live_boolean on` turns Live boolean on); `set extract_thickness 0.05; mask_rect 0 0 1 0.335; extract_masked` masks the top third of the view and extracts it.

## Retopology, UVs and baking

**Retopologize** (Object palette, Rebuild's Quads; baking is in Build > UVs and baking) makes a new object of about Quads quads (4000 by default) lying on the selected object's shown level, and puts it next to it, as ZBrush's ZRemesher does: every face a quad, closed where the sculpt is closed, facing out. The way there is simpler than ZRemesher's, and its edge flow does not follow the sculpt's curves: the sculpt is decimated by quadric error (as Decimate does, so more faces go where the shape bends), neighbouring triangles that make a near-square quad without folding pair up (about three in four do), every face is then cut into quads at its middle and its edges' middles, as a Catmull-Clark step cuts, and twelve rounds relax each vertex toward its neighbours' middle along the surface and put it back onto the sculpt along its normal. About four vertices in five end up with four quads around them. The new object is ordinary: it can be subdivided and sculpted, and its base level keeps its quads (drawn and sculpted as two triangles each) for export and baking; deleting its lower levels or turning dynamic topology on drops them. It takes about 11 s from a 10M sphere (mostly the decimation) and under a second from 1M (4 threads, VM).

It comes with **UVs**: the quads are grouped into charts that face within 55 degrees of their first quad, each chart laid flat by projecting it onto the plane facing that quad (so no quad folds over), turned to its smallest box, and the boxes packed in rows into the unit square at the largest scale that fits, with 8 texels of a 4096 map between charts; a sphere gets about a dozen charts filling 70 to 80% of the square. New UVs lays them out again after the shape changed a lot; it is an undo step, and it removes the object's texture maps in the same step, since they followed the old layout.

**Bake maps...** asks for a folder and bakes the object chosen in Bake from (by default the one the quads came from; its top level) onto the selected quad object's UVs, at Size texels square (512 to 4096, default 2048): each texel's point on the quads casts a ray each way along the quads' smooth normal, as far as Reach (2 average quad edges by default), and the nearer hit gives the displacement (distance along the normal, outward positive) and the sculpt's normal there. It writes `NAME_normal.png` (tangent space with green up, OpenGL's way; the tangent frame comes from the UVs per vertex and chart and is blended across each face, as MikkTSpace does, though not to the bit), `NAME_displacement.png` (16-bit grey: 32768 is the quads' surface, 0 and 65535 the largest distance in and out), `NAME_displacement.txt` (that distance in the OBJ's units) and `NAME.obj` (the quads with their UVs). Charts' edges are grown 8 texels outward so filtering at seams finds no background. Both objects are read in their own spaces, so moving either changes nothing. 10M-vertex sphere onto 5,000 quads at 2048: 3.7 s, with every covered texel finding the surface. Script: `set retopo_quads 5000; retopologize`. Flags: `--bake=DIR` (on exit, from the first object onto the newest), `--bake-size=N`.

The same rays bake the **bake set**, as ZBrush's Multi Map Exporter and Marmoset do, each map a switch under More options (remembered): **Base colour** (on; `NAME_base_color.png`, sRGB) is the source's polypaint over the material of the face each ray meets (its polygroup's own material, else the object's), mixed in linear light as the viewport mixes it, and the material's colour where there is no paint; **Occlusion, roughness, metal** (on; `NAME_orm.png`, linear) holds ambient occlusion in red, and the met face's material's roughness in green and metal in blue, glTF's ORM layout; **Polygroup colours** (off; `NAME_id.png`) is an ID map, each group in its viewport colour. Occlusion casts **Occlusion rays** (16; 0 turns it off, leaving red white) from each texel's point on the sculpt, spread over the hemisphere above the face it met, and counts those stopped within **Occlusion reach** (20% of the source's size); the pattern turns from texel to texel by a hash of the texel alone, so a bake is the same on any number of threads. The base colour and ORM maps grow out from texels whose rays found the sculpt (the ID map takes the colour most of its neighbours have, so no new colours appear at a chart's edge), and texels no chart reaches show the object's own material. Paint done on a lower subdivision level is carried up to the top level first. A dynamic-topology source has neither paint nor groups to bake, and the note says so.

Every bake also writes **`NAME.glb`**: the quad object as triangles (each quad along the diagonal the viewport draws it with, which the bake rasterizes too), split where its UVs are, with normals, UVs, the tangents the normal map was baked in, and one material whose textures are the maps just baked, embedded (base colour, the ORM map as metallic-roughness and, when occlusion was baked, occlusion, and the normal map), so Blender, three.js or a game engine shows the baked look straight away. The normal map and the tangents are made together: a reader that uses the file's tangents the MikkTSpace way turns the map back into the sculpt's normals to within 2 degrees (a test bakes a bumpy sphere and checks every triangle); Blender recomputes its own tangents, which agree closely but not to the bit. Without the base colour map the material takes the source's colour, without the ORM map its roughness and metal. Checked with the Khronos validator (no errors or warnings) and Blender 5.2 (UV map, three pictures, normal map wired). 2048 texels onto 5,000 quads with every map (release, 4 threads): from a 1M sphere 1.9 s without occlusion and 8.3 s with 16 rays, from 10M 5.1 s and 14 s. Script: `set bake_base_color on`, `set bake_orm on`, `set bake_id on`, `set bake_occlusion_rays 32`, `set bake_occlusion_reach 0.1`, `set bake_reach 3`; a run with `--frames` reads no settings file, so a `--bake` run takes these from its start-up script.

**Texture maps on the object.** A bake also leaves its base colour, ORM and normal maps on the quad object it baked onto (the ID map stays a file), as one undo step, and the first bake of a run turns on Shading > Show textures, so the result shows at once in the viewport: the maps are drawn through the object's UVs with the same split mesh, normals and tangents the `.glb` carries, so the normal map turns back into the sculpt's shading (a baked low mesh's pixels differ from its sculpt's by 0.24/255 on average, against 2.3 without the map, on a 1,500-quad sphere from a 300k stroke). The Shading menu's texture views (also the palette and the gesture map; setting and script `set texture_view off|textured|normal_map|uv_checker`, remembered) are **Show textures** (the base colour times the object's tint, roughness, metal and occlusion from the ORM map, which also turns on the physically based lights; occlusion dims only the ambient light and the studio's reflections, as glTF has it), **Normal map only** (the object's own material lit through the normal map, to compare with the sculpt) and **UV checker** (8 by 8 cells laid out by the UVs, tinted by column and row, which needs no maps), each picked again for clay. An object shows its maps only on its base level, with no hidden parts, no dynamic topology and no HD, while polygroup colours are off, and not while a stroke is on it (it draws as clay then, at sculpting speed: a sculpted low mesh no longer matches maps baked from its old shape anyway); the maps are decoded on a thread of their own, so the object shows as clay for a moment after a scene opens, and the viewport shows a map at most 4096 texels a side (2048 on tablets), halving bigger ones. Thumbnails stay clay. The maps are stored in the scene file as the PNGs the bake wrote (scene version 18), live as long as the object's quads and UVs do (a remesh, dynamic topology or deleting lower levels drops them, and their undo brings them back with the quads), and count toward the undo memory limit when a step keeps them.

**Loading and removing maps.** The Object palette's **Textures** section (under Build, beside UVs and baking; for an object with quads and UVs) has the texture view as tabs (Clay, Maps, Normal, Checker, the Shading menu's choices), a line saying what the viewport holds of each map (its size, or none, loading or unreadable), and **Load base colour map...**, **Load ORM map...** and **Load normal map...** (also in the command palette as Load map), each a file picker for a PNG, JPEG, BMP, TGA or QOI image of at most 8192 texels a side, as painted in Substance Painter, Blender or Krita for these UVs; the image replaces that map alone, as one undo step, and the texture view turns on if it was off. The file is kept as it was picked (a JPEG stays a JPEG in the scene file); only its header is read at once, and an image that then does not decode leaves a note and shows without that map. The ORM map is glTF's: occlusion in red, roughness in green, metal in blue; the normal map is OpenGL's (green up), as the bake writes them. Under More options, **Remove maps** takes them all away in one undo step. Script: `texture base_color path/to/paint.png`, `texture normal detail.png`, `remove_textures`.

## Mirror and weld, duplicate

Mirror and weld in the Object palette's Modify topology section (as ZBrush's Mirror And Weld and Blender's Symmetrize) replaces one half of the selected object with a mirror image of the other, across the object's own X, Y or Z plane (the one brush symmetry uses), keeping the + side unless "Keep the - side" is on. Triangles crossing the plane are cut at it and the two halves are welded along the cut (`geom.mesh_symmetrize`, `src/geom/symmetrize.odin`), so a closed object stays closed; vertices within 1/100,000 of the object's size of the plane move onto it so the cut makes no slivers. It works on the level the object is on and rebuilds the object like a remesh: levels, HD detail and mask go, and Ctrl+Z brings them back. 100k vertices take about 0.1 s on the test VM. Script: `set mirror_axis 0; mirror_weld` (axis 0 x, 1 y, 2 z; `set mirror_negative on` keeps the - side).

Duplicate (the Scene palette, or the object list's right-click menu; Ctrl+Shift+D) adds a copy of the selected object beside it, with every subdivision level, the mask and dynamic topology (HD detail is not copied, as in a saved scene), and selects it; Ctrl+Z removes it.

## Mesh repair

Object > Shape > Modify topology holds the repairs, the best of ZBrush's Modify Topology and Mesh Integrity, Blender's Clean Up and Meshmixer's Inspector (`src/geom/repair.odin`, `src/app/repair.odin`). Close holes and Delete hidden are in sight; Leave the largest hole, Delete half, Weld distance with Weld points, Check mesh and Fix mesh are under More options. Each but Check mesh rebuilds the object as Mirror and weld does (levels, HD detail, mask and strokes go, the shown level's sculpt layers come across) and is one undo step with a note saying what it did; polypaint and polygroups stay where the vertices and triangles stay.

- **Close holes** caps every opening (a loop of border edges). Each cap is ear clipped seen along the loop's own normal, evened out to triangles about as long as the border's edges (as Slice's caps are), then raised off its plane as a membrane over the border, so a cap in a curved part meets it without a step; its new vertices take the colour of the nearest border vertex and every cap gets a polygroup of its own (ZBrush's way), so a Ctrl + Shift click on it shows it alone. Border triangles that stick out and face away from the cap (a jagged border left by Delete hidden) are taken off first, since the cap would otherwise fold back over them. A loop that crosses itself seen from every side is fanned from its middle instead. Leave the largest hole keeps the opening with the longest border (a neck or a base) open. 1M vertices cut in half close in 0.7 s, 5M in 1.4 s (release build, test VM).
- **Delete hidden** deletes what is hidden (Hide, clip and slice), as ZBrush's Del Hidden, leaving holes for Close holes. Not with dynamic topology, as Split.
- **Delete half** deletes the side of the object's own plane on the section's axis that Keep the - side does not keep, leaving it open for Mirror and weld or Close holes, much as ZBrush's Delete By Symmetry.
- **Weld points** joins each vertex to the earliest one no further than Weld distance away (a share of the object's largest side, 0.001 % to 1 %, 0.01 % at first), as Blender's Merge by Distance does, then drops triangles that collapse, duplicates and loose vertices.
- **Check mesh** changes nothing and says what is wrong: holes, edges of more than two triangles, edges between triangles facing opposite ways, triangles with no area, duplicates, loose vertices, inside out, and how many pieces; a sound object gets "closed" and its volume. 5M vertices take 1.1 s.
- **Fix mesh** drops triangles with a repeated corner, a corner past the vertices or not finite, duplicates (both of a pair facing opposite ways) and loose vertices, turns triangles to agree with their neighbours piece by piece (changing the fewer), and turns each closed piece that faces inward the right way round. Edges of more than two triangles stay (Weld points or a remesh is for those).

Scripts: `close_holes` (`set holes_leave_largest on` first to leave the largest), `delete_hidden`, `set mirror_axis 0; delete_half`, `set weld_distance 0.0001; weld_points`, `check_mesh`, `fix_mesh`. The exit stats print `repairs= repair_ms= repair_last=` and the last check's `check_holes= check_pieces= check_non_manifold= check_duplicates=`.

## Decimate

Decimate (Rebuild's Fewer, as ZBrush's Decimation Master) rebuilds the selected object with about the vertex count its Density sets while keeping its shape: edges collapse where that moves the surface least (quadric error, `geom.mesh_decimate`, `src/geom/decimate.odin`), so the triangles stay dense where the detail is and go sparse where it is flat, unlike a remesh, which spreads them evenly. It is meant for exporting a sculpt to other programs. A collapse that would fold the surface, pinch it or leave a needle is refused, so a closed object stays closed and sound (`--check-mesh` stays clean); among collapses that change the shape equally, as on a flat area, shorter edges go first. Large meshes are first thinned in parallel, a quarter of the triangles a round in 64 cells whose borders move every other round, then finished as a whole. It works on the level the object is on and, like a remesh, drops levels, HD detail and mask (Ctrl+Z brings them back). The section shows how far the surface strayed at most. On the 4-thread test VM: 1M vertices to 3k in 0.7 s; 5M to 100k in 8 s and to 10k in 3 s; 10M to 100k in 12 s. Script: `set rebuild_goal fewer; set rebuild_fewer 100000; rebuild`.

## Dynamic topology

Ctrl+T, or the switch in the Object palette's Dynamic topology section, turns dynamic topology on for the selected object, as Sculptris Pro in ZBrush and dyntopo in Blender do: each dab first splits the edges under the brush longer than Detail pixels on screen and, with Collapse short edges (on by default), collapses the ones shorter than 0.4 of that. The brush makes triangles where it goes, as many as the detail you see needs, so you can start from a coarse ball and pull out horns and folds without stretching anything. Since Detail is in pixels, zooming in makes finer triangles. On a dense mesh seen from afar the edges are already shorter than Detail, and with collapse on the brush coarsens what it touches; lower Detail or switch collapse off to keep the density. Every brush works with it, symmetry too; Mask leaves the triangles as they are, and so do fully masked areas. Smooth splits nothing, as Sculptris Pro's smooth only takes detail away: with Collapse short edges on it collapses edges under 0.6 of Detail (where other brushes collapse under 0.4), so smoothing over a patch an earlier close-up made finer coarsens it back toward the detail; with it off Smooth leaves the triangles as they are. Grab and Elastic drag the vertices they picked up and split nothing while dragging (a vertex made then would stay behind); at the release they split every edge round what they moved that is longer than Detail (the size the press had), longest first, then even the new vertices out along the surface, in the same undo step, as Sculptris Pro's Move does, so a long pull leaves triangles of the detail size rather than slivers. The exit stats give `grab_refine_splits` and `grab_refine_ms`. The object needs a single subdivision level and no HD level, and turning it on or off is an undo step of its own (its earlier strokes can no longer be undone one by one). Remesh selected works on a dynamic object and turns it off.

Detail is measured one of two ways, the tabs above its slider: Screen (pixels, as above) or Radius, a share of the brush's radius (2 % to 50 %, 10 % at first: Blender's Brush detail), so a small brush adds fine triangles and a large one coarse ones at any zoom. A partly masked area refines and coarsens less, in proportion to its mask (the edge's least masked end counts), and a fully masked one not at all. Under the section's More options: the held brush's *changes triangles* switch (off: it moves the surface but never splits or merges, for a Grab that should only move what is there), *Use global detail* (off: the brush keeps its own Screen or Radius and Detail, a copy of the global ones to start from, as its own pressure is kept), and *Detail follows the falloff*, which scales each edge's detail by the brush's falloff at its middle, so the triangles are finest at the middle of the brush and grow coarser toward its edge, where none split, and a stroke's rim blends into the surface around it (Sculptris Pro's adaptive size). Settings `detail_by_radius`, `detail_radius`, `detail_falloff` and `dyntopo <brush> <off> <own> <by radius> <px> <share>`.

Stretch repair: Snake hook splits before each pull, so its last pulls leave long edges, and Cloth splits nothing while it simulates; at the end of either stroke the edges where it pulled longer than its detail split, longest first, and the new vertices are evened out along the surface, in the stroke's undo step, as Grab and Elastic do at the release (`sculptor_stretch_repair`, `src/sculpt/stretch.odin`). Remove stretching (under the section's More options, and in the command palette; script `remove_stretching`) does the same over the whole selected object for slivers left from before (a brush with changes triangles off, say): every edge longer than twice the object's median edge splits, masked parts keeping theirs, as one undo step. The exit stats give `stretch_repair_splits` and `stretch_repair_ms`. On a sphere with nothing to split (`build/bench --dyntopo`, 4 threads, the cloud VM), Remove stretching takes 48 ms at 1M vertices and 275 ms at 10M, which is the time to measure the edges and look over every one.

How it works (`src/sculpt/dyntopo.odin`): the sculptor works per node as it does on a fixed mesh, but each node gets a page of the mesh's arrays (1,280 vertex slots and 2,048 triangle slots) and uses the first of them, so it grows and shrinks in place; unused triangle slots hold zeros, which draw nothing. A node whose page fills up splits in two along its longest axis, and the BVH over the nodes is rebuilt then, in well under a millisecond. Each vertex lists its triangles (up to 16), so splits and collapses only touch what is around them. Splits go longest first and cut each triangle across its longest side (Rivara's bisection): when either triangle beside an edge has a longer one, that splits first, even just outside the brush. Triangles stay well shaped however often an area is refined: no angle went below 24 degrees in our measurements, and a test strokes one ring 160 times and checks for angles under 15 degrees, folds and the whole layout every 20 dabs. A collapse is refused where it would fold or pinch the surface, give a vertex more than 12 triangles, make an edge longer than Detail, or leave its worst triangle thinner than before and under 0.3 on a scale where an equilateral triangle is 1 (2√3 times the area over the sum of the squared sides), so thin slivers do not pile up where the brush coarsens. Surfaces that are not closed work too (an imported scan, say): an edge on the border splits its one triangle, and collapses leave border vertices where they are, so the border keeps its shape. Turning it on is refused on a mesh with an edge shared by more than two triangles or with triangles facing opposite ways across an edge. A dab makes at most 2,048 splits and 2,048 collapses, and the app stops laying dabs for a frame once they have taken 10 ms, so a large brush over a coarse area keeps the frame rate and catches up over the next frames. Undo saves each node a stroke changes, whole, before its first change, and swaps it back. The GPU gets the index pages that changed along with the changed vertices; when the mesh outgrows its buffers, they are made again at the larger size.

`build/bench --dyntopo` (4 threads, this repo's cloud VM). A frame is 4 dabs plus the normal update, as in the main table. Fixed is the large-brush stroke on the usual layout, Plain the same with dynamic topology on but nothing to split, Refine with a detail that splits every edge under the brush once (each triangle into four), which is far more than a stroke on a coarse mesh asks for: from 5M up every dab stops at its 2,048 splits. Upload is the changed vertices plus the changed index pages:

| Vertices | Turn on ms | Fixed frame ms | Plain frame ms | Refine frame ms | Splits | Triangles after | Upload MB/frame | Undo ms | Redo ms | Turn off ms |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 500k | 34 | 0.23 | 0.24 | 13.1 | 117k | 1.16M | 0.91 | 11.6 | 9.9 | 83 |
| 1M | 71 | 0.34 | 0.35 | 16.8 | 157k | 2.27M | 1.46 | 35.0 | 33.0 | 191 |
| 5M | 319 | 0.99 | 1.04 | 22.4 | 157k | 10.31M | 2.07 | 46.4 | 43.4 | 714 |
| 7M | 994 | 1.58 | 1.51 | 28.8 | 157k | 14.33M | 2.43 | 60.1 | 57.7 | 1053 |
| 10M | 1312 | 1.83 | 2.70 | 31.7 | 157k | 20.32M | 2.78 | 74.6 | 72.1 | 1446 |

A split or collapse costs about 1.5 microseconds at 500k and 4 at 10M, normals included. Plain strokes cost about what they do on the fixed layout, up to half again at 10M (this VM's timings vary by about that much from run to run). The pages cost memory: right after turning it on each node fills about half its page, so the mesh takes about twice its fixed-layout memory, mostly in the per-vertex triangle lists; peak memory at 10M was 2.5 GB. Turning it on at 7M and 10M is mostly the VM's slow first touch of that memory. Turning it off packs the mesh and prepares it as any new mesh of that size.

## Files

The menu (the top bar's second button, after Home) opens and saves scenes (Open scene, Save scene, Save scene as, Save a copy, Earlier saves) and imports and exports meshes, each through the system's file dialog (SDL's); what the dialog picks runs at the start of the next frame. The outcome shows as a note at the bottom of the window for a few seconds, amber when something failed or needs attention, and goes to the log.

**The Export sheet** (Menu > Export..., Ctrl+E, or Export... in Search; `src/app/lens_export.odin`, after round 2's export2 screen) gathers every way out in one place: the formats down its side (Sculpt scene, OBJ, PLY, STL, glTF, USD, Screenshot, Turntable and 3D print, each with its icon and what it makes), the chosen one's options beside them, and at the foot the file name the dialog will suggest (the scene's name with the format's suffix, or Untitled), an estimate of its size from the selected object's triangles and vertices, Cancel and Export. The scene's options are Save as or Save a copy; a mesh format shows the selected object with its triangles and vertices; Screenshot has Without background; Turntable its frames and Without background; 3D print the sizes and unit of Scene > 3D print. Export runs the format's own command, so each writes what it always did, and a mesh format's save dialog offers only that format. The sheet opens on the format used last (`export_format` in the settings file) and Esc closes it. Classic keeps its menus; every format's command stays in Search.

**Scenes** (`.sculpt`: Open scene, Save scene, Save scene as): every object with all its subdivision levels as shown and their sculpt layers, a retopologized object's quads and UVs, the mask, polypaint and polygroups of its current level, dynamic topology if on, its name and place, whether its eye is off, it is a ghost or it is locked (from version 25; an older file opens with nothing locked), a Model mode cage's polygons, creases and corners in place of its levels (from version 26) its selected faces (from version 27) and its Smooth steps and Flat (from version 28), an applied cage's Catmull-Clark rule, polygons and creases, from which its levels are built again (from version 29), the sculpt a cage was taken from by Edit cage (from version 30), a cage's Thickness and Bevel creases (from version 31) and its live mirror (from version 32), its folder, the folders (names, folded or open, eye and ghost), Solo, and the camera. Each level is stored as positions, so it opens exactly as it was saved. Since the vertex numbering changes whenever a level is built, the levels above the base store their vertices in an order the topology alone decides: the vertices that continue the level below first, then those on its edges, by the edge's ends. Opening builds every level above the base again from it, as subdividing does, and moves its vertices where they were, so it takes about as long as subdividing to that size; the base itself keeps its node layout in the file, so it opens without being laid out again (see below). HD detail is not saved, only each object's number of HD steps; the HD section says so while an object has stored detail, and so does the note after saving. Nor is the undo history. Opening reads and uploads the whole new scene before the old one goes, so a damaged file leaves the scene as it was (both are in memory for a moment). Saving writes beside the file, makes sure every byte reached the disk (fsync), and then replaces the file with it (on Linux and Android the folder is synced too; on Windows the rename is write-through), so neither a failed save, a crash nor a power cut leaves a half-written file, and "Saved" shows only once that is done; exports, bakes and `.strokes` files are written the same way, screenshots are not. A save of a scene over about 1M vertices (32 MB) stops the app for a moment (about 0.6 s at 2M and 4.6 s at 10M on the VM), so it first shows "Saving head.sculpt..." at the bottom, saves once that is on the screen (a quarter of a second later, or at once when a scene change, another file action or closing the app comes first), and then replaces it with the outcome; a second Ctrl+S meanwhile is dropped.

**Save a copy** (Menu, or the command palette) writes the scene to another file and leaves it saving where it did: a backup, or a variation to come back to; the note says which file the scene still saves to. **Save next** (the command palette, or a bar button; `save_scene_next` in a script) saves the scene as the next numbered file beside its own and keeps saving there, so each stage of a sculpt stays as a file of its own: `dragon.sculpt` becomes `dragon_002.sculpt`, `dragon_002` becomes `dragon_003`, `head_9` becomes `head_010` (the number keeps its width, at least three digits); a name already taken is skipped, and after a thousand taken it says so and saves nothing. It needs a scene file to number from (Save scene as first), and an Android picked file, which has no folder to put the next one in, cannot. Earlier saves then lists the new file's. **Earlier saves**: saving over a scene file (Save scene, or Save scene as onto it) first keeps the file it replaces in a folder beside it, `head versions/head 2026-10-26 14.02.33.sculpt`, stamped with when that file was saved in the device's local time (a second save in the same second gets `-2`, `-3`, ...). The old file is hard-linked there, so it costs no time and no space until the new save replaces it, or copied where the file system has no links (FAT cards). The newest five are kept and older ones deleted (Settings > System > Earlier saves: Off, 3, 5 or 10; remembered as `versions_kept`); Off keeps no more and deletes none already there, and nothing else in the folder is ever touched. At 10M five versions are about 1.8 GB beside the file. If the earlier save cannot be kept (the folder cannot be made), the save still happens and its note says why in amber. Save a copy keeps no versions. **Earlier saves...** (Menu, or the command palette, once the scene's file has any) lists them under the menu button, newest first, as "Today 14:02", "Yesterday 18:40" or "12 Oct 09:15" (with the seconds when two share a minute) and their sizes; the list is read when it opens and after each save, never every frame. A tap opens that save as the scene, which still saves to its own file, so the next Save makes it the current one and keeps the one it replaces: going back loses nothing. With changes the file does not hold, the first tap turns the row red ("Discard changes and open?") and a second tap within three seconds opens it, as New scene asks. The format is little-endian binary with a version number: `scene_save` in `src/app/files.odin`, then per object `levels_write` in `src/sculpt/levels_file.odin`. Since version 22 a scene starts, right after its version, with a **preview block**, as a `.blend` keeps its thumbnail, so a list of scenes can show each by reading only the start of its file, never the whole scene (also through Android's `content://` files): its length, when it was written, how many objects and vertices it holds, and a picture of the viewport as it was last drawn (without the brush ring or gizmo), at most 320 pixels on its long side, as a PNG (`src/app/scene_preview.odin`). The picture is taken while the app rests (a second after the last press, at most every two seconds, only when the scene or the view changed), copied back from the GPU without waiting for it and shrunk by averaging every pixel it covers, about 4.6 ms in a release build on the VM at 1600 x 900; a save brings it up to date first, and an autosave writes the last one taken. A length that runs past the file, or past 4 MB, is a damaged file; a picture that does not decode only shows none. The exit stats add `previews` (pictures taken), `preview_size`, `preview_kb` and `preview_ms`.

**Import** (Import mesh, `.obj`, `.ply` or `.stl`): OBJ `v` and `f` lines (polygons become fans of triangles; negative indices and `v/vt/vn` corners work; everything else is skipped), read in parallel chunks of 4 MB. PLY in ASCII or binary of either byte order, with any vertex properties (x, y and z are kept) and polygon faces. STL in text or binary (a binary file is known by its length matching its triangle count, so a binary header starting with `solid` still reads), its facet normals ignored. Then a cleanup: vertices at exactly the same place are welded (exporters split vertices along UV seams), triangles left with a repeated corner and vertices no triangle uses are dropped, and a closed surface that faces inward is turned outward. The mesh is centred and sized like a sphere (radius 1) and placed beside the other objects; its place in the file is kept for export. A surface that is not closed sculpts, also with dynamic topology, which keeps its border, but subdivision, HD and remesh need a closed one, and the note says so.

**Export** (Export selected, `.obj`, `.ply`, `.stl`, `.glb` or `.usda` by the name, or by the dialog's filter when the name has none): the selected object's shown level (packed, when dynamic), as OBJ with nine significant digits, which read back as the same floats, as binary PLY, as binary STL for 3D printing (each triangle with its normal and no colour, in the file's units, so a slicer scales it), as binary glTF 2.0, or as USD text, with polypaint as vertex colours when it has any, STL aside (Import does not read colours back yet). It goes back where it was in the file it came from, so an imported mesh exported again lines up with the original; Move tool placement is not written.

**3D print** (Scene > 3D print, or the Export for printing... command): the selected object's size as it would print (the section is in the Scene palette, since the scale it sets is the scene's), its Width (X), Height (Y, up) and Depth (Z) in millimetres, centimetres or inches (the tabs; the unit is remembered), with its placement in the scene applied. Drag any of the three sideways and the other two follow. Every object prints at one scale, the scene's millimetres per unit (25 at first, so the starting sphere prints 50 mm across; saved with the scene, scene file version 23, which older versions of the app do not open), so the parts of a figure printed one by one still fit together: sizing one sets them all, as a modelling app's scene units do, while the sizes are typed in by the side, as a sculpting app's print hub has them. Export for printing... writes an STL file the way a slicer expects it: in millimetres whatever the tabs show, Z up (the scene's front faces the printer's front), its lowest point on the bed at Z 0 and centred over the origin, so it lands in the middle of the plate. The note after it gives the printed size and whether the surface is closed, with its volume in cm³ then (a guide to the material it takes), or how many open edges it has, which a slicer cannot fill reliably; meshes over 4M triangles skip that check. The size readout is the box round the shown level, close rather than exact on a turned object; the note's is exact. `--export-print=PATH` writes the selected object for printing on exit; the exit stats give `print_mm`, `print_unit` and `print_exports`.

**glTF** (`.glb`, one file) carries what Blender and other programs need to keep sculpting it: the normals, the polypaint as linear colours (16 bits a channel, so dark paint does not band), and the level's **sculpt layers as shape keys** (morph targets): each layer's displacement at strength 1, named as the layer, with the layer's strength as the key's value (0 for a hidden layer, whose strength is not kept). The base mesh written is the surface without the layers, so at those values the keys rebuild what the app shows (to rounding: 6e-8 apart at most on a 40k-vertex sphere with two layers, one at 1.5), and each key's slider then does what the layer's did, past 1 and below 0 as well. The object's **material** goes as glTF's metallic-roughness material: a physically based one as it is (base colour, metallic, roughness), a classic one with no metal and the roughness whose highlight is about as wide as its own (Clay comes out at 0.47), a MatCap by the colour painted into it (a loaded image as mid grey) at its highlight's roughness, each tinted by the material's colour as the viewport tints it, and named as the material. Groups with materials of their own go as one primitive a material, all sharing the vertices and shape keys, which Blender imports as one mesh with a material slot each. With polypaint the colours are the paint over the material's colour as far as it covers, as the viewport shows them (OBJ and PLY keep the paint over white), and the material's colour goes as white, since glTF multiplies the two; Blender wires the colour attribute into the base colour. With paint and group materials, a vertex on the edge between two of them is written once for each side, so each keeps its own colour under the paint. A layer is stored only where it moved the surface, so one over a small patch costs little. Checked with the Khronos glTF validator (no errors or warnings) and by importing into Blender 5.2 (vertices, triangles, shape keys and their values, the colour attribute; `scripts/export_check.py` runs both, see below); Blender turns Y up into Z up as it imports. Dynamic topology bakes the layers, so a dynamic object exports without them; polygroups and a retopologized object's quads and UVs are not in glTF exports (OBJ and USD keep the quads). A file over 4 GB, which the format cannot count (about 30 layers over the whole of a 10M mesh), is refused with a note. 10M vertices with a layer over all of them: 610 MB in about 7 s on the 4-core VM (PLY: 362 MB in about 4 s). An export over about 1M vertices first shows "Exporting head.glb..." at the bottom, as a big save does.

**Lights** go out with glTF and USD exports, as Blender, Marmoset and KeyShot carry theirs: every light in the Lights section that is on, by its name, placed and pointing as the view shows it now, in the exported object's own space (so it lights the mesh from where it did, whatever the object's placement), in its colour (its colour temperature's, when it has one). glTF writes them as `KHR_lights_punctual` lights, a node each beside the mesh's: a sun as a directional light of pi x 683 lux at Intensity 1 (Blender's importer makes it a sun of pi watts a square metre, which lights a white surface facing it white, as the app's sun does), a point light or a spot in candela that make it that bright at the distance it matches a sun of its Intensity, with its reach as the light's range and a spot's cone and soft edge as its outer and inner cone. USD writes the same lights as a `DistantLight` and `SphereLight`s treated as points (a spot with `ShapingAPI`'s cone and softness), their intensities chosen so Blender's USD importer gives each light the same type, colour, place, direction, cone and watts as its glTF importer does (checked with Blender 5.2: identical, sun 3.14, spot 290 W). A sun with an Angle writes it as the `DistantLight`'s `inputs:angle`, the angle across in degrees as UsdLux defines it (Blender's importer reads it as half that and shows twice the angle); a lamp's Radius stays out of USD, which measures a sphere light's intensity per area of it, and glTF's lights have no size. The ambient light and the environment do not go. `scripts/export_check.py --lights "SUN=3.1416,SPOT=290.17"` checks each light's type and energy as Blender imports it.

**USD** (`.usda`, text) carries the same as glTF, in the form Blender's, Houdini's and Maya's importers read: a `Mesh` under a `SkelRoot` named `Root`, the layers as `BlendShape`s (each the points it moves and how far, named as the layer; the prim names are the names with anything but letters, digits and `_` as `_`) bound to a skeleton with no joints whose animation gives the strengths, the paint as linear `displayColor`, the material as a `UsdPreviewSurface` bound to the mesh (the same values as glTF's; with paint its diffuse colour reads `displayColor` through a primvar reader, which Blender turns into its colour attribute), group materials as a `materialBind` partition of `GeomSubset`s, one a material in use with its own `UsdPreviewSurface` (with paint, `displayColor` is then given a face corner at a time), normals, `subdivisionScheme` none (the surface as it is, not a cage), the polygroups as a per-face `polygroup` primvar, and a retopologized base level as its quads with UVs (`st`, per corner), as OBJ keeps them (a quad takes the group its first corner shows). The strengths are written both as the default and as a time sample at frame 1: Blender's importer reads only time samples and keys them in an action, so the shape keys take their values at frame 1, and it keeps each key's range at 0 to 1, so a layer at 1.5 shows as 1 until the key's Range Max is raised (the action holds 1.5). Checked with USD's own 30 validators (no errors) and by importing into Blender 5.2: the evaluated mesh at frame 1, layers applied, is within 8e-8 of the OBJ export; Blender turns Y up into Z up. Text spells out every number, so it is the slow one: 10M vertices with a layer over all of them is 1.9 GB in about 24 s on the 4-core VM (glTF: 7 s); an export over about 180k vertices shows "Exporting..." first.

`scripts/export_check.py FILE.glb|FILE.usda` checks an export with a validator (Khronos's for glTF, USD's own for USD) and Blender itself (`--vertices`, `--triangles`, `--keys "Layer 1=1.5,Layer 2=0"`, `--colors`, `--material "Gold,1,0.3"`, the first material's name, metallic and roughness, `--uv` and `--textures 3`, a UV map and how many pictures the first material's textures show (one of them feeding the normal), `--face-materials "Gold=2,Steel=10"`, how many faces each material shades, and `--lights "SUN=3.1416,POINT=145"`, each light's type and energy by name, say what to expect; the material's base colour, or what feeds it, is reported); `--setup` installs them into `build/export-tools` (node's gltf-validator package, and the `bpy` and `usd-core` wheels; bpy wants Python 3.13). A tool that is missing is reported as skipped, never as passed. They are development tools only; nothing of them ships with the app.

An object made by Retopologize exports from its base level as quads with their UVs (OBJ only; PLY stays triangles). Normal, displacement, colour, ORM and ID maps are baked into a folder from the Object palette, with a textured `.glb` of the quads (see Retopology, UVs and baking). An object with texture maps (baked or loaded) exports to `.glb`, while it shows its base level (not in HD and without dynamic topology), as that same textured file, with its sculpt layers as shape keys on the split vertices and its polypaint as vertex colours unless it has a base colour map, which then stands for it (glTF would multiply the two); polygroup materials stay out, since the maps make one material. PNG and JPEG maps go in as they are and others as PNG, the two pictures glTF holds. OBJ, PLY and USD leave the maps out.

`build/bench --files` (4 threads, this repo's cloud VM, in memory, so without the disk). Parse is the reader alone, Clean the weld, centring and edge check after it, Sculpt-ready the setup any new mesh of that size gets (nodes, BVH, normals). Scene read includes that setup:

| Vertices | OBJ MB | OBJ write ms | OBJ parse ms | PLY MB | PLY write ms | PLY parse ms | Clean ms | Sculpt-ready ms | Scene MB | Scene write ms | Scene read ms |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 500k | 40 | 81 | 62 | 18 | 4 | 8 | 77 | 61 | 17 | 24 | 52 |
| 1M | 81 | 152 | 102 | 36 | 9 | 18 | 172 | 137 | 34 | 44 | 93 |
| 5M | 430 | 1177 | 605 | 181 | 39 | 143 | 1004 | 1029 | 172 | 221 | 610 |
| 7M | 605 | 1190 | 706 | 254 | 57 | 104 | 1221 | 1431 | 241 | 342 | 1625 |
| 10M | 866 | 1790 | 1310 | 362 | 74 | 156 | 1669 | 1747 | 344 | 495 | 2378 |

A scene opens faster than a new mesh of its size gets ready: the file keeps the base level's node layout (how many triangles and owned vertices each node has; 8 bytes a node, about 160 KB at 10M), so reading skips partitioning and renumbering the mesh, which was over half of it, and only works out the shared vertices and the tree above the nodes again. A layout that does not fit the mesh (a damaged file) is refused and the mesh is laid out from scratch instead, and files from before it get that too. Scene read at 10M went from 3.4-3.7 s to 1.1-2.4 s on the VM (it varies run to run with how fast the VM hands out fresh memory). In the app on the VM, opening a 10M scene went from about 6.5 to about 4 s in all, of which about 2.5 s is the llvmpipe software GPU copying the 360 MB of buffers, which a real GPU does in a small fraction of that.

Scenes with subdivision levels, laid out as in the `--levels` table (a base of at most 40k vertices). Only the base's triangles are stored, so the file is a third smaller than one level of that size; reading builds every level, as subdividing does:

| Top vertices | Levels | Base vertices | Scene MB | Scene write ms | Scene read ms |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 501k | 3 | 31k | 8 | 24 | 188 |
| 1.02M | 4 | 16k | 16 | 38 | 354 |
| 4.96M | 5 | 19k | 76 | 259 | 3151 |
| 6.92M | 5 | 27k | 106 | 306 | 4433 |
| 9.84M | 5 | 38k | 151 | 435 | 6300 |

Much of the time from 5M up is this VM's slow first touch of fresh memory (about 0.5 s per GB, sometimes several seconds), which a desktop does far faster: copying 120 MB into fresh memory took 0.7 s here. A real disk adds its read or write time; the Scene palette shows the last file's times (for an import, reading the disk and parsing apart).


## Home screen

Home (Menu > Home, the command palette, or `home on` and `home off` in a script) covers the window with a page to start from, as a tablet sculpting app's home and a desktop modeller's splash screen do (`src/app/kit_home.odin`, `src/app/home.odin`). On a window narrower than 760 points it is one column that scrolls: at its top the new scene starters, Sphere, Head, Bust, Figure, Hand and Creature, as tiles (New scene, at the Scene palette's Add size; with work to lose, the first tap turns its name to Discard? and a second goes ahead), and Open a file...; under them, two tabs of scenes as pictures: **Recent**, the last 24 scenes saved or opened, newest first, each with its name, when it was saved and how many vertices it holds; and **Autosaves**, the newest autosave of each earlier session (the last few that closed, and any that ended unexpectedly, marked "did not close"), named after the scene it was of. A tap opens a scene (an autosave is recovered, as the recovery card does); with changes the scene's file does not hold, the first tap marks the tile "Discard changes and open?" and a second within four seconds opens it. The scene open now is ringed in the accent and a tap on it only closes Home. A scene whose file is gone shows a crossed-out file, and a tap takes it off Recent. A long press (or a right-click) on a tile offers Remove from Recent (the file stays) or, for an autosave, Delete this autosave (a second tap deletes it). The close button, Escape or Android's Back go back to the scene. While Home shows, only its own key and Open scene's reach past it, and the recovery card waits under it.

**On a wider window** Home is round 2's home2 screen (`src/app/lens_home.odin`), which leads with going back to work. A large **Continue** card holds the newest Recent scene's picture, its name and "Saved 2 min ago · 3.7M vertices · 5 objects" from its file, and Open (a tap on the picture does the same; with work to lose the first tap turns it to Discard and open, as a tile does; Back to it for the scene open now). With nothing on Recent it offers the newest autosave (Recover), and with no autosave either, a sphere (Start). Beside it, **Start new** is six big tiles, each a drawing of what it starts: Sphere, Head, Cube, Voxel block (a box of voxel clay, at the voxel resolution whose surface has about the Add size's vertices), Plane (a square slab 1.6 across and 0.24 thick, closed so every tool works on it) and Empty scene (no object, for Add object or Import). Under them, More starters names Bust, Figure, Hand and Creature. Then Recent and Autosaves as the narrow page has them, with **Open...** and **Import OBJ, PLY, STL...** at their right; the whole page scrolls. Every start is also New scene in Search and in Menu > New, and `new_scene cube|plane|voxels|empty` in a start-up script.

Each picture is the preview block at the start of the scene file (see Files), read with the file's other numbers on a thread of its own, so a slow card or a `content://` file never holds a frame, decoded there, and put into a picture atlas (`render.Pictures`, 6 by 6 cells of 320 pixels, about 15 MB) at most two a frame from the frame's own command buffer; the atlas is made when Home first shows a picture and let go when it closes, and every opening reads the pictures afresh. A file saved before version 22 shows a plain tile. An interactive run given no scene file, script or recording starts on Home (Settings > Interface > Start at Home; off, it starts straight on a sphere). The exit stats add `home` (1 while it shows), `home_opened`, `home_read` (files read), `home_pictures` (pictures put in cells), `recent_scenes`, `home_autosaves` and `scene_name` (the scene's file name, `none` for none).

## Blender link

Send to Blender and Get from Blender (Settings > System, or Search; `send_to_blender` and `get_from_blender` in scripts) pass objects to and from Blender with no file dialogs, as ZBrush's GoZ does, through a folder: `link` beside the settings file (`<name>-link` for a run given `--settings-file`), whose path Settings > System shows, with Copy path to paste into the Blender add-on. It is files in that folder and nothing else: no network and no other program started. On tablets (Android and the iPad), where there is no Blender to link with, both are hidden.

**Send to Blender** writes each selected object's shown level to `<id>.ply` (binary, in the object's own space and not where an export would put it, so a mesh Blender leaves alone comes back exactly; paint goes as vertex colours, a voxel object as its surface, a dynamic one packed) and then `link.txt`, which names them: `sculpt_link 1`, `scene <token>`, `sent <number>`, and an `object <id> <level> <name> <file> <vertices> <triangles> <x> <y> <z> <rx> <ry> <rz> <scale>` line each (the name with `%`, spaces, control bytes and every byte past ASCII as `%XX`, `-` for none; the place as the object's, Y up, degrees). Then a `light <name> <kind> <x y z> <dx dy dz> <r g b> <intensity> <range> <inner> <outer> <size>` line for each of the scene's lights that is on, in the app's axes, as a glTF export gives them (`directional`, `point` or `spot`; where it is; the way it shines; linear colour; lux or candela; range, 0 for none; a spot's half angles in radians; its size, a sun's angle across in radians or a lamp's radius, 0 for none; the add-on reads the line without a size from before and leaves words after it alone), so Blender lights the objects as the app does; lights go to Blender only, and a reader that does not know the line skips it. The number rises with each send, so the other side can tell a new one; the files of objects an earlier send listed and this one does not are deleted. The token names the scene: made by its first send and kept in the scene file (version 17), so objects sent from one scene never replace another's that happen to share an id.

**Get from Blender** reads Blender's `blender.txt` (`blender_link 1` and the same lines, its meshes as `blender-<id>.ply`; id 0 for an object made in Blender) and takes each object in turn, all as one undo step:
- an object of this scene with the same triangles at the level it was sent from moves its vertices to Blender's (the level is shown first; an HD window is left, and its detail rides on the move as on a stroke; the mask and hidden parts hold vertices back, as for any deformation, and the note says how many; a recording sculpt layer takes the move);
- a voxel object or one under dynamic topology that comes back exactly as it went keeps everything and takes only Blender's placement;
- one with other triangles, a voxel object or a Model mode cage (which becomes a mesh) or one under dynamic topology (which stays on) is rebuilt from Blender's mesh, welded and turned outward when closed but never resized, as a remesh is: its levels, mask and strokes go, the shown level's layers come across, and Undo brings the old mesh back;
- one Blender made, one of another scene, one deleted here since, or one whose level is gone (or renumbered by Delete lower since the send) is added as a new object, named as Blender named it;
- Blender's placement becomes the object's.

The note names each ("From Blender: Head (moved 1,204 vertices), Hat (new topology), Cube (new object)") and warns when an object had changes made here after it was sent, which Blender's version replaced (Undo brings them back). Every file is read and checked against the manifest's counts before anything changes, so a send Blender is still writing, or a damaged file, changes nothing and is tried again by the next get. Each send is taken once: `taken.txt` in the folder notes the newest taken, so a later run does not take it again. While the History timeline plays or a stroke replays, whose undo steps a get would cut short, Get from Blender says so and does nothing. Switching back to the app's window gets a new send for this scene by itself, once no file dialog, save, stroke, History playback, replay, script or pack install is under way, and never in scripted runs (`--frames`, `--run`, `--script`). A big send or get first shows "Sending to Blender..." or "Getting from Blender...". Paint does not come back yet.

**The Blender add-on** is `tools/blender/sculpt_link` (Blender 4.2 or later). `python scripts/link_check.py --package` writes it as `build/sculpt_link.zip`; in Blender, Edit > Preferences > Get Extensions > Install from Disk installs that file. Its Link folder preference starts on the app's own folder for the system (`%APPDATA%\Implicit\Sculpt\link` on Windows, `~/.local/share/Implicit/Sculpt/link` on Linux, `~/Library/Application Support/Implicit/Sculpt/link` on macOS); paste Copy path there for any other. The 3D view's sidebar gets a Sculpt tab:
- **Get from Sculpt** brings in what the app sent: an object it made before from the same scene and id gets the new vertices in place when the counts match, so its UVs, modifiers and materials stay, or a new mesh; others become new objects. Positions stay in the object's own space and the turn from the app's Y up to Blender's Z up goes in the object's matrix, so a mesh left alone goes back exactly. Paint comes in as the colour attribute "Sculpt paint". The app's lights come too, while **Bring lights** is on (the default): each one that is on as a Blender light of its name in a "Sculpt lights" collection, updated in place by the next get and removed once a send no longer lists it, with the watts Blender's glTF importer gives the same light (a sun of Intensity 1 is pi watts a square metre), its size (a sun's Angle, a lamp's Radius), and a spot's cone and blend. **Auto get** does it by itself for each new send, looking once a second.
- **Send to Sculpt** writes the selected mesh objects: a mesh of triangles in its own order (one from the app keeps it, so the app moves its vertices), any other split into triangles over its own vertices; **Apply modifiers** sends the meshes as the modifiers leave them, which the app rebuilds its object from. A placement that is still the one the app sent goes back word for word; a moved, turned or uniformly scaled one goes in the app's axes, and a scale that differs between axes or mirrors (which the app's objects cannot have) goes into the positions, a mirrored mesh's triangles turned to keep facing out. A copy of an object from the app (Shift+D keeps its link properties) goes as a new object; the first of them keeps the link. Get from Sculpt replaces a mesh whose triangles were changed in Blender since, even at the same counts.

`scripts/link_check.py` runs the round trip with Blender itself, as `export_check.py` does exports (bpy from `export_check.py --setup`; SKIP without it): the app moves, turns and sends a sphere and saves; the add-on gets it, checks the counts, the placement and the lights (two suns at pi and 0.3 pi watts a square metre and the point light the script made), raises one vertex, moves the object, makes a cube of quads and sends both, then runs its two buttons as a person would with the add-on enabled; the app opens the scene and gets them back, and the saved result must have exactly that vertex moved, exactly where Blender put it, the sphere moved by Blender's move, and the cube as a new object. Like `export_check.py` it is outside the gate.

The exit stats add `link_sent` (objects), `link_moved`, `link_swapped`, `link_added` and `link_token` (1 when the scene has one).

## Autosave and recovery

An interactive run saves a recovery copy of the whole scene, as Save scene would, every minute while there is a change to save (Settings > System > Autosave: Off, 1, 2, 5 or 10 minutes; remembered as `autosave_minutes`). It never interrupts work: a copy starts only once nothing has been pressed, typed, touched or stroked for 1.5 s, and a worker thread of low priority reads the scene and writes the file while sculpting goes on. A press, key, stroke, scene change or file action while it still reads the scene makes it stop and try again at the next pause, so it never reads a half-changed mesh; the frame that stops it waits for it a few milliseconds at most (0.7 to 8 ms at 10M with and without levels on the 4-core VM, `autosave_wait_ms` in the exit stats). Once it has read the scene it writes the file while the app goes on. A copy is the same `.sculpt` format as Save scene and takes as long to read, without HD detail (as saving) or work inside an open HD window; the System tab says so while one is open.
