A release 2M stroke in the main window runs at about the same frame time with one view window open as with none, and a little slower with three (16.9, 16.9 and 19.3 ms p50 under Xvfb's software GPU, 1.8, 2.0 and 2.2 ms of CPU): each window lays out its head every frame and draws its picture once the stroke is over. Only the Linux desktop under Xvfb was tried; a real second monitor, a display of another density, and Windows are not yet.

## Command palette

Ctrl+K, `/` (on keyboards where it has its own key; Ctrl+K works on every layout) or the top bar's Search field opens the command palette under it: a search field over a list of every command the menus, the tray's buttons and the shortcuts run, one row per brush, tray section, view, mask command, paint, group command, remesh or decimate size and New scene shape, plus each Settings tab ("Settings: Touch") and each Help tab ("Help: Gestures"). Type part of a name, or a word another app uses for it (dynamesh, zremesher, subtool, freeze, polygroup, bpr, ...): rows whose name is the text come first, then names starting with it, then names with a word starting with each word typed, then those other words, then, from three letters, names holding the letters in order (`wfr` finds Wireframe). Among equals, a row run recently comes first, then one that can run now. Each row shows its icon, its shortcut, and a check when it is on; one that cannot run now (Duplicate object with nothing selected) is dimmed, does nothing, and its help line below starts "Not now". Up and Down pick a row (the list, eight rows or as many as the window's height leaves room for, scrolls to follow; with no keyboard, typing more narrows it), Enter or a tap runs it and closes the palette, and Escape, Android's Back, Ctrl+K again or a press outside closes it. With nothing typed the list starts with the starred rows, in the order they were starred, then the last eight rows run, which are remembered (`palette_recent`, by name) and cleared by Settings > Reset everything; a starred row shows a small star after its name. Under the help line three buttons act on the picked command, whether it can run now or not: Star (Starred when it is; up to 16 commands, remembered as `favourite` lines), Put in a pie (see Pies) and Assign key (see Controls). While the palette is open its field has the keys, so letters never run their shortcuts, and on a tablet the keyboard comes up with autocorrect and capitals off (for every text field, layer names too); hiding the keyboard (Back while it is up) closes the palette. Two commands that act where the pointer is (A's HD window and R's replay) are not listed.

## Help

Menu > Help, F1 or the command palette ("shortcuts", "gestures", "glossary") opens Help on the right, in the tray's place as Settings opens (opening one closes the other, and Escape or Back closes it). Three tabs, scrolling as the inspector does:
- Keys: every shortcut, grouped by what it acts on (file, edit, tools and the brush, the selected object, the scene, the view, the app) with every key bound to it (Redo: Ctrl+Shift+Z, Ctrl+Y), then the keys that act where the pointer is (Space, S, U, Escape) and the mouse, as the tool in use has them. It is built from the shortcut table itself, so it lists what works.
- Gestures: the touch and pen controls as the tool and Settings > Touch have them now; a gesture turned off is not listed, and the Undo row says "Hold two fingers still" once the two-finger tap runs something else. A button opens Settings on its Touch tab, and another shows the first-run tips again.
- Glossary: the app's words (Object, Brush, Mask, Hide, Group, Level, Layer, Remesh, Retopologize), what each means, and what ZBrush, Blender, Nomad and 3DCoat call it; the palette finds a command by any of those names.

## Settings

Menu > Settings opens the app's preferences; every change takes effect at once. It is one sheet (`src/app/lens_settings.odin`, after round 2's settings2 screen) under the top bar, 780 points wide: down its side **Search settings** and the sections Interface, Camera, Touch, Pen, Dial, Performance, Frame rate, Bars, Files and Shortcuts, with Reset all settings at the foot (a second tap within three seconds confirms it); beside them the section's settings, ending with Reset (that section's). While Search settings holds text, only the sections with a matching setting show, and only those settings (a setting matches by its heading and the words of its rows, so "palm" finds Touch and "autosave" Files). Touch lists every gesture as a row with what it does on the right: one finger on the model (Sculpt or Turn the view), one finger on empty space (turns the view), two fingers (Pan, zoom and roll, Pan and zoom, Pan only, or Zoom and roll), three fingers dragging (Size and focal shift, or Nothing), then each tap, hold and the pen's side button from the gesture map; under them Zoom and roll about the fingers, Lift one of two fingers to turn and Double tap, then PALM AND PEN: Ignore touches while the pen is near and Finger can sculpt when no pen is used. Dial holds the modifier wheel's settings and Keep the dial in clean view; Pen, where the pen and mouse go on empty space, the hover names, and PENCIL AND BUTTONS (double-tap, squeeze, the second button and the eraser end; see Touch and pen); Frame rate, the frame rate and Rest when idle; Bars, the bars, the action bar and the pies; Files, autosave, earlier saves, undo history and the Blender link; Shortcuts, the Keys tab. Exit stats: `settings_section= settings_found=`. A window under 840 points wide gets a narrower panel instead, on the right in the tray's place, so the model stays in view while a change is tried; it shows the same settings, worded the same (`src/app/settings_blocks.odin` holds each heading's group once for both), in five tabs:
- Interface: the look (Lens, the default: a near-black frame with a sea-green accent; Studio, the default before Lens: neutral greys with an orange accent; or Graphite, the earlier cool dark grey with a blue accent; a settings file from before Lens that holds Studio opens in Lens once), the interface size (100% to 150% of what the screen asks for, never smaller, so every target stays at least 48 points), Screen layout (Right-handed, the default, or Left-handed, which swaps the sides: the off-hand island, the view chips, the Stats panel and the modifier wheel go to the right; the navigation head, the view rail, the panel stack, Settings, Help and the other windows to the left; popovers open the other way; a window you moved goes to the other side as far in as it was; the top bar and the shelf stay as they are; `left_handed` in the settings file and scripts), Reduce motion, Lay the brush ring on the surface (on by default; see Controls), Show next steps over the shelf (the action bar, on by default; see Interface), Modifier wheel (Off, Left, the default, or Right, for the other hand, named as the sides are on the screen, its size, what its sectors show and its turn; see Touch and pen), Show every option (the tray's sections without their More options folds, off by default; see Interface), Work in any view (on by default; see View grid; `panes_work`), Start at Home (on by default; see Home screen), Edit pies (see Pies) and Reset panel positions.
- Camera: the orbit style, Turntable (the default, as most 3D apps orbit, ZBrush's Rot Y: sideways turns about the model's up axis and the horizon stays level; it goes over the top too unless "Stop at the top and bottom" is on; "Twist two fingers to roll the turntable", off by default as Nomad's and Blender's turntables keep the horizon level, lets two fingers tilt it on screen: see Touch and pen), Trackball (ZBrush's Rot XYZ and its default, `free` in the settings file and scripts: a drag turns the model about the screen's own axes, all the way round both ways and over the top, and the view may end up tilted; two fingers roll it), X axis or Z axis (sideways turns about the world's X or Z axis instead of its up axis, and up and down turns freely, for working on a model lying along that axis; which way round is decided as each drag begins, so the model follows the pointer), the orbit, pan and zoom speeds (a quarter to four times), inverting the orbit sideways or up and down and the mouse wheel, what the view turns about (Turn about: Last stroke, the default, Pointer, Selected objects, Whole scene or View centre; with Pointer, Off the model, turn about the air; Show the pivot dot; see Controls), Wheel zooms toward the pointer (off by default), the safe zone (Edges always turn the view, on by default, its width from 24 to 200 points, 64 by default, and whether its edge is outlined; see Touch and pen) and a button to Help's list of the mouse controls.
- Touch: what one finger does on the model (Sculpts, the default, or Turns the view, leaving sculpting to the pen), Touch strength (10 to 100%, 100 by default: a finger's strokes work at that much of the brush's strength, the pen's and the mouse's always at full, so a finger can soften what the pen builds up), Hold delay (0.1 to 1.5 s in steps of 0.05, 0.25 by default: how long a finger or the pen holds a control still for its long press, such as a button's menu; below about 0.2 s a slow tap may become a long press; see Touch and pen), what the pen and the mouse do on empty space (Turns the view, the default, or Waits for the model: a stroke that starts off the model begins once it reaches it), Names while the pen hovers (Always, the default, With a keyboard, shown only while one is connected, or Never: the pen held over a control without touching shows its name and shortcut as the resting mouse does; a long press always does), Predict the cursor (on by default, `predict_cursor`: while a stroke draws, the brush ring runs ahead of the pointer to where it will be when the frame is shown, by the iPad's predicted Pencil touches where UIKit gives them and elsewhere by one frame of the pointer's motion, at most 48 px; only the ring moves ahead, never a dab, and not while the stabiliser makes the brush trail), what the Pencil's double-tap and squeeze, the pen's second button and its eraser end do (see Touch and pen), ignoring the hand while the pen is near, and each gesture on or off: pinch to zoom, zoom and roll about the fingers, two-finger pan, twist to roll, three fingers for size and focal shift, and the gesture map (see Touch and pen), and when drawn shapes apply (Mouse at once, the default; Always at once; Always after Apply; see Hide, clip and slice).
- Keys: what the mouse's buttons do to the view, as ZBrush (the default: right drag turns, middle drag pans), Blender (middle drag turns, Shift + middle pans, Ctrl + middle zooms) or Maya (Alt + left turns, Alt + middle pans, Alt + right zooms) has it, with what each drag does under the choice; the wheel, left drags, the edges and the brush pie's right click are the same in all three, and a change takes effect from the next press. Under it, every command with its keys, in Help > Keys' groups, with a search field that finds a command by its name or the words Search finds it by, or by a key: with a `+` the start of a key ("ctrl+z" finds Undo, "ctrl+shift" every key with both), without one a key's last part ("q" finds Pie 1 and Pie 2, "s" is not every Shift key). Keys you changed show in the accent colour; a command without one says No key. A tap on a row opens Assign key beside it (see Controls), which follows the row as the list scrolls and closes once it scrolls out of view. Escape in the search field lets the field go and leaves Settings open. The mouse choice is remembered (`mouse_scheme`).
- System: Show statistics, the Stats panel's Simple or Advanced view, Frame rate and Rest when idle (see Frame rate), Anti-aliasing (Off, 2x or 4x; see Anti-aliasing), and Autosave: how often to save a recovery copy (Off, every minute, the default, or every 2, 5 or 10 minutes), when the last one was written and how long it took, and Open the last autosave (see Autosave and recovery), Earlier saves: how many of the files a save replaces to keep (Off, 3, 5, the default, or 10; see Files), Undo steps kept (No limit, the default, or 20, 50, 100, 200 or 500; see History timeline), and on a desktop the Blender link: its folder, with Copy path, and Send to Blender and Get from Blender (see Blender link).

Each tab has a button that puts its own settings back as a fresh install has them (the Keys tab's gives every command its default keys and the mouse back to ZBrush's), and Reset everything (a second tap within three seconds confirms it) puts all five back. They are remembered with the rest (see Remembered settings): saved when the panel closes, when the app goes to the background (Android) and on exit. Brush and tool settings stay with the tool, in the tray; the drawing switches for comparing speed stay in the Stats panel's Advanced view.

## Statistics

F11, Menu > More > Stats or Settings > System shows the Stats panel (Settings > System > Statistics also opens Input diagnostics in developer builds: see Input diagnostics) at the top left, beside the off-hand island. Simple, the default, says how smoothly the app runs in plain words, in four groups: Performance (frame rate; frame time and interface update time, each typical and in slower moments; the GPU's 3D time when measured), While sculpting (brush work per frame and input to the GPU, once a stroke has run), Mesh (objects, vertices, triangles, triangles drawn last frame) and Memory (the undo history's size; the memory of the whole app is not measured, so it is not shown), then Last operation (what the last remesh, smooth, file action and so on did, and how long it took). Typical is the median (p50) of the last 240 frames: half took less and half more. Slower moments is the 99th percentile (p99): 99 frames in 100 took that long or less; it is not the slowest frame. Interface update is only the time to lay out and draw the panels, one part of a frame. On Match display (or Limit), the frame time includes waiting for the screen (or the limit), so it sits near the refresh time even when the app has time to spare. "What do these numbers mean?" at the bottom says the same. Advanced shows every measurement by its exact name (`frame_ms`, `cpu_ms`, `ui_ms`, `cursor_ms` for laying the brush ring on the surface, ...) with its p50 and p99, the frame-time graph, the drawing switches and what the brush costs; a measurement with no samples yet says so instead of showing 0.

## Screenshots

Menu > Save screenshot (F12) writes the viewport, as the last frame drew it and without the brush outline or gizmo, to a PNG file at the viewport's size. The encoder is this repo's own (`src/png_write`; Odin's `core:image/png` only reads): each row filtered with Sub or Up, deflated with greedy matches and the fixed codes, so a 1214 x 862 viewport is about 200 KB and takes about 25 ms in a release build. `--dump-viewport=PATH` does the same on exit, or writes a binary PPM when the path ends in `.ppm`, which `scripts/ppm.py` reads.

**Transparent screenshots** (Scene > Background > Screenshots without background, or the command of that name; remembered with the settings) save the objects alone on a see-through ground, for a picture laid over others, as a renderer's transparent film or another sculpting app's export with alpha does: no background, floor grid or environment picture. The frame after Save screenshot draws the viewport once more, into a target of its own, on a clear transparent ground with the same camera and objects; its alpha is how much of each pixel the objects cover, soft at their edges with multisampling. A pixel the objects cover wholly keeps the viewport's colour (live occlusion, the studio render and Render mode included); an edge pixel takes the second drawing's colour, which the background never mixed into, so the cut-out shows no fringe over any ground. See-through objects (Transparency, ghosts) leave no alpha of their own: they show only over other objects. A `--frames` run with `--dump-viewport` writes its last frame this way while the setting is on (script: `screenshot_transparent on`). The exit stats give `screenshot_transparent`.

**Turntable** (Scene > Turntable, or the Record turntable... command): Record turntable... asks for a folder, then the camera goes once round the scene about the world's up axis, as a model on a turntable before a still camera, and every frame is saved at full detail: as `turntable_00000.png`, `turntable_00001.png` and so on at the viewport's size, which any video tool joins into a film (`ffmpeg -framerate 30 -i turntable_%05d.png turntable.mp4`), and as `turntable.png`, one animated PNG (APNG) that browsers and image viewers play as it is, looping at 30 frames a second, halved until it is at most 960 pixels wide so that it stays small enough to send. The turn is seamless: the last frame stops one step short of the first. Frames (24 to 720, 120 at first, remembered) sets how many it takes and so how long the turn lasts (4 s at 120). With Screenshots without background on, the frames and the animation have no background, as a transparent screenshot. Each frame is drawn and read back before the next, so recording takes over the window until it ends, about as long as saving that many screenshots; the button becomes Stop recording with how far it is, and a stroke stops it too, leaving the frames written and no animation. The camera goes back where it was afterwards. The studio render and Render mode start over at every turn, so their frames show their first samples; live occlusion and the plain viewport show as they do still. Not with a time-lapse at the same time. `--turntable=DIR` records at the last of the `--frames` (or once `--bench-stroke` ends) and the run lasts until it is done; `--turntable-frames=N` sets Frames. The exit stats give `turntable_frames` and `turntable_written` (frames saved). The animated PNG is this repo's own writer (`png_write.apng_begin`, `apng_frame`, `apng_end`), written to the file a frame at a time.

## Pen and brush pie

The Brush palette's Pen section holds what a drawing tablet changes, on three pages, as ZBrush and Nomad keep size and strength apart. The Strength page's "Pressure changes strength" and the Size page's "Pressure changes size" choose what pen pressure scales; a mouse counts as full pressure. Each page has its own pressure curve, mapping how hard the pen presses (along) to how much that counts as (up) for the strength or the size: a cubic Bézier from corner to corner whose two handles drag anywhere in the square, so it never falls back. Linear, Soft (light pressure goes far), Firm (it takes a press) and S are presets. A dot on the curve follows the pen; the pen's pressure is smoothed first (Pressure smoothing, in the Stroke section), for both. The Response page sets what pressure does as a whole in one tap: Default (strength follows the pen, the size stays), Clay (Blender's Clay: the size follows the cube of the pressure and never drops under a fifth of the radius, the strength takes a press), Steady (ZBrush's lazy pressure feel: over nine tenths of the strength from half pressure on, so a stroke keeps an even height) and Light (Nomad's light-pressure default: a light touch goes far for size and strength). Its Smallest size slider is how far pressure may shrink the brush (5% to 100% of the radius). Use global, on the same page, is on for every brush but Clay, which starts on its own Clay response: with it on the brush uses the global pressure; switched off, the brush in use keeps its own switches, curves and smallest size, starting from the global ones, and switching it back on leaves them kept for the next time. The settings file keeps the size's curve (`pressure_size_curve`; a file from before takes the strength's), the smallest size (`pressure_size_floor`) and each brush's own (`pressure_own`), and `.strokes` files (version 22) the pressure each stroke worked with. The exit stats print `pressure_response=` (the response the brush in use is on, -1 for none) and `pressure_own=`. The section's Lean page holds what the pen's lean does. Its Tilt slider (0 by default) lets a leaning pen turn the brush: the direction Draw, Clay, Flatten, Pinch and Crease push in leans toward the pen's top, by the pen's lean times the slider, at most 81 degrees, as Blender's tilt does; mirrored dabs lean the mirrored way. Lean grows the size and Lean softens (both off by default, `tilt_size` and `tilt_soften`) let a pen laid toward the screen shade as a pencil's side does, as Procreate's tilt does: the brush grows up to twice its size and loses up to all its strength as the pen leans further from upright, in full from 60 degrees (`lean_of`). Where the platform reports how far the pen hovers above the screen (an iPad's Pencil), the brush ring fades as the pen rises, so it says how close the pen is. With tilt on, a line from the brush circle shows the lean. The readout under it shows the last pressure, what it counts as, and the lean. Tilt costs one small rotation a dab: a 2M stroke took the same brush time with it and without (1.27 to 1.33 ms p50 either way, run to run). SDL reports pen pressure and tilt on Windows (pointer messages) and on Linux (X11 and Wayland); neither is tested on a real tablet yet.

**Every pen sample.** Windows, Android and iOS gather the pen's samples between two frames into one update (a pointer message with a history, a MotionEvent with historical samples, a touch with coalesced touches at the Pencil's 240 Hz), and SDL 3.4 hands on only the newest of each, so on a tablet a fast stroke arrived as one point a frame: its path laid as straight chords and its pressure and lean changing in steps. The app reads the rest itself, as Blender does on Windows: a Windows message hook reads the pointer's history (GetPointerPenInfoHistory), the Android activity the MotionEvent's historical samples, and on the iPad a gesture recogniser on SDL's view the Pencil's coalesced touches; each sample goes in ahead of SDL's newest, at its offset from it and with its own pressure and lean (`src/app/pen_samples.odin`). Every pen sample also takes the pressure and lean of its own update, where SDL's order (place first, then pressure) gave each the update before's. X11 and Wayland already deliver every sample. The Stats panel's Advanced view and the exit stats give `samples_frames` (frames of a stroke), `samples_frame_mean` and `samples_frame_max` (pointer samples in those frames) and `samples_coalesced` (samples the bridges added), so a run on a tablet shows the count; `--ui-events` takes `FRAME:pcoal:X,Y` or `FRAME:pcoal:X,Y:P` to send one as a bridge does. Not yet tried on a real tablet.

**Stabiliser.** Stroke > More (and the shelf's quick settings, which show Lazy mouse and Smoothing) holds three steadiers that work together, as Krita's and Clip Studio's do. Lazy mouse trails the brush behind the pointer on a string that many pixels long. Smoothing (1 to 16 samples, off at 1) makes the brush follow the mean of the pointer's last that many samples before the string, so the jitter of a hand or a tablet's sensor averages out while the stroke still goes where the pen went; while either is on the rope from the pointer to the brush is drawn. Pressure smoothing (1 to 16 samples) does the same to the pen's pressure on its own, so a swell has no steps. Catch up on lift (on by default) carries the stroke on, at its spacing, from where the brush trails to where the pen lifted, so a stabilised line ends under the pen and not short of it; Grab, Snake hook, Cloth, Rectangle and Line strokes end where they are. Presets keep Smoothing and Pressure smoothing; settings.txt keeps all three as `smoothing`, `pressure_smoothing` and `catch_up`; `.strokes` files from version 14 keep them too, so a smoothed stroke replays as drawn.

The brush pie puts every brush in a ring around the pointer, in key order clockwise from the top: each slice shows its number near the middle and the brush's name further out, a long name stepping down to the small size where its neighbours sit beside it, so no two names meet. Hold Space over the viewport, point toward a brush and let go to pick it, so a flick picks without aiming. A tap of Space, or a right click without dragging (a pen's side button sends one), leaves it open: click a brush, or click the middle, press Escape or tap again to close it. While it is open, nothing reaches the tools or the camera, and no press reaches a panel under it. The slice of the brush in use has a band along its outer edge.

In Lens the brush pie holds eight brushes, placed by direction so a flick learns them: Clay up, then clockwise Draw, Inflate, Crease, Grab (down), Flatten, Pinch (left) and Smooth. Space, a right click, the pen's side button or the dial's Pie sector opens it (the dial's in the viewport's middle). Each slice shows the brush's icon and name; the one pointed at stands out, tinted and outlined, with a dotted line from the middle toward the pointer, and the middle names it with "lift to pick" or "tap to pick". Holding Space, let go to pick; opened by a tap, tap a slice (a lifted finger points at nothing, so nothing is lit until you touch). Resting on a slice for half a second with Space, a press or the pen held picks its brush and opens its quick settings beside the slice. The pie editor's first row is the brush pie's eight slots: a tap on one opens Search to choose what goes there (any command a bar takes), a tap with a command to put puts it, a long press or a right click empties it, and Reset pies puts the eight back. The brush pie is kept in settings.txt as the shelf's pages are (`bar brush_pie ...` lines), not in a workspace's layout.

## Pies

Four pies of your own, eight commands each, open the same way around the pointer, as Blender's Q Quick Favourites and ZBrush for iPad's quick menu do. Q opens Pie 1 and Shift+Q Pie 2 over the viewport (in the window's middle when the pointer is elsewhere, kept whole on screen); Pies 3 and 4 have no key until given one (Assign key). All four are in the command palette ("Pie 3"), which is how a tablet without a keyboard reaches them. Tap or click a slice to run its command; tap the middle, press Escape, right-click or press the pie's key again to close it, and another pie's key switches to that pie. Each slice shows the command's icon and name and a band when it is on (Wireframe, Orthographic); it is dimmed, and does nothing, when the command cannot run now, and an empty slot reads Empty; a tap on either keeps the pie open. While a pie is open no press reaches a panel under it, and the tap that opened it from the palette picks nothing from it. A fresh install has Pie 1 for the view and the object (Focus selected, Frame all, Wireframe, Flat shading, Orthographic, Render, Remesh, Search) and Pie 2 for the mask and hiding (Clear, Invert, Blur, Sharpen, Grow and Shrink the mask, Show all, Invert hidden); Pies 3 and 4 start empty.

The command palette's Put in a pie opens the pie editor for the picked command: four rows of eight slots, where a tap puts the command in a slot (replacing what was there), a long press or a right click empties a slot, and Reset pies puts the defaults back. Settings > Interface > Edit pies opens it to empty slots and reset only. A pie that differs from its defaults is remembered whole (`pie <pie 0-3> <slot 0-7> <command|empty>` lines, saved at once). Presets cannot go in a pie yet, and the user pies have no hold-and-flick: that is Space's brush pie.

## Brush falloff

The Brush palette's Falloff section shows how strongly the brush in use works from its centre (left) to its rim (right). By default it is the built-in smooth falloff, (1 − d²)² for the distance d in radii. Dragging either handle draws your own: a cubic curve read from the rim in, as the pressure curve is drawn, so it is always 0 at the rim and full at the centre. Presets: Smooth (the built-in), Linear (a cone), Sharp (a point in the middle, for creases and pores), Firm (most of the radius at full strength) and Flat (full strength to the rim, a hard-edged stamp). Each brush keeps its own falloff, Grab and Mask included; an alpha replaces it. Under the curve, Focal shift (-100 to 100, 0 by default, each brush its own, also on the brush bar and three fingers sideways) reshapes whichever falloff the brush has without changing its ends: the weight at d radii is the falloff's at d^p with p = 2^(−shift/50), so 100 pulls the brush to a point and −100 spreads it into a flat top with a hard edge. While it is not 0 the brush ring shows an inner ring where the brush works at half strength. A shifted falloff is a table too, so it costs what a drawn one does. The sculptor reads a drawn falloff from a 257-entry table over the squared distance, so it costs no square root: a 2M stroke's brush time was 7 to 12% higher with one than with the built-in (0.24 vs 0.25 to 0.27 ms p50, release, test VM). Script: `falloff sharp` (or another preset's name) gives every brush that preset, keeping their focal shifts, and `focal 40` gives every brush that focal shift.

Under Focal shift, Hardness (0 to 95%, 0 by default, each brush its own, as Blender's) keeps that share of the radius at full strength and squeezes the falloff into the ring outside it, so 50% is a flat-topped brush with a soft edge half a radius wide; it shapes the table as the focal shift does, so it costs what a drawn falloff does, and the inner ring shows its half-strength point too. Falloff shape Sphere or Projected (Blender's) picks how the distance is measured: Sphere round the dab's centre in 3D, Projected across the view, out to a radius either side of the centre in depth, so the brush covers the ring on screen however the surface curves away under it, and a ridge or a lump standing out of the surface is caught as wide as the flat around it. Clay and Flatten start Projected, the rest Sphere; Grab, Elastic, Cloth and Snake hook, which drag what they pick up, and a brush with an alpha (laid on the surface already) stay Sphere. A preset keeps them as the named values `hardness=` and `shape=sphere|projected` (a preset without `shape=` takes its brush's start), and a `.strokes` recording (version 26) as each stroke's brush; one from before replays Sphere, as it was drawn. Script: `hardness 50` gives every brush that hardness.

## Brush samples

The Brush palette's Samples section, after Falloff, is ZBrush's Samples sub-palette: how a brush reads the surface it works on, so a stroke behaves the same on rough ground and over itself.
- Stabilize orientation (0 to 95%, 0 by default): the plane Draw, Clay, Flatten, Pinch and Crease (and any brush with an alpha) work from (its direction and height) is averaged along the stroke instead of read afresh each dab, each dab keeping that share of the stroke's plane so far, so the brush keeps a steady plane over a bumpy surface. Lock plane, when on, wins (it keeps the first dab's plane for the whole stroke).
- Sample radius (25% to 100%, 100% by default): how much of the radius a dab reads that plane over. Smaller follows a narrow ridge's top instead of leaning to its sides.
- Accumulate (each brush its own; on for all but Draw): off, a stroke going back over itself does not build up past what one pass lays, as ZBrush's and Nomad's Draw: each vertex keeps the height the stroke has given it and a dab only raises it to the dab's own height there, a Dots stroke's dabs each made as high as its whole row piles up along the middle, so one pass stands as high as with Accumulate on. It works for Draw and Inflate, which push along the normal (the plane brushes stop at their plane anyway, and Layer never builds up); for the others the switch is greyed, as the two sliders are for brushes that do not read the plane.

Settings keep them as `stabilize`, `sample_radius` and `accumulate <brush> 0|1`; a preset keeps them as the named values `stabilize=`, `sample_radius=` and `accumulate=0`, and a `.strokes` recording (version 25) as each stroke's brush.

## Brush alphas

An alpha, as in ZBrush (Blender calls it a brush texture), is a square grey image that shapes each dab in place of the round falloff: white moves fully, black not at all. The Brush palette's Alpha section shows the round brush and every alpha as thumbnails; tap one to use it. Six are built in: Square (flat stamps and edges), Noise (bumps of every size, for skin, stone and bark), Rings, Stripes (ridges along the image's up, so along a stroke they rake parallel grooves), Dots (rivets, scales) and Star. "Load image..." adds a PNG, JPEG, BMP, TGA or QOI file: its brightness times its opacity, padded to a square and averaged down to at most 512 pixels a side, so a grey height map and a white shape on a transparent ground both work. Every brush but Grab uses the alpha; Shift's smoothing stays round. Load image also keeps a copy of the file in the library, a folder `library/mine/alphas` beside the settings file (`<name>-library` for `--settings-file=<name>.txt`, and none for a run that keeps no settings), so the image is back after the built-ins at the next start, and so is every preset that uses it, and the brush's own alpha. The same image loaded again is found there by its bytes and not kept twice; another image of a name already there is kept as `rock-2` and on, and the file's name is made safe for every system (letters, digits, spaces, `-`, `_`, brackets). Presets, the settings file and scripts name a kept alpha `mine/<name>` (`alpha mine/rock`) and a built-in by its name; an image a script loads by its path is not kept. With a kept alpha selected, Remove from library (tap it twice) deletes its file, and the brush and every preset that used it go back to round, with a note counting those presets. A file in the library that is not an image the app reads is skipped at start, with a note naming it, and left on disk. The exit stats add `library_alphas` (kept alphas), `library_skipped` and `alpha` (the brush's alpha by reference, or `-` for round).

### Content packs

A content pack is a `.sculptpack` file: a ZIP archive, so any zip tool makes one, holding `pack.txt`, brush alphas under `alphas/` (PNG, JPEG, BMP, TGA or QOI images, right in that folder), brush presets in `brushes.txt`, base meshes under `meshes/` (OBJ or PLY files, right in that folder, at most 64), and MatCaps under `matcaps/` (images of a lit sphere, right in that folder, at most 32, each at most 1024 x 1024) with materials in `materials.txt`. `pack.txt` has one `key value` line each for `name` (needed), `author`, `version`, `description` and `license`; lines starting with `#` and other keys are skipped, and values are cut to 200 characters. Menu > Library (or Search) opens the Library panel in the tray's place: Install pack and Make a pack at its top, then Your own (the alphas Load image kept) and every pack installed, with its author, version, licence, how many alphas, presets, meshes and materials it holds, and its description, and a bin to remove it (tap it twice). Install pack picks the file with the system's file dialog (on Android every file is offered, since the picker cannot filter by `.sculptpack`, and the archive's own bytes decide) and installs it on a thread of its own, so a big pack never holds up a frame. Before anything is written the archive is checked: at most 4096 entries and 512 MB unpacked, sizes read from its directory and every entry inflated no further than it says (a zip bomb is refused), no encryption or ZIP64, names that cannot climb out of the folder or mean something else on another system (no `..`, absolute paths, drives, backslashes or Windows device names, and no two that differ only in case), every entry's checksum, a `pack.txt` with a name, every alpha an image the app reads, every MatCap one too and no larger than 1024 x 1024, and every mesh one that reads and cleans as Import would (the note names a bad mesh's line); one failure refuses the pack, with a note saying why. `brushes.txt` holds `preset` lines exactly as `settings.txt` writes them (see Brush presets), at most 256, whose alpha is `-`, a built-in's name or the name of one of the pack's own alphas without its extension; a line that is damaged, repeats a name, takes a brush's own name or names an alpha the pack lacks is skipped, and the note counts those lines. A second mesh of one name (Boulder.obj and Boulder.ply) and anything in a folder below `meshes/` are left out, and so is anything else in the archive (scripts, shaders), with a note counting it: a pack is data only and never brings code. The pack is unpacked into the library's `.incoming` folder and renamed into place as `library/<id>` (its name in lower case, letters and digits with dashes, numbered when another pack has it), so an install cut short leaves nothing half there. Its alphas join the Alpha section under the pack's name and are named `<id>/<name>` in presets, the settings and scripts (`alpha rock-set/pebble`). Its presets join the brush browser, the dock's slot menu and the Preset section's list under the pack's name, and are named `<id>/<name>` in scripts (`preset rock-set/stone clay`). They are read-only: picking one copies its values onto the brush as any preset does, and the Preset section offers Revert and Save as new preset (a preset of your own named after it, "Stone clay 1") but not Save changes, Rename or Delete. The brush and the dock slots on a pack's preset are remembered (`brush_pack_preset <id> <name>` and, after the slot's own `dock` line, `dock_pack <slot> <id> <name>`, which older versions skip). Installing a pack of the same name and author as one installed asks first, in the Library panel: Replace moves the old folder aside, renames the new one in, then deletes the old, and the alphas still there keep their names, so presets that use them keep working; presets on an alpha the new version dropped go round, with a note counting them; the brush and dock slots on one of its presets stay on the preset of that name in the new version, or go back to the plain brush when it has none. Removing a pack deletes its folder, sends the brush and presets that used its alphas back to round, the brush on one of its presets back to the plain brush and those slots back to their brush. Packs only add: they cannot change the built-in alphas. Its meshes are listed with their vertex and triangle counts in the pack's `meshes.txt`, so a start lists them without reading them again. Menu > New scene lists each pack's meshes under its name after the six shapes (a tap starts a scene of that mesh alone, cleaned, centred and sized as Import does, with the same second tap to discard work); Scene > Add object lists them after the shapes ("Boulder (Rock Set)", with no size to pick) and the Objects card's Add menu under the pack's name, each adding the mesh beside the others as one undo step, named after its file. Replacing a pack keeps Add object's pick on the new copy of a mesh of the same name; removing it puts the pick back on the shape, and objects already made from its meshes stay. `materials.txt` has one `material <name> <r> <g> <b> <metallic> <roughness> <matcap>` line a material, at most 64: the name escaped as preset names are (`%20` for a space), the colour in linear light from 0 to 1 (as the Custom material keeps it), metallic from 0 to 1, roughness from 0.04 to 1, and the MatCap `-` for the lights, a built-in's word (`red-wax`, `clay`, `grey`, `skin`, `pearl` or `toon`) or the name of one of the pack's own without its extension, which wins over a built-in's word. A line that is damaged, has a value out of range, repeats a name, takes a preset material's name or Custom, or names a MatCap the pack lacks is skipped and counted in the note. A MatCap no line names becomes a material of its own, white and named after its file, so a folder of MatCap pictures is a pack as it stands. The pack's materials come after Custom in the Material section's list ("Moss (Rock Set)"), and are named `<id>/<name>` in scripts (`set material rock-set/moss`) and the settings, whose `material` line keeps the one new objects take (older versions skip it and start on Clay), as `custom_material` keeps Custom's MatCap when it is a pack's. A MatCap's texture is made the first time something shows it and let go once something else is made and nothing shows it, so a library of many uses GPU memory only for those in use; one whose file no longer reads leaves a note and the lights. A scene file (version 16) keeps a pack's material or MatCap by name with its values, an object's and a polygroup's alike, and opened where that pack is not installed the object has a Custom material of the same colours with the lights, with a note counting those objects. Replacing a pack moves objects, the material new objects take and Custom's MatCap to the new version's of the same name; removing it, or a replace without it, leaves those objects a Custom material of the same colours (with the lights where the MatCap went), Custom with the lights and new objects on Clay, with a note counting the objects. Make a pack writes your own alphas (the ones Load image kept) and your own presets as a `.sculptpack`, named after the file you save it as ("Bolt clay.sculptpack" is the pack "Bolt clay"), with stored entries any zip tool opens; a preset on one of your alphas names it by its file, one on a built-in by its name, and one on another pack's alpha goes round in it, since that alpha is not yours to pass on, with a note counting those presets. With no alpha or preset of your own it is disabled. The script verb `install_pack PATH` installs a pack in place (replacing one of the same name and author), `make_pack PATH` makes one, and the `library` command opens the panel. The exit stats add `packs`, `pack_alphas`, `pack_presets`, `pack_meshes`, `pack_materials`, `pack_matcaps`, `pack_matcap_textures` (made this run), `packs_installed` and `packs_refused`, and `selected_material` names the selected object's material.

The image lies on the plane across the area under the brush, its middle on the dab's centre and its sides one radius away, so a stamp sits flat on a curved surface. A vertex takes the image's value where it falls, blended between pixels, and fades as it lies further above or below that plane. Which way the image's top faces is the mode: Follow stroke turns it with the stroke, Fixed keeps it to the screen's up, Random turns every dab a new way, for texture without a visible repeat (the turns come from the stroke's seed, as Spray's do, so a replayed stroke turns each dab the same way), and Pen roll turns it as the pen turns about its own length, as Procreate's barrel roll turns a brush's shape (an Apple Pencil Pro, or a pen whose platform reports its roll; facing up without one); Angle turns it further. The brush outline becomes the image's square, turned as the next dab will be, with a tick at its top, so with Pen roll the hovering pen shows where the image faces before it touches down. Symmetry mirrors the image too, so a stamp on the left reads backwards on the right as a mirror would show it.

A dab looks for vertices as far out as the image is above 0, measured when it loads: up to the square's corners, 1.41 radii, but no further than the round brush for images that fade out inside their circle, like the built-in ones but Square. With the Noise alpha a dab costs about a fifth more than Draw and a frame under a tenth more (`build/bench --brushes`, the large brush; Draw measured 0.037 and 0.312 ms a dab and 0.27 and 1.91 ms a frame at 1M and 10M in the same run, beside the alpha row in the table above), the extra being three dot products and one image lookup a vertex.

## Viewport materials, flat shading and wireframe

The Scene palette's Look section (also "look", "matcap", "shading" or "style" in the command palette, and `viewport_look sculpt_studio` or `viewport_look classic` in a script) puts on a whole look at once, the way Nomad's and Blender's viewport shading presets do, while each part stays its own control. **Sculpt studio** is soft grey clay (the Studio grey material) under a key light from the upper left, the fill all but off and the rim light behind on, deeper Cavity (0.7), styled shading (below), Occlusion while working, and the studio render at full Occlusion, Shadow 0.6 and Shadow softness 8.6°, so soft shadows fall from the jaw onto the neck once the view is still, on a flat dark grey backdrop. It matches renders of grey studio sculpts: on a 3.8M-triangle head the lit face is about 200 of 255, the neck in the jaw's shadow 40 to 70, the backdrop 42. **Classic** is the look the app always had: Clay, the starting lights, the gradient, plain shading, both occlusions off. A preset sets the material new objects take and the selected objects' (as a pick in Material does), the scene's lights and the starting ones, and the rest; it is not an undo step. Its tab shows lit while the style and the studio render are as it set them. Beside the tabs: Key light (the first light's Intensity), Ambient, Shadow and Shadow softness; under More options Occlusion, Styled shading with Rim and Underside, and the Background's brightness. A fresh install, and settings from before there were presets, start in Sculpt studio once; after that the settings keep what was chosen. Runs with `--no-settings`, `--frames` or a settings file of their own keep Classic, so scripts, tests and benchmarks look as before.

The Scene palette's Material section picks how the selected objects are shaded. The classic presets are Clay (the code's default and Classic's), Red wax, White, Grey, Skin and Studio grey (Sculpt studio's, a soft grey with a faint broad sheen): each sets the selected object's colour, a muted one for the others, and the light's ambient, highlight and wrap (wax and skin let the light reach past the shadow line, as scattering does). Below them are physically based materials: Gold, Copper, Chrome and Steel (metals, coloured by measured reflectance), Plastic, Ceramic, Rubber and PBR clay. These use metallic and roughness with GGX highlights from the lights (see Lights) plus the reflection of a procedural studio that roughness blurs: a cool sky over a dark floor, a warm softbox above and beside the key light, a cool strip light behind on the right that catches edges, and a bounce card behind the camera so faces turned to the viewer reflect something. Where the normal varies within a pixel, as on a dense sculpt seen from afar, highlights widen instead of sparkling (specular anti-aliasing, as Filament does it). It is all computed in the fragment shader, so they take no memory and cost the same at any vertex count. The colour, Metallic and Roughness sliders under it edit whichever material is in use into Custom, leaving the presets as they are. Every colour in the panels (the material's, Polypaint's and the Paint brush's) is a swatch beside its hex code over Red, Green and Blue sliders from 0 to 255, all in the values a screen shows (sRGB, as image editors and Blender's hex field give them), so a code copied from elsewhere gives the same colour here. A tap on the hex field empties it to type a code over its hint, the code it had: `#RRGGBB` or `#RGB`, with or without the `#`, in either case; the colour follows as soon as the code is whole, and Enter or a tap elsewhere ends it. The material's colour is kept as linear light, which is what the shader lights, so its sliders and swatch go through the sRGB curve (moving one channel converts only that channel); paint is kept as shown. Settings and scene files hold the same numbers as before. Every material, classic or physically based, goes through the same display path: the lit colour is scaled by **Exposure** (in stops, -3 to 3, under More options; 0 by default), tone mapped with Khronos PBR Neutral (colours below about 0.8 pass through nearly unchanged, brighter ones roll off toward white instead of clipping) and encoded with the exact sRGB curve, which keeps the darkest values dark where a plain 2.2 power used to grey crevices out; polypaint is decoded with the same curve. **Cavity** (0.5 by default) darkens creases and **Ridge** (0.3, under More options) lightens crests, as ZBrush's materials and Blender's cavity shading do, so fine sculpted detail reads at any light angle: the shader measures how far the normal turns across each pixel, so it needs no extra pass, vertex data or memory, works with partial redraw and the idle cache, and picks out grooves and pores while leaving broad forms alone (a crest's lightening fades toward the silhouette). Because it is measured per pixel, a form drawn small (zoomed out) shows more of it than the same form close up. Flat shading turns both off. Script: `set cavity 0.6`, `set ridge 0`, `set exposure -0.5`; all three are remembered.

**Each object has its own material**, kept in the scene file (version 14; version 16 keeps a content pack's material by name, see Content packs). The Material section shows the selected object's, and a pick or a moved slider goes to every selected object and becomes the material new objects take (Add object, import, New scene); the others keep theirs. An object made from another takes its material: a duplicate or paste, both parts of a slice, split pieces, an extracted shell, a retopologized copy, an Insert of an object; Merge visible takes the first merged object's. A material change is no undo step (as in ZBrush, where materials are outside Undo History), but the scene counts as changed, and undoing or redoing something else (a remesh, a slice) leaves materials as they are. A scene saved before version 14 opens with every object in the material new objects take, which is how it showed. **Dim the others** (under More options, on by default, remembered) shows the objects that are not selected in their material's muted colour, as the app always has, so the one being sculpted stands out; off, each shows its own colour. A Custom MatCap material uses the one loaded image, which the scene does not store: loading another changes every object using it. Script: `set material steel` gives the selection steel too (the settings file's `material` line only sets what new objects take), `set dim_others off`.

**Each polygroup can have a material of its own**, as ZBrush's polygroup materials and Blender's material slots give one: gold trim on a steel plate, in one object. When the selected object has polygroups (Object palette, Polygroups), the Material section shows Object and Group tabs. Group's **Pick a group** (also in the command palette) waits for a press on the selected object and takes the group under it; group 0, the faces in no group, is always the object's own. A pick from the list (the lit presets and Custom) or a moved colour, and under More options Metallic and Roughness, give that group its own material; **Use the object's material** gives it back. An object has at most 15 groups with their own, and a MatCap cannot shade a group, since a draw has one matcap picture, the object's. The renderer looks each triangle's group up in a small table sent with the frame's uniforms (16 slots: slot 0 the object's own), so it adds no vertex data, costs the same at any vertex count, and partial redraws and the idle cache stay exact; HD windows, the boolean preview and the thumbnails show the object's material. Like the object's material it is outside Undo and kept in the scene file (version 15; an older file opens with none), but a group command that renumbers the groups (Visible, Masked, Creases, Clear) takes the materials with them in the same undo step: each goes to the group that now holds most of its faces, and is dropped when that is group 0. Rebuilds that keep the groups keep them; dynamic topology, which has no groups, drops them. Split gives each piece its groups' materials (a piece of one group with its own takes it as the object's), and Merge keeps every part's look, a merged object's groups taking its material. Script: `set material gold` then `group_material 3` gives group 3 gold, `group_material 3 off` takes it away.

**MatCaps** come last in the list, as ZBrush's MatCap materials and Blender's MatCap shading: Red wax, Clay, Grey, Skin, Pearl and Toon MatCap. A matcap is a picture of a lit sphere that stands in for the lights: the shader looks the picture up by where the surface's normal points as the camera sees it (with Blender's correction, so the sides of a wide-angle view do not smear), so the light, colour, sheen and rim painted into it cover the whole mesh with one texture fetch a pixel. The light therefore turns with the camera, never with the object. The six built-in ones are painted in code at start-up (`src/app/matcaps.odin`: a key light from the upper left, sky and ground light, a highlight, a broad sheen, a rim, light scattered along the shadow's edge for wax and skin, and three hard-edged tones for Toon), 256 x 256 each with a full mip chain, 1.4 MB of GPU memory for all six, and the app ships no image files. The picture is multiplied by the material colour (white for the presets), so polypaint and the mask show as they do on the other materials, and Cavity, Ridge and Exposure work on them too; Metallic and Roughness are hidden, since the picture holds the sheen. Under More options, **Load MatCap...** takes any PNG, JPEG, BMP, TGA or QOI picture of a lit sphere (the sphere filling the square, as matcap libraries share them; grey, 16-bit and alpha pictures work, alpha is ignored) and makes it the Custom material in white; moving the colour then tints it. The Custom material remembers its matcap, and the image loaded last is loaded again on the next start (a `matcap_image` line; a missing file leaves a note and the lights). Script: `set material "clay matcap"`, `matcap path/to/sphere.png`.

Styled shading, for the look of a studio's grey sculpt renders, changes two things on the lit materials (classic and physically based; MatCaps carry their own light): the ambient light falls off toward surfaces facing down, to Underside's share facing straight down (0.15 at first), so a chin shades the neck and a brow the eyes; and the classic rim is narrower and gathers on the upper silhouettes, at Rim's strength (0.35 at first). Down is the light rig's, so it turns with the lights. Off, the shading is exactly as before. Script: `set styled on`, `set rim 0.5`, `set underside 0.2`; all three remembered with the settings.

View > Flat shading, or Shift+G, lights each triangle with its own normal, so the facets show instead of the smoothed normals; the shader takes the triangle's normal from screen-space derivatives of the position, so it needs no extra vertex data and works with every material. While the view moves, far parts drawn from simplified copies show those copies' larger facets. View > Wireframe, or Shift+F as ZBrush's PolyFrame, draws the selected object's triangle edges over it: the same visible chunks again with line fill, pulled toward the eye by a depth bias so they sit on the surface, so it roughly doubles the selected object's draw cost while it is on. A long press on Wireframe picks what it shows, as ZBrush's PolyFrame does: Lines (the edges, on at first), Fill (the selected object's polygroups in their colours, as Polygroups shows them on every object) or both. Script: `set material steel`, `flat_shading on`, `wireframe on`, `set polyframe_fill 1`.

The Camera menu's Field of view (15 to 100 degrees, 40 at first; the view rail's Camera button) sets the lens: wider strengthens the perspective, narrower flattens it toward a long lens's. The eye moves along its line as it changes, so the model keeps its size on screen; Reset camera keeps it, and an orthographic view does not change with it. Script: `set fov 0.7` (radians); remembered with the settings.

Floor grid (Shift+P, a bar button, as ZBrush's Floor) draws grid lines under everything shown, and on the walls behind it as you look when its section (a long press, or Scene > Floor grid) turns them on: Floor, Back (square to Z) and Side (square to X), each on the scene's box, the walls on whichever side faces away from the eye, so the grid never stands in front of the model. A brighter line comes every fifth cell, and with Axes in colour the lines through the origin show as the axes, X red, Y green and Z blue. Fit cells to the scene (on at first) makes a cell a round 1, 2 or 5 times a power of ten near an eighth of the scene's size; off, Cell size sets it. Each plane is one full-screen triangle whose pixels find where their ray meets the plane (`src/render/shaders/floor.frag.slang`): the lines stay one pixel wide and smooth at any distance or slant, the minor ones fade where their cells grow too small to tell apart, all of them fade out a little way round the scene, and each point writes its own depth, so a mesh in front hides the grid and the grid crosses a mesh behind it. It is drawn after the meshes in every pass and never into the idle cache, so partial redraws stay exact. The scene's box is taken again only when the work, an object's place or what shows changes, never during a stroke or a drag, so the floor holds still while you sculpt. Script: `floor_grid on`, `set floor_z 1`, `set floor_fit 0`, `set floor_cell 0.5`; remembered with the settings.

Background (Scene > Background) sets what the viewport shows behind everything: one colour, or with Gradient (on at first) a top colour that shades into a bottom one down the viewport, a soft grey from #454547 to #17171A. With the gradient, Top and Bottom pick which edge's colour the picker below edits, as a swatch, a hex code and Red, Green and Blue sliders. The gradient is dithered under half a step of 8 bits, so it shows no bands, and the same pixel always gets the same value, so partial redraws stay exact. Screenshots (unless Screenshots without background is on, below the colours), the time-lapse and the studio render show it as the viewport does; thumbnails and the navigation head stay transparent. Script: `set background_gradient off`, `set background_top 0.17 0.17 0.17`, `set background_bottom 0 0 0.1`; remembered with the settings. The exit stats give `background_gradient`, `background_top` and `background_bottom` (as hex codes).

Transparency (a bar button, or Transparency in Search) draws every shown object but the selected one see-through, at 30%, so the selected one shows through whatever stands before it, as ZBrush's Transp does. The see-through objects blend over everything drawn before them and leave the depth alone, so the idle cache is not used while it is on, and with anti-aliasing a stroke's frames redraw the whole view rather than a rectangle. The studio render's occlusion and shadows see only the solid ones. Script: `transparency on`; remembered with the settings.

## Lights

The scene has its own lights, up to eight, as in Blender, Marmoset and KeyShot, edited in the Scene palette's Lights section as ZBrush's Light palette is. Each light is a **Sun** (from a direction, as from far away), a **Point** (from a place, every way, brighter close up) or a **Spot** (from a place, into a cone), switched on or off, with its own Intensity and colour, and an Ambient light lifts every side evenly. Each one **moves with** the camera, as ZBrush's and Blender's studio lights do, so a form reads the same way however it is turned, or stays in the **scene**, so the lit side stays put while the camera goes round, which is how a form is judged on a turntable. A new scene starts with four suns moving with the camera: Light 1 (the key, upper left) and Light 2 (a weak fill, lower right) on, Light 3 (a rim from behind on the upper right) and Light 4 (a bounce from below) waiting off, so the default look is the one the app always had, pixel for pixel.
- **The light sphere** at the top is a ball lit by the lights as the camera sees them, with a numbered marker for each light that is on: filled when the light is in front of the model, a ring when it is behind. The picked light is circled. A point light or a spot shows where it stands as seen from the orbit's centre.
  - A press on a marker (within a finger's reach) picks that light. A drag on the sphere moves the picked light to the finger, the pen or the mouse, keeping it in front or behind, and switches it on; a point light or a spot moves round the orbit's centre at the distance it was, a spot still aimed at the centre.
  - A tap on the picked light sends it behind the model, or brings it back in front, at the same place on the sphere: from behind, a light catches the model's edges. A tap on the empty sphere puts the picked light there.
- **Markers in the viewport**, while the section shows, stand for every light, as Blender, KeyShot and Marmoset draw their lamps: a dark plate with the light's kind's icon ringed in its colour, faded when it is off, the picked one ringed in the accent. A point light or a spot stands where it is, a spot with a line to where it is aimed; a sun, which has only a direction, stands on a large circle round the orbit's centre (0.42 of the view's shorter side) where the light sphere would show it, on a lighter plate when it is behind the model. A marker whose place is off screen, behind the camera or under the bars or the palette is held smaller at the edge of the room left, on the line from the middle toward it. A press on a marker (within a finger's reach, for any tool; it never sculpts) picks that light, and a drag moves it: a sun round the circle, keeping the side it was on, as on the light sphere; a point light or a spot across the view at the depth it had, a spot still aimed at the point it was. The picked point light or spot also has the Move tool's handles about its marker, as Blender's lamps take its axis moves and Marmoset's lights their gizmo: an arm along each of the scene's axes (red x, green y, blue z) moves it along that axis alone, and a square between two arms across their plane, the third axis held exactly; the marker in the middle still moves it across the view. A mouse or pen hovering lights the handle it would take; a press on one is the lights' for any tool, as a marker's is. A marker held at the edge has none, since its light is not where it shows. Each drag is one undo step ("Move light").
- **Place highlight** (a switch under Intensity), as Marmoset's light placement and KeyShot's highlight tool: while it is on, a press on the model moves the picked light so its highlight, seen from the camera, lands under the finger, the pen or the mouse, following it while held. The light comes from the view direction mirrored about the surface there: a sun turns; a point light or a spot moves round that spot at the distance it was from it (a sun's place, the camera's distance), a spot aimed at it. It switches the light on, and each press is one undo step ("Place highlight"). A press off the model still turns the view.
- **The list** under it shows each light, as the Scene palette lists objects: its kind's icon (a sun, a bulb, a ceiling lamp), its name (Light 1 to Light 8, numbered as on the sphere), what it is and moves with, and an eye that switches it on or off. A tap picks the light the controls below edit. **Add** Sun, Point or Spot puts a new light after the others and picks it (up to eight): a sun moving with the camera, shining from the upper right in front; a point light or a spot standing in the scene up and to the right of the orbit's centre, as far away as the camera, a spot aimed at the centre, as a new lamp is placed in Blender or KeyShot. Then **Intensity** (0 to 2) and, under More options, its **name** (typed, up to 23 bytes; the typing is one undo step, "Rename light", and an empty name shows the light's number), its colour (a swatch, hex code and Red, Green and Blue, as every colour is), **Colour temperature** (a switch: on, the light shines in the colour of a black body at **Temperature**, 1700 K to 12000 K, 6500 K when switched on, as a lamp is set in Blender, KeyShot or Marmoset; picking a colour switches it off), its kind (**Sun**, **Point**, **Spot**; a point light or a spot adds **Distance**, how far it stands from the orbit's centre, from a twentieth of the camera's distance to twenty times it, moving it along the line from the centre while it points the same way), its size (a sun's **Angle**, 0° to 60° across, the real sun's being about half a degree; a point light's or a spot's **Radius**, in scene units, the slider reaching half the camera's distance; each kept while the light is the other kind, as Blender keeps a sun's angle and a lamp's radius; a bigger light gives broader, softer highlights, a smooth or glossy surface most, by Karis's representative point with the highlight's energy kept, and 0, the default, shades exactly as a light without a size; a spot adds **Cone**, 1° to 160°, and **Soft edge**, the share of the cone from its edge in that fades), what it **Moves with** (Camera or Scene; switching keeps it exactly where it is on screen) and **Delete** (the lights after it move up). Adding and deleting are undo steps of their own ("Add light", "Delete light").
- Under More options, after the picked light's controls, **Ambient** (0 to 2, 1 by default) scales the material's ambient light and, on the physically based materials, the studio's diffuse light; their reflections stay.
- Under More options, **All fixed to the scene** moves every light, and the environment, to the scene, or back to the camera, keeping each light exactly where it is on screen; the environment keeps where it was for a camera turned about the up axis (Turn all takes the camera's own turn in or out).
- **Turn all** (-180° to 180°) and **Height all** (-90° to 90°), under More options, swing every light moving with the camera and the studio the physically based materials reflect round together, about the view's up axis or up and down; lights in the scene stay where they are.
- **Use as starting lights** makes the lights the ones every new scene, and every scene saved before scenes kept lights, starts with (remembered with the settings); **Reset lights** puts the scene's lights back as a fresh install has them.

A point light or a spot is as bright as a sun of its Intensity at the distance it was first placed (the orbit's distance then), and falls off with the square of the distance from there, as a real lamp does, so moving it closer brightens the model. Its reach (`light1_reach`, in scene units, 0 for none) fades it smoothly to nothing there, so a far light never pops off. A spot shines along the way it points, inside its cone, its edge faded over the soft edge with a smoothstep. Every change in the section, from a press to its release, is one undo step ("Lights" on the History timeline), and the lights are saved in the scene file (version 19, with each light's colour temperature and name from version 20; an older file opens with the starting lights, a version 19 one's lights with no temperature and no names, and a damaged lights section is refused like any damaged scene). Autosave notices a change to them.

Every light that is on casts the Fast render's shadows (see Render), each as wide as its own size; with every light off it shows the occlusion alone. MatCaps carry their own light and ignore the lights. A light lights both the classic and the physically based materials, at three times its Intensity on the latter. The shader loops over eight lights sent per draw and hashed into both frame keys, so partial redraw and the idle cache stay exact; lights that are off cost one comparison. Script: `set light1_on on`, `set light1_kind point` (`sun`, `point`, `spot`), `set light1_space scene` (`camera`, `scene`; the numbers are then read in the scene's axes), `set light1_x -0.5`, `set light1_y 0.6`, `set light1_z 0.6` (toward the light, in front of the model for z above 0 when it moves with the camera, made unit length where it is used), `set light1_px 1`, `set light1_py 0.5`, `set light1_pz 2` (where a point light or spot is: from the orbit's centre in the lights' frame when it moves with the camera, in the scene otherwise; left at 0 0 0, it is placed along its direction as far away as the camera), `set light1_strength 1.3`, `set light1_ref 2` (the distance at which it is as bright as a sun), `set light1_reach 10`, `set light1_cone 35`, `set light1_blend 0.3`, `set light1_color 1 0.8 0.6` (linear light, 0 to 1), `set light1_kelvin 3200` (0 for the colour as picked; below 1700 it is 1700), `set light1_angle 5` (a sun's size, degrees across, 0 to 60), `set light1_radius 0.2` (a point light's or spot's, 0 to 1000 scene units), the same for `light2` to `light8`, `set light_count 6` (0 to 8), `set light_ambient 0.5`, `set light_turn 90`, `set light_height 20`; `set lights_fixed on` moves every light to the scene with its numbers read in the scene's axes turned by Turn all, as it did before each light had its own space; the older `set light_key` and `set light_fill` set Light 1's and Light 2's Intensity. Scripts set the scene's lights; the settings file keeps the starting lights under the same names, a starting light's name as `light1_name`, with `environment_fixed`, and a settings file from before reads its lights as the starting lights (one from before the four lights keeps its key and fill strengths, one with the old `lights_fixed` its lights in the scene where they were). The exit stats add `light_pick`, `lights_on`, `light1` (its view-space direction), `light1_on`, `light_flips`, `light_count`, `light1_kind`, `light1_space`, `light_moves` (the drags of a marker or its handles and Place highlight presses made), `light1_name` (spaces as underscores), `light1_kelvin` and `light1_at` (where a point light or spot stands in the scene; 0,0,0 for a sun).

**Image environments.** The Scene palette's Environment section (after Lights) swaps the procedural studio for a panorama, as Blender's LookDev HDRIs do: Built-in (the studio above, the default) or Image, **Load environment...** (a Radiance `.hdr` file, or a PNG, JPEG, BMP, TGA or QOI picture read as sRGB; equirectangular, twice as wide as tall within a pixel, from 64 x 32 to 8192 a side, pictures at most 4096 wide on tablets) and **Strength** (-4 to +4 stops). Under More options, **Background** shows the panorama behind the scene in place of the flat or gradient background, and **Blur** (0 to 1, 0.3 by default) how soft it shows. The physically based materials (and textured objects) then reflect the panorama, sharper or blurrier with their roughness, and take their diffuse light from it; the Lights section's lights still add theirs (switch them off there for the panorama alone). The classic materials and MatCaps are not lit by it. The panorama turns with the Lights section's Turn all (Height all does not tilt it, as a world rotation only spins) and, with All fixed to the scene, stays with the scene so the camera orbits inside it; otherwise it stays put on screen as the studio does. A file is decoded on a thread, halved by 2 x 2 averages until it is at most 2048 wide (1024 on tablets); a Radiance file is read and halved row by row, so an 8k one never sits in memory whole, while a picture is decoded whole once its header shows it is within the limit (on Android the file picker offers every file, since it has no type for `.hdr`, and the bytes decide); Radiance files with flat or run-length rows are read, the old 1 1 1 run form and XYZE files are refused, and every value is clamped to a half float's largest (65504), so a blazing sun cannot become infinite. On the GPU it becomes an RGBA16F texture with a full mip chain and a copy half its size prefiltered once into six levels (roughness 0, 0.2 ... 1: 64 GGX samples a texel, each read from the mip that matches its solid angle, so few samples show no noise), about 27 MB for a 2048 panorama and 7 MB on a tablet (the section's status line says how much), and its diffuse light as nine spherical harmonics worked out on the CPU. A reflection is one texture fetch a pixel and the diffuse light nine multiply-adds, so the cost does not grow with the mesh; partial redraw and the idle cache stay exact (both frame keys hash which panorama and how it shows, and the background pixel depends only on its own ray). In a constant white panorama a white rough material with the lights off shows white (1 + the Fresnel term, about 1.016, before the tone map). With Built-in every image is the same as before, bit for bit. A file that is not a panorama, damaged or refused by the GPU leaves a note and changes nothing, and while a file decodes the one before keeps showing; one picked while another decodes waits its turn, the newest winning. Remembered like a loaded MatCap: `environment_source built_in|image`, `environment_fixed`, `environment_strength`, `environment_background`, `environment_blur` and an `environment_image` line naming the file, read again (and waited for) at the next start; a file gone since leaves a note and Built-in. It is a view setting, not kept in the scene file. Script: `environment path/to/sky.hdr` (waits for it and picks Image), `set environment_source built_in`, `set environment_background on`, `set environment_blur 0.5`, `set environment_strength -1`. Exit stats: `environment`, `environment_size`, `environment_background`, `environment_mb`.

## Frame rate

Settings > System > Frame rate says how often the app draws. Match display, the default, waits for the screen's refresh (vsync), so the app runs at whatever the screen runs at, 60, 120 or 144 Hz, and never draws a frame the screen would not show. Limit draws at most 30, 60, 90, 120, 144, 165 or 240 frames a second (`--frame-limit=N` for any other), shown as soon as they are ready (mailbox where the screen offers it, so without tearing), to save the battery or to keep an uneven rate steady. Unlimited draws as fast as it can, for measuring speed (`--no-vsync`). The wait for the limit comes before the frame reads its input, as the wait for the screen does, so a limited frame still shows the newest pointer position. Below the choice the panel says what the screen refreshes at and how frames reach it, so a screen set to 60 Hz in the system's display settings shows as such.

Android runs an app's screen at 60 Hz unless the app asks for more, even on a 120 Hz tablet, so on Android Match display and Unlimited ask for the highest rate the screen offers at its resolution (`ANativeWindow_setFrameRate`, from `android/app/jni/android_main.c`), and Limit asks for the limit, which lets a screen that can run slower save power; the system makes the final choice (a battery saver may still hold it at 60). The panel shows the highest rate when the screen runs below it. A settings file from before the choice keeps VSync off as Unlimited.

Rest when idle (on by default, below the frame rate) saves the battery between strokes. Once a second has passed in which no frame changed anything (no input, nothing held down, the same panels, nothing redrawn in the viewport, no stroke, replay, script, studio samples, thumbnails, Home's pictures or a thumbnail view waiting to be drawn), the app waits for the next event before each frame instead of drawing the same frame again, waking at least every 100 ms so timers and work finished on other threads still show. Any touch, key or pen event ends the rest at once, and the frame it wakes reads that input, so the first stroke after a rest is not late. Whether a frame changed anything is judged from what was drawn (a hash of the interface's triangles, and the viewport's partial redraw), so a fading panel or a moving progress bar keeps it awake without a list of what might animate. Scripted runs never rest unless `--idle-sleep` asks; the exit stats print `idle_rests` and `idle_rest_ms`. On the 4-core VM, 300 frames of an untouched scene with `--idle-sleep` took 18.7 s of rest after the first second, and used about a thirtieth of the CPU time per second of the frames before it.

## Anti-aliasing

Settings > System > Anti-aliasing (Off, 2x or 4x; 4x by default, 2x on tablets) smooths the stepped edges of outlines, creases seen side on and wireframe lines with multisampling (MSAA): each pixel keeps 2 or 4 coverage and depth samples, the surface is shaded once a pixel, and the samples are averaged into the image the interface shows. Shading inside a surface is not multisampled; the specular anti-aliasing of the physically based materials and the matcaps' mip chains keep that from sparkling. It is an exact technique, so the same view gives the same image every time and partial redraw stays exact; there is no temporal or post-process blur over fine sculpted detail. The samples are kept from frame to frame (partial redraw loads them to redraw a rectangle, and the idle cache is multisampled too and copied sample for sample), so 4x costs about four times the viewport's colour and depth memory, about 130 MB more at 1920 x 1080 with the cache, and the bandwidth to load, store and average them each frame: on the order of a few tenths of a millisecond of GPU time at 1080p on a desktop GPU (not measured yet; `gpubench` reports it). With multisampling a partial redraw draws the meshes one pixel past the rectangle it clears, as some rasterizers (llvmpipe) test the scissor at each sample and would otherwise leave a seam; the depth test keeps what is already there. Thumbnails and the navigation head stay single-sampled. The studio render, which reads the camera's depth, draws a single-sampled copy of it once per still view. Where the GPU cannot use the count asked for it uses the other of 2x and 4x (Vulkan promises 4x), and where the viewport cannot be made at that count (out of memory) it turns anti-aliasing off, logs it and carries on. Script: `set msaa off` (or `x2`, `x4`); remembered with the settings.

## Graphics

Settings > System > Graphics (Performance in Lens) chooses the GPU backend that draws: Automatic (the default), Vulkan or SDL_GPU, applied when Sculpt next starts, since windows are made for the backend. Automatic and Vulkan draw with the app's own Vulkan backend, and with SDL_GPU on a device whose Vulkan lacks what that backend needs (Vulkan 1.3 with dynamic rendering, synchronization2 and timeline semaphores, common on desktops and recent tablets but not on every older Android tablet); the menu then says why. SDL_GPU is SDL's own renderer, kept for a driver that misbehaves with the app's. Both draw the same image, pixel for pixel. Mac and iPad offer only Automatic and SDL_GPU, which draws with Metal there, until the app has a Metal backend of its own. `--gpu` overrides the choice for one run. Script: `set graphics vulkan` (or `auto`, `sdl-gpu`); remembered with the settings.

## Brush presets

A preset is a named brush, as in Nomad or Blender: a base brush with its own strength, falloff and focal shift, alpha (with its mode and angle), stroke kind, lazy mouse, Lock plane and Connected only, paint colour and cloth mode. Size, the pen settings, symmetry and front faces only stay the same for every brush, so changing brush never changes how big it is. The Brush palette's first section, Preset, names the one the brush is on even while folded ("Preset: Soft clay · changed"). On a plain brush it offers Save as preset (named "Clay 1", "Clay 2", ...), which keeps the brush as it is and puts it on the new preset, and Reset brush, which puts the plain brush's strength, falloff and focal shift back to a fresh install's. Its list picks any preset, or None for the plain brush. On a preset: its name, to type a new one (Enter or a tap elsewhere renames it; an empty name, another preset's or a brush's own is refused), Save changes and Revert (while it differs from what was saved), Save as new preset (a copy), and under More options Delete preset.
