The brush's own slots hold the preset's values while it is on one, and leaving it (picking a brush by its key, the shelf, the pie or the list) gives the plain brush its own back, so a preset never changes a plain brush and what is remembered for it. The palette finds Save brush as preset, Save preset changes, Revert preset, Delete preset and Reset brush.

**The brush browser** (`src/app/kit_brushes.odin`) is laid out as ZBrush for iPad's Brushes palette: it opens under the menu from All in Search or B while a workspace hides the shelf (as Inspect does); with the shelf shown, All and B open the brush picker over it instead (see Interface). Its header holds Categories (which hides or shows the column at its left), the title, Search and Close. Down its left are the current brush's card (a large tile of it, whose tap or long press shows its settings, its name and what it is: a brush, a library preset, one of yours or a pack's, or a tool; and a heart that keeps it in Favorites) and the categories, each with how many it holds: Recent (the last twelve brushes, presets and tools picked, from the browser, the shelf, a key or a pie, the last first), Favorites (in the order the heart added them), All, Clay (Draw, Clay, Clay strips, Layer, Inflate and the library's Standard, Clay buildup and Blob), Crease (Crease, Pinch, Sharp crease, Slash, Hatch), Smooth (Smooth, Flatten, Trim dynamic, Hard polish, Trim, Fill, Scrape and the three Smooth presets), Move (Grab, Snake hook, Elastic, Move, Move connected, Move all depth, Move topological), Cloth, Mask (the Mask brush, Mask rectangle and Mask lasso), Paint, Clip and hide (Hide rectangle, Hide lasso, Clip, Slice and Knife, as the reference app's SelectRect, SelectLasso, ClipCurve, SliceCurve and KnifeCurve), Curve and insert (the two tools), History (Morph, History), My presets, and each content pack's presets under its name. The built-in library is the brushes ZBrush for iPad's library opens on, each a preset over one of ours with the strength, focal shift, alpha and options that make it. In the middle the category's items are large tiles, three to a row and alphabetical (Recent and Favorites in their own order): a lit ball of clay (the paint colour for Paint, dark grey for the masks) with the brush's icon pressed into it, and its name under it (a long one is cut, and shown whole in its tooltip); what is chosen is ringed in the accent (the brush or preset held, the tool, and the mask shape and gesture the modifiers draw, so two can be at once). A tap picks one and closes the browser, a long press (or right-click) shows its settings (a preset's with the Preset section open, a tool's in the tray). A mask shape or a gesture tile makes it what Ctrl + drag on empty space, or Mask on the wheel, draws for the mask shapes, and what Ctrl + Shift + drag, or Hide on the wheel, runs for the rest, leaving the brush held alone and saying so in a note; neither goes in a dock slot. Down the right is an A to Z index: a tap or a drag on a letter scrolls the tiles to the first one starting with it (or with the next letter that has one); letters that start none are faint. Search turns the title into a field that lists, from every category, what has the typed text in its name, in any case. The grid opens scrolled to the brush held and scrolls with a drag or the wheel, as the categories do when they are taller than the room. The category, whether the categories are hidden, Recent and Favorites are remembered (`brush_category`, `brush_sidebar_hidden`, and `brush_recent` and `brush_favorite` lines that name each item by its kind and name, `brush/Clay` or `library/Trim`, so they outlive a preset deleted before one; one naming nothing lists nothing); the exit stats print `brush_category=`, `brush_recent=`, `brush_favorites=`, `brush_sidebar=`, `brush_search=` and `brush_browser_picks=`.

The built-in ones are read-only as a pack's are: Save as new preset makes one of your own from one ("Blob 1"), and their names are refused for your own (a preset of yours from before them by such a name is kept as "Trim 1" and so on, with the brush on it); the Preset section's list has them after yours, scripts name them by name (`preset Smooth valleys`), and settings.txt keeps the brush on one by name. Up to 24 presets of your own; they are remembered with the settings, and an alpha is remembered by name, so a loaded image's comes back only when that image is loaded again (the built-in ones always do).

## Remembered settings

An interactive run remembers, for the next one, the brush (which one, its size, each brush's strength, falloff curve, focal shift, hardness and falloff shape, as `focal <brush> <shift>` and `hardness <brush> <share>` lines for the brushes where they are not 0 and a `falloff_shape <brush> sphere|projected` line for each), the pen settings (pressure, its curve, tilt), lazy mouse, Smoothing, Pressure smoothing and Catch up on lift, the stroke, front faces only, Lock plane, Connected only, All depth, Grab's mode, Normal weight, Same side, Silhouette and Topological, Elastic's Volume, Snake hook's Pinch, Rake and Elastic tip, Smooth only, symmetry (the axes, Local, and Radial with its copies and axis), the paint channel and both colours, the dynamic topology detail, the alpha (by reference, a `brush_alpha` line), its mode and angle, the material new objects take (with the Custom one and the MatCap image loaded last) and Dim the others, the viewport's Exposure, Cavity and Ridge, the lights, flat shading, wireframe and what it shows, orthographic view and the field of view, the floor grid, the background and Transparency, the Scene palette's shape and size for new objects, the mask steps and mask shape, and the command palette's recent rows, the scenes Home lists under Recent (`recent_scenes`, paths escaped as %20, newest first; written as soon as a scene is saved or opened), and the brush presets with the one the brush was on (`preset` and `brush_preset` lines, names escaped as %20), and the dock's slots (`dock <slot> brush <brush>` or `dock <slot> preset <name>`), and the keys given to commands (`key` lines; see Controls), the pies that were changed and the starred commands (`pie` and `favourite` lines; see Pies). So do the Settings panel's choices (the interface size, the brush ring, Show every option, how far the first-run tips got, the camera and touch settings with what the view turns about and the safe zone, the Stats panel, the frame rate and its limit, Rest when idle, anti-aliasing, the graphics backend, how often to autosave and how many earlier saves to keep; see Settings), and where each panel window was moved to and whether it is folded (`window` lines). They go to `settings.txt` in SDL's per-user folder (`%APPDATA%\Implicit\Sculpt` on Windows, `~/.local/share/Implicit/Sculpt` on Linux) when the app closes, when the Settings panel closes and when the app goes to the background, as plain `key values` lines after a `version` line; delete the file to start from the defaults. A file saved before version 2 holds the trackball orbit, the default then, so it opens with the turntable, the default now; a style picked after that is kept. Runs with `--frames`, `--gpubench`, `--bench-stroke` or `--no-settings` neither read nor write it (`--settings-file=PATH` reads and writes PATH instead, scripted runs included), so scripted runs and benchmarks stay the same everywhere, and flags given on the command line win over it. Which sections are open is not remembered, nor are the scene, the speed toggles in the Stats panel or Smooth all's "On the GPU" switch.

## Cloth brush

The Cloth brush (no key; in the brush browser and the pie) turns the surface in and around the brush into cloth for the length of a stroke, as Blender's cloth brush does, so it folds and wrinkles instead of stretching. Its mode, in the Brush palette's Stroke section: Drag (what is under the brush follows the pointer on the plane facing the camera, as Grab, and the cloth around it is pulled along into folds), Gravity (the region sags toward the bottom of the screen while the button is held, settling at about half a radius at full strength; Ctrl lifts it), Pinch (gathers the cloth toward the brush into radial folds, easing in over the first 40 frames up to 55% of the way at full strength; Ctrl expands) and Expand (pushes it outward so it buckles into a ring ridge; Ctrl pinches). The simulation (`src/sculpt/cloth.odin`) is position-based dynamics in the object's space: a damped Verlet step with the mode's force, then five Jacobi rounds over every triangle edge of the region that keep the edges at their lengths, puff squeezed cloth out along its starting normals so it folds outward together, and even out each vertex's displacement with its neighbours' (bending), so folds stay smooth on dense meshes. The region's outer 30% is increasingly held where it was, and masked or hidden vertices are held completely, so the cloth stays sewn to the rest of the mesh. It runs every frame while the button is down, even with the pointer still; the stroke is one undo step. Symmetry is not applied. With dynamic topology on it simulates the triangles as they are, splitting nothing while it runs, and at the stroke's end splits what it stretched down to the detail size, in the same undo step (see Dynamic topology). Cost on the test VM (4 threads, release): on a 1M-vertex sphere about 2.3 ms a frame with a 60 px brush; at 10M (about 170k simulated vertices) about 18 ms a frame and 50-150 ms to set up at the start of a stroke, so it is a slower brush than the others, which it leaves untouched. Script: `brush cloth`, `set cloth_mode gravity` (or `drag`, `pinch`, `expand`).

## Render

The Scene palette's Render section has one **Render** switch (Shift+R, the right bar's sun button, and the command "Render", found as "bpr" too) and its **Quality**, remembered: **Fast** (the default, below) lays occlusion and soft shadows over the viewport's own shading, MatCaps and classic shading included, is quick even on tablets and runs on every GPU; **Full** (render mode, further below) path traces the view instead, for the finished look. The switch runs one quality at a time: picking the other while it is on swaps them, and the command "Full render" turns it on at Full directly. Fast's on or off is remembered, and the Sculpt studio look turns it on (a Full render running stays); Full is off at every start. Each quality's own controls show under the tabs; the section's summary says Off, Fast or Full. Occlusion while working is in the Look section. Script: `studio_render on` (the switch, at the Quality), `render_mode on` (Full), `set render_quality full` (`fast`; `preview`, Fast's name before, still reads as Fast, so older settings files and scripts keep working). The exit stats add `render_switch`, `render_quality` and `studio`.

### Fast

Fast builds the viewport up into ambient occlusion and soft shadows from every light while the view stays still, like a path-traced preview: crevices and creases deepen, and raised forms cast soft shadows onto what is behind them and onto other objects. Each still frame adds one light sample (`src/render/studio.odin`): it renders every object's depth from a direction into a 2048² shadow map, then for every viewport pixel reconstructs the surface point and normal from the viewport's depth and adds whether that light reaches it (3x3 filtered) to a half-float accumulation image. Three samples in four come from directions spread evenly all round the scene (an R2 sequence), weighted by how directly they face the surface, which gives the occlusion; one in four comes from one of the lights, jittered over its disc, which gives the soft shadows. Each of those picks a light by its share of the lights' brightness (a dim fill gets few samples), and counts at a pixel by how much of the light there that light gives, the ambient light included, so a shadow takes away only the light it blocks: a pixel lit mostly by the key darkens fully in the key's shadow, one lit by a dim fill and the ambient light only a little in the fill's. A sun's disc is as wide as its Angle, a point light or spot is a sphere of its Radius whose rays stop at it, and a light of size 0 takes Shadow softness. The image shown is the viewport darkened by both, by the Occlusion and Shadow strengths in the Render section's Fast; it settles within the Samples count (256 by default, at most 1024), after which it costs nothing. That section shows the count while it builds. Any change to the viewport, a stroke, the camera, a resize, starts it over; until the view is still again the plain viewport is copied through, so with it on a stroke costs one screen-sized copy a frame (10M stroke on the test VM: CPU frame p50 3.8-3.9 ms off and 4.8 ms on, where the llvmpipe software GPU makes that copy on the CPU; on a real GPU it is a fraction of a millisecond). Screenshots, `--dump-viewport` and the time-lapse save what the viewport shows. Shadow softness (under More options, 0.6° to 29°, 3.4° by default) is the radius of a light without a size of its own, how wide its shadow's soft edge spreads; a change starts the samples over. A change to Occlusion or Shadow once the samples are done shows at once. Whether it is on, its Occlusion, Shadow and Shadow softness are remembered with the settings. It needs a depth format shaders can read; where the device has none it logs a warning, the section says so, and the plain viewport shows (or live occlusion, when that is on). Script: `studio_render on`, `set studio_samples 64`, `set studio_softness 0.15` (radians).

Turning, panning or zooming the view keeps what Fast built, as EEVEE's and Marmoset Toolbag's raster modes keep their shading while the camera moves: each frame of the move carries the samples to where their surface now is and adds one more (`studio_reproject.frag.slang`). A pixel keeps last frame's samples only where last frame saw the same surface point there (its depth lies on this pixel's surface plane), read over the four pixels around the spot, each tested on its own; nothing is clamped to its neighbours' colours as TAA does, which would eat the occlusion. What a pixel carries is capped at about 32 ambient samples and 4 light samples, half a second of new ones, so a turn's stale samples fade as the eye follows; a surface the move uncovers shows Occlusion while working's picture until about 16 samples land on it. The occlusion always carries; the shadows only when every light stands still in the world (lights fixed to the scene). Lights fixed to the camera turn with it, so their shadows are left out while the view moves and build again once it rests. A stroke, a moved object, a light or a resize still starts over; a change of material or colour alone keeps the samples. Above 2 million triangles a moving frame only carries and adds no sample (each would draw every triangle into the shadow map), except where samples are traced. A view that never moves draws exactly as before. The exit stats add `studio_samples`, `studio_sequence`, `studio_carried`, `studio_carries`, `studio_carried_bare` and `studio_restarts`.

Where the device allows, the samples are traced instead of shadow-mapped (`src/render/trace.odin`): each still frame casts one ray per pixel from the surface toward that sample's light against the real triangles, so occlusion and the lights' shadows keep detail finer than a shadow map's texel (about 1/1000 of the scene), and nothing in a shadow map can shadow a surface itself. Occlusion rays are cosine-weighted about the surface and unbounded; light rays aim at the picked light over its disc, and toward a point light or spot end at its sphere, so what stands beyond the lamp casts nothing; the shadow-mapped fallback casts a point light's shadow along one direction from the scene's middle. Each ray's directions are turned by a hash of its pixel, so the image settles as fine grain rather than bands. Every mesh keeps a tree over each chunk's triangles (leaves of up to 12, about 11-12 bytes a triangle on the GPU) and a tree over its shown chunks. A chunk's tree is built on the CPU, on all threads, only when its triangles change (remesh, levels, HD, dynamic topology, a voxel stroke; hiding does not count, since hidden triangles are skipped). It is refit on the GPU when only vertices moved (any stroke), and the chunks' tree is rebuilt only when their boxes moved or which are hidden changed. All of it happens at the first sample of a still view, so a stroke never waits for it. Cost on the test VM (4 threads, release, llvmpipe): the 3.8M-triangle head scan builds in 0.18 s (44 MB), a 500k one in 26 ms. See-through objects cast nothing. Meshes the device will not let a compute shader read, a tablet's mesh over 128 MB a buffer, or `--no-trace` keep the shadow maps, which give the same image as before. The traced result agrees with the shadow-mapped one to within 12 of 255 on all but a few silhouette pixels, where the shadow map's offsets had darkened edges. Bounded occlusion rays (a Reach, as Blender's AO Distance) are later work. The exit stats add `trace` (samples traced), `trace_mb`, `trace_build_ms` and `trace_chunks_built`.

Occlusion while working (Scene > Look, with its Reach under More options; also a command, found as "ssao" or "ambient occlusion" in the palette) darkens creases and contact every frame, while sculpting and turning the view too, where the studio render waits for a still view. It is screen-space ambient occlusion, GTAO as Blender's EEVEE and Intel's XeGTAO compute it (`src/render/live_ao.odin`). For each pixel it reads the camera's depth along two directions out to Reach (under More options: a share of the scene's radius, 0.1 by default, 0.01 to 0.4, at most 48 pixels on screen), finds how high the surroundings rise either side, and integrates the cosine-weighted sky that leaves. The two directions turn, and the steps along them shift, in a 4 x 4 pattern that a 4 x 4 blur then averages away, weighted by depth so it does not bleed across silhouettes. That gives 32 directions a pixel with no noise left over and nothing that changes from frame to frame. It darkens the finished image with the studio render's Occlusion strength and curve, so it works on MatCaps as well as on the lit materials, the view does not jump when the studio render takes over, and the background is untouched. With the studio render on as well, it shows until the studio has built up 16 samples, a quarter of a second at 60 frames a second, so a stroke keeps its occlusion where the studio starts over. With partial redraw, a stroke's frame reruns the occlusion only over the rectangle the viewport redrew, padded by how far it reads, and keeps the rest. The result is the same to the bit as a full pass, with anti-aliasing off or 4x (which draws a single-sampled copy of the camera's depth, again only over that rectangle). Tablets work it out at half size each way and upsample it in the blur. Open surfaces keep their shading, and the seam where two spheres meet darkens about as much as the converged studio render darkens it (within about 10 of 255 across it). Script: `set live_ao on` (remembered with the settings), `set live_ao_reach 0.2`, `set studio_ao 1`. The exit stats add `live_ao`, `live_ao_full`, `live_ao_partial` and `live_ao_skipped` (frames it ran whole, over a rectangle, and not at all).

### Full: render mode

The Render section at Full quality, render mode (also the command "Full render", found as "render mode", "path trace" or "cycles" in the palette), path traces the view while it stays still, as Blender's rendered viewport does with Cycles: every light in the scene casts a soft shadow, light bounces between surfaces (a chin lights the neck from below, a coloured object tints its neighbour), and the materials are taken as physically based surfaces. It builds up a sample a pixel a still frame and shows from the fourth sample on; until then the viewport shows as it otherwise would (the studio render's or live occlusion's image when on). Any change, a stroke, the camera, a light, a material, starts it over, so it costs nothing while sculpting. To keep the render beside the view you sculpt in rather than in place of it, Camera > Render preview pane shows it in a view pane instead (View grid, above). It stops at Samples (1024 by default, 16 to 4096) and follows each path for up to Bounces surfaces (6 by default, 1 to 12; 1 is direct light only). Denoise (on by default) smooths the grain each time a sample is in, with the edge-avoiding À-Trous wavelet filter (Dammertz et al. 2010, `render_atrous.frag.slang`): the first sample goes through each pixel's centre and keeps what it met's colour, normal and distance as guides, and five passes of a 5 x 5 kernel with taps 1 to 16 pixels apart blur the light with the colour divided out, each tap counting less where the guides say it is another surface or its light differs by more than the samples' noise explains, so creases, texture, paint and shadow edges stay sharp. It takes a pass fewer each time the samples grow four times past 64, down to two. The head at 16 samples looks finished where the raw image is grain. The section shows how many samples it has built and how long one takes. How it lights (`src/render/path.odin`, `render_trace.comp.slang`):
- The lights are the viewport's, in the world, at their own size (a sun's Angle, a lamp's Radius, in the Lights section); a light of size 0 takes the studio's Shadow softness instead, a sun spanning a cone that wide and a point or spot light a sphere that wide seen from the scene's middle, each as bright as the viewport makes it where its light arrives. At each bounce one light is picked by its share of the brightness and sampled toward its shadow ray, and the surface's own reflection direction is weighed against it (multiple importance sampling), so small lights and wide ones both settle quickly.
- A path that leaves the scene takes the environment: the image environment at its Strength, or the built-in studio (the soft sky, the softbox, strip and card the materials reflect) at the ambient light's strength.
- Physically based materials keep their metallic and roughness (GGX with visible-normal sampling); the classic shading becomes a dielectric whose roughness matches its highlight's width, and a MatCap object its colour, half rough. Polypaint, polygroup colours and polygroups' own materials show as in the viewport. A textured object (one with maps, while View > Textures shows them) renders as the viewport shows it: Maps gives its base colour map, the roughness and metallic of its ORM map and its normal map, read in the frame the bake made it in; Normal gives the normal map over its material; Checker the UV checker. The maps are read at the size of a pixel where the camera's ray meets them, and coarser along later bounces; the ORM map's occlusion is left out, since the paths find occlusion themselves, as Cycles does with a glTF's. Where a normal map tilts the normal so far that a smooth surface would mirror into itself, the normal is bent just enough not to, so no black specks show. The maps of three textured objects show at once (the sampler slots a GPU stage has); further ones render in their material, and the Render section says how many. See-through objects are left out.
The scene is copied on the GPU into one set of buffers at the first sample of a still view, reusing each mesh's trees from the traced studio render, with a tree over every object's chunks built on the CPU; render mode needs a device that can trace (as the studio does) and write 32-bit float images from a compute shader, and otherwise the section says so. Tablets refuse a scene over 256 MB of copies. A frame takes a band of rows of one sample, as many as keep frames under about 50 ms, so the interface stays responsive. The image is the same every run. Half resolution (on by default on tablets, off on the desktop) traces half the viewport's size each way, a quarter of the pixels, and lays each traced pixel's light smoothly over the viewport, blended by how much of it met the scene so silhouettes stay soft over the viewport's own background: about three times as fast (0.2 s a sample against 0.55 s for three spheres on the test VM), a little softer. Render mode is off at every start; Samples, Bounces, Denoise and Half resolution are remembered. Cost on the test VM (llvmpipe software GPU, 1600 x 900): about 0.5 s a sample for three spheres, 0.8-0.9 s for a 500k-triangle head (20 MB of copies), 2.5 s for the 3.8M-triangle head scan (154 MB); a real GPU is far faster. An image environment is also sampled by its brightness, so a sun in the panorama lights the scene in a few samples where it used to show as scattered bright specks: each surface a path meets takes, besides a light, a direction the panorama's bright parts are likelier to give, in proportion to how much light the environment gives against the lights, weighed against the bounces that find it so nothing counts twice. Render mode reads the image at its full size, not the blurred copy the viewport reflects. The built-in studio is broad enough that bounces find it well, so it is sampled this way only when every light is off. Script: `render_mode on`, `set render_samples 64`, `set render_bounces 3`, `set render_denoise off`, `set render_half on`. The exit stats add `render`, `render_samples`, `render_ms` (a sample's time), `render_mb` (the scene copies) and `render_failed`.

**Depth of field.** The Scene palette's Lens section (after Render) gives render mode a camera's lens: **Depth of field** (off by default) blurs what is nearer or farther than the point in focus, and **Blur** (0 to 1, 0.4 by default) is how wide the lens is, its radius a quarter of the scene's radius at 1, so it reads the same for any model size (a scene has no physical scale, so an f-stop would mean nothing). The focus is the pivot the view turns about until **Pick focus** (in the section, or found as "focus" or "depth of field" in the palette) takes a point from the model, as KeyShot's pick focus point and Blender's focus eyedropper do: the next press on the model puts the focus where it meets the surface, a drag moves it with the finger, a press that meets nothing leaves it as it was, and Escape stops it waiting. A picked point stays in focus while the view turns round it; **Focus on the pivot** goes back, and New scene and Open do too. While Pick focus waits, and while the Lens section is open with depth of field on, a focus mark (a ring with four ticks) shows where the focus is. Each sample traces from a point on the lens (a thin lens across the camera's right and up, through where the pixel's ray meets the plane of focus facing the camera), so the blur is the real one, with near edges blurring over what is behind them and no halo; render mode then lays itself over the background and floor alone, never the sharp viewport, and the denoiser's guides become the mean colour, normal and distance over the samples rather than the first one's, so they blur as the image does. It works in orthographic views too. Any change to it starts render mode over; off, every image is as before, bit for bit. Depth of field and Blur are remembered (`render_dof`, `render_blur`); the point in focus is not, nor kept in the scene file. Script: `set render_dof on`, `set render_blur 0.5`, `render_focus 0 0 1`, `render_focus pivot`. The exit stats add `render_dof`, `render_blur`, `focus_picked`, `focus` (x,y,z) and `focus_armed`.

### Finish

The Scene palette's Finish section (after Lens) finishes the picture as a camera does, while the Render switch is on (Fast or Full): Marmoset Toolbag keeps the same controls on its camera, and Blender in its colour management. Sculpting with Render off shows the viewport unfinished, so picked colours are the colours shown. **Tone curve** picks how light comes into the display's range: **Neutral** (the default, the curve every view uses: colours below about 0.8 pass through), **AgX** (Blender's filmic method, built here from its description: bright light rolls off over more stops toward white without skewing hue, with Blender 4's restored colour in the midtones) or **ACES** (Stephen Hill's fit of the film industry's transform: more contrast and colour in bright light). AgX and ACES are scaled so mid grey shows as Neutral shows it, so changing the curve changes how light rolls off, not how bright the picture is. **Contrast** (0.7 to 1.5) steepens or flattens the light about mid grey, **Temperature** (2500 to 12000 K, 6500 none) is the white balance (a higher temperature warms the picture, as Lightroom's and Blender's do; Bradford's cone space), **Vignette** darkens toward the corners, **Bloom** scatters light as a lens does (a glow where bright meets dark: a share of each pixel's light is swapped for the light blurred around it, so the picture grows no brighter in all; unlike EEVEE Next it stays live in the viewport). Under More: **Sharpen** is contrast-adaptive (AMD's CAS method: midtone detail gains, edges near black or white get no halo, as an unsharp mask's do; unlike CAS it leaves alone the dither in a smooth gradient), **Grain** adds film grain, strongest in the midtones, **Saturation**, **Tint** (toward magenta above 0, green below), **Lift**, **Gamma** and **Gain** (the blacks, midtones and whites, on the encoded values as a grading panel's wheels), **Vignette softness**, **Grain size**, **Bloom size** (how far the glow reaches), **Bloom threshold** (0, the default, lets all light scatter, as a real lens does; above it only light brighter than it glows, fading in over a soft knee), **Aberration** (colour fringes toward the corners, as a lens bends each wavelength a little differently) and **Reset finish** (also in the palette as "finish"). The aberration, bloom, vignette, white balance, contrast and saturation work on linear light before the curve, as a lens and a sensor would; the grade, sharpening and grain after it.

It is one full-screen pass over what the viewport would show (the studio's Fast, Full's samples, live occlusion or the plain viewport), skipped while nothing changed; Bloom adds a chain of half sizes before it (down to 1/64 of the picture, after the Call of Duty: Advanced Warfare chain, a bright pixel's weight evened out so it cannot pulse the glow) and Sharpen a second pass after it, which then adds the grain. The viewport image holds display values, so the pass first turns them back into the light Neutral made them from (its exact inverse); what an 8-bit step loses near white, and colours Neutral never makes (a MatCap, wires, a picked background), carry over as they were, so with every control at none the picture changes by at most one 8-bit step. Full hands it the traced light itself, so its curves are exact. Grain is fixed to the pixel and moves on only when the picture changes, so a still view stays still. Transparent screenshots finish their second drawing the same way. The second view, view panes and view windows finish their own picture the same way while they show Studio or Render, the two rendered passes; their other passes stay a look at one signal, unfinished. With every control at none (the default) nothing runs and every image is as before, bit for bit. The controls are remembered (`finish_curve` neutral/agx/aces, `finish_contrast`, `finish_saturation`, `finish_temperature`, `finish_tint`, `finish_vignette`, `finish_vignette_softness`, `finish_lift`, `finish_gamma`, `finish_gain`, `finish_grain`, `finish_grain_size`, `finish_bloom`, `finish_bloom_size`, `finish_bloom_threshold`, `finish_sharpen`, `finish_aberration`), which scripts set too (`set finish_curve agx`). The exit stats add `finish` (whether it ran), `finish_curve`, `finish_passes` and `finish_failed`.

## History timeline and time-lapse

The History panel (the clock button beside undo and redo) opens over the brush dock and lays every undo step on one bar, oldest at the left: a block per step, coloured by what it was (brush strokes gold, mask changes blue, hiding grey, Smooth all and Clip green, rebuilds such as remesh or add and delete orange), dimmed once undone. Press or drag anywhere on the bar to go to that point (a finger or the pen goes there on a tap, or scrubs once it moves past the slop); resting the pointer on it shows the step's brush or command, its object and how long ago it was made. The outer buttons jump to the start and the newest step, the ones beside them step one at a time, and Play replays the History forward at the Steps a second speed (from the start when it stands at the end). It moves only with undo and redo, as many as it takes, so it is exactly Ctrl+Z or Ctrl+Shift+Z pressed that many times: a stroke made after scrubbing back drops the steps ahead of it, and what the undo memory limits dropped is not on the bar. Scrubbing spends at most 25 ms a frame stepping, so a long drag stays responsive; a stroke undo takes about 2 ms at 10M vertices in a release build on the test VM, so the whole History of 40 strokes there rewinds in well under a second.

A right-click, or a long press of a finger or the pen, on a done step makes it what the History brush paints back to (to before it, on that step's object), without moving through the History, and a note names it; the step must be on the level (or HD window) its object shows, and the History must not be moving; a small mark at the foot of the bar shows where the selected object's History brush point is, and resting on that step says so. A step that is not sculpting (a move of the object, a change to the scene tree, a rebuild), or out of the brush's reach, is refused with a note. The panel's Marker button does the same for a tap: while it is lit, a tap (or click) on a done step sets the History brush point there instead of going there, and the button goes out after one mark; a second press turns it off unused.

Settings > System > Undo steps kept (No limit, the default, or 20 to 500; `undo_steps` in the settings file and scripts) keeps only that many of the newest steps: once a step more is made, the oldest goes, and its strokes go from their object's undo memory too, so the memory comes back. A step of several entries (a move of several objects at once) counts once and goes whole, so "Step N of M" on the bar, which counts entries, can read more than the limit. Nothing is dropped while a stroke, a drag or the History itself is moving. **Delete older history** (the History panel, or the command palette) forgets every step that can be undone and frees their memory; the steps undone ahead of the bar stay, so Redo still works. It takes two taps: the first turns the button red ("Tap again to delete") for three seconds. A note says how many steps went and how much memory came back; it cannot be undone, and the History brush point goes with them.

Record time-lapse... asks for a folder, rewinds the History to the start, then redoes Steps a frame steps each frame up to where the timeline stood and saves the viewport after each as `frame_00000.png`, `frame_00001.png` and so on, at full detail; with Turn above 0 the camera also turns that many degrees around the scene each frame, as a turntable, and goes back where it was afterwards. Any video tool turns the frames into a film (`ffmpeg -framerate 12 -i frame_%05d.png sculpt.mp4`). A stroke, or Stop recording, ends it early. Flags: `--timelapse=DIR` records once `--bench-stroke` ends (or at the start), and the run lasts until it is done; `--timelapse-steps=N` and `--timelapse-spin=DEG` set Steps a frame and Turn; `--bench-lifts=N` lifts the synthetic pen N times so the stroke becomes N + 1 steps.

## Stroke recorder

The Brush palette's Stroke recorder keeps strokes to play back, as ZBrush's Record and Replay Last do. The app always keeps the last stroke as it was drawn: every frame's pointer samples, pen pressure and tilt, Shift and Ctrl (and the modifier buttons), the camera and viewport size, and the brush settings. Record starts a recording that keeps every stroke until Stop recording. Replay plays the recording, or else the last stroke, back where it was drawn, with the camera it was drawn with, whatever the view is now. R over the viewport plays it so its first stroke starts under the pointer, in the current view and size, which turns a stroke or a set of strokes into a stamp to lay anywhere, on any object (a rivet, a scale, a monogram, a spiral). With Recorded brushes off the path is replayed with the brush as set now: record a path once, then lay it with Clay, then Crease. Instant plays every recorded frame at once instead of one a frame, as drawn. Escape stops a replay, and switching to Move does too.

A replay feeds the recorded frames through the same code a drawn stroke goes through (`src/app/replay.odin`, `sculpt_frame`), so lazy mouse, symmetry, Drag rectangle, Spray, Roll, Grab, Snake hook and Cloth replay as they were drawn, each replayed stroke is one undo step and shows on the History timeline, and a replay where it was drawn on the same mesh gives the same vertices, bit for bit, as the original strokes (checked frame by frame and Instant on a 200k sphere: three strokes recorded with `--record-strokes`, saved, then replayed on a new sphere, export files identical). Instant brings normals up to date between recorded frames (`sculpt.sculptor_refresh_normals`), as frame-by-frame drawing does. While a replay runs the pointer does not sculpt; the camera still moves.

Save strokes... writes the recording (or else the last stroke) to a `.strokes` file and Load strokes... reads one into the recording, to replay in another scene or share. A file keeps each stroke's brush kind, size, strength, falloff and focal shift, pressure settings and curve, lazy mouse, Front faces only, stroke mode, Spray's spread and the stroke's seed, Line and Join strokes, Lock plane and Connected only, All depth and Smooth only, spacing and colour spray, Smooth blurs, Invert erases, symmetry, paint colour, cloth mode and the stroke's strength scale (Touch strength, for a finger's stroke); the alpha, pen tilt strength and dynamic topology detail come from the brush as set when it is loaded, since an alpha names an image file that may not be there. Every number in a file is checked (finite, in range) before it reaches a brush, and a damaged file is refused. A frame costs 60 bytes plus 21 a pointer sample in the file and a little more in memory. Version 3 files keep whether a frame's stroke painted the mask through the Mask modifier button, and version 4 files each stroke's Spray spread and seed, so a Spray replays as drawn, version 5 files its Line and Join strokes switches, version 6 files its Lock plane and Connected only switches, version 7 files its focal shift, version 8 files its strength scale, so a finger's stroke replays as light as it was drawn whatever points now, and version 9 files local and radial symmetry, and version 10 files the paint channel, and version 11 files All depth and Smooth only, and version 12 files spacing and colour spray, and version 13 files the pen's roll in every frame, so a Pen roll alpha replays turned as drawn, and version 14 files the Smooth blurs, Invert erases switch, and version 15 files the stabiliser's Smoothing, Pressure smoothing and Catch up on lift, and version 16 files Even strength, Scene spacing and Feather, and version 17 files Grab's mode, Normal weight, Same side and Silhouette and whether Alt was held in each frame, and version 18 files Topological, and version 19 files Elastic's Volume, and version 20 files Snake hook's Pinch, Rake and Elastic tip, version 21 the size's own pressure curve, version 22 the smallest size under pressure, version 23 Smooth's Stable, version 24 its mode, version 25 the Samples section, version 26 the falloff's Hardness and shape, version 27 No back and forth, Directional and Size fixed in the scene with the world size of a pixel, version 28 Clay strips' tip, Trim dynamic's angle and Flatten's plane height and depth, and version 29 Grab's Past the outline; versions 1 to 28 still load (Grab strokes from versions 17 to 28 replay with Past the outline on, as they were drawn) (strokes from before version 4 take the spread as set and seed 0, those from before 5 and 6 have the switches they lack off, those from before 7 no focal shift, those from before 8 full strength, those from before 9 no local or radial symmetry, those from before 10 only sculpt, those from before 11 have Grab's round reach and Smooth on everything, those from before 12 a spacing of 25%, the default then, and no colour spray, those from before 13 a pen facing up, those from before 14 the switch off, those from before 15 no smoothing and no catching up, and those from before 16 every dab at the brush's strength, spaced on screen, unfeathered, and those from before 17 Free grabs on both sides with no Alt, and those from before 18 Grab's falloff in a straight line, and those from before 19 the default Volume, and those from before 20 a plain Snake hook, and those from before 21 to 28 one pressure curve, no smallest size, plain Smooth, whole-radius sampling that builds up, a ball falloff with no hardness, strokes both ways at screen size, and a fresh brush's tip, angle and plane reach). Flags: `--record-strokes`, `--save-strokes=PATH`, `--load-strokes=PATH`, `--replay-shift=DX,DY`, `--instant-replay`.

## Controls

Every shortcut, menu row, brush key and tray action button runs a command from one table (`src/app/commands.odin`: name, help, icon, whether it can run now, whether it is on), so the shortcut a menu or tooltip shows is always the one that works. A shortcut's modifiers must match exactly: Ctrl+Z undoes and Ctrl+Shift+Z redoes, and a key held with a modifier it does not list does nothing (Ctrl+B, Shift+1 or Ctrl+F are not shortcuts). Each key press keeps the modifiers held when it went down, so two quick shortcuts in one slow frame both work.

Any command's key can be changed where it is: Ctrl+Alt+click a panel control that runs it (a button, a menu row, a switch, a tab, a tool, a brush tile, a row of the command palette) and, instead of running, it opens Assign key beside the control, as ZBrush's Ctrl+Alt+click and Blender's Assign Shortcut do. The command palette has an Assign key button for the picked row too, which reaches commands that cannot run now (a greyed control takes no click) and works by touch with a keyboard attached. The popover names the command and its keys now, then takes the next key pressed, with Ctrl, Shift or Alt if wanted, as the command's only key: Redo's two keys give way to the one chosen. A key another command has is a question there and then: Use it here moves it (that command keeps any other key, else has none, and the popover says so) and Keep it there leaves it. Space, Escape, Enter, Backspace, Android's Back and the lock keys are refused with the reason, and so are bare S and U (held over the model they size the brush and set its strength) and a key pressed with Cmd or the Windows key. Remove key leaves the command with none; Reset gives it its default keys back. Changes are saved at once (`key <command> <chord|none>` lines in settings.txt); every menu, tooltip, Help > Keys and the palette show the keys as they are. A key given this way runs after the default keys in a frame where several went down. Presets have no keys yet, and a Ctrl+Alt+click on a preset tile picks it as a plain click does.

Files: Ctrl+O opens a scene, Ctrl+S saves it (where it was last saved or opened, else it asks), Ctrl+Shift+S saves it somewhere else. F12 saves a screenshot.

Tools (also on the toolbar): W picks Move (W again steps its gizmo through Move, Turn and Scale), B Sculpt (the brushes), C Curve and I Insert. Ctrl+K or `/` opens the command palette (see Command palette). Q opens Pie 1 and Shift+Q Pie 2 (see Pies). Tab shows or hides the palette tray, F11 the Stats panel, F1 opens Help (every shortcut, the mouse and touch controls, and a glossary), and Escape (or Android's back gesture) closes whatever menu is open. F points the camera at the selected object; Shift+F shows its wireframe; Shift+P the floor grid; Shift+G switches flat shading; Shift+W shows polygroups, Ctrl+W groups the masked part (or what is shown). Delete deletes it (Ctrl+Z brings it back). Ctrl+D subdivides the selected object; D and Shift+D go up and down its levels; Ctrl+Shift+D duplicates it; Ctrl+C copies it and Ctrl+V pastes the copy below the selected object (see the Objects card). Ctrl+R remeshes it at its own vertex count. Ctrl+T turns dynamic topology on or off for it. A opens an HD window where the pointer is on an object with an HD level, or closes the open one.

Move: click or tap an object to select it (a click on empty space clears it, a drag there turns the view), and drag it to move it in the view plane, whatever the gizmo shows. The gizmo has three modes, picked by W again while the Move tool is on (Move, Turn, Scale, Move...), by the action bar's chips or by the Move tab of the inspector, and remembered (`gizmo_mode`, and the Snap latch as `gizmo_snap`):
- Move: drag an arm (red X, green Y, blue Z) to move along that axis, a square between two arms to move in their plane (it takes the colour of the axis it holds still), or the middle square to move in the view plane.
- Turn: drag a ring to turn about that axis (the world's, through the object's origin; the far half of each ring is dimmed), or the white outer ring to turn about the view's axis. A ring seen nearly edge on turns by how far the drag runs along it on screen.
- Scale: drag the middle disc or the circle round it: an arm's length further out scales by e (about 2.7), as far in by its inverse; the scale stays between 0.01 and 100.
While a drag goes, a label by the pointer says what it has done ("X +0.250", "Turn Z +45.0°", "Scale ×1.30"). Ctrl held through a drag snaps it (pressed after the press for a drag on the object's body, since Ctrl at the press starts a topological mask instead, see Posing below), and the Snap latch snaps every drag: moves to whole steps of a grid that follows the zoom (a power of ten about half an arm long on screen), turns to 15°, scales to steps of 0.1; the label names the step. Esc puts the object back mid-drag, and so does a second finger landing (a gesture). Each drag is one undo step. A tap or click on an arm, a ring or the scale disc that moves nothing opens a field over it for the value (with the on-screen keyboard on a tablet): for an arm a number sets the position along it and `+0.5` adds to it, for a ring the number is the turn in degrees, for the scale a number sets it, `+0.5` adds and `*2` (or `x2`) multiplies. Enter or Apply places it as one undo step; Esc, Cancel or a press outside closes it, and text that is no number is refused and says so. Set pivot (the action bar's Pivot chip, the Move tab or the palette), then a tap or click on any surface, puts the gizmo there, so turns and scales go about that point; the selection stays and the latch lets go. The pivot rides with the object as it moves, turns and scales, a dot marks the object's origin meanwhile, and Reset pivot puts the gizmo back on the origin; it belongs to that object only and is not saved. Handles are picked within 8 pixels with the mouse or pen and within half a touch target (24 points) with a finger, wherever they are, on the model or off it; a finger or the pen starts moving only past the slop, so a tap on a handle moves nothing. The Object palette's Transform sets position, turn and scale exactly: drag sideways on a value, or Reset transform (under More options, and in the command palette) to put the object back at the middle, unturned, at scale 1. The exit stats give `gizmo_pivot` (where the pivot is, or none), `gizmo_pivot_latch`, `gizmo_entry` (the handle whose value is open, or none), `gizmo_at`, `gizmo_mode`, `gizmo_hover`, `gizmo_last` (the handle of the last drag that changed something), `gizmo_drags`, `gizmo_snap`, `transform` (the selected object's position, turns and scale) and `transform_undo` (transform steps that can be undone); `scripts/touch_check.py 'gizmo_*'` runs the ring, finger scale, finger-sized handle, plane square, snap, chip, typed value, pivot and cancel scenarios.

Posing: with one object selected and a mask on it that leaves something shown free, the gizmo's handles pose the object's unmasked part instead of moving the object, as ZBrush's gizmo does with a mask (a drag on the object's body still moves all of it). Each vertex takes the move, turn or scale by how free it is: a half-masked vertex moves half as far, scales by half the change, and turns through half the angle at its own distance from the pivot, so the mask's soft edge bends like a joint instead of pinching. The gizmo goes to the middle of the mask's soft edge (its partly masked vertices; with a hard edge, the free vertices touching a masked one; else the free part's middle) whenever pose mode finds a mask it has not placed it for, and Set pivot still puts it anywhere until the mask changes again; a move carries the gizmo along with the part. The action bar's Gizmo group reads Pose meanwhile, the label by the pointer starts "Pose:", and each drag is one undo step ("Pose: Turn" and so on), Esc or a second finger putting the mesh back. A typed value poses too: an offset along the arm, degrees, or a scale factor (`+0.5` adds half). Hidden parts stay still. Posing is refused, with a note, in an HD window and with dynamic topology on. The exit stats give `pose_mode`, `pose_drags` (drags and values that posed) and `pose_moved` (vertices the last pose could move), and `mesh_sum` (the selected object's coordinates added up, so a scenario can tell a mesh put back exactly).

Topological mask: with the Move tool, Ctrl + a press on an object (or a finger or pen press with the rail's Mask on) selects it, and a drag from there masks it along the surface, as ZBrush's topology masking of its gizmo does: free within three quarters of the reach of the press, a smooth band to the reach, masked beyond it, the reach being the drag's length on screen at the press's depth (a circle and "Mask reach" show it). Ctrl+Alt (or Mask with Invert) makes the opposite: masked near the press, free far away. Distance is measured along the triangles' edges (Dijkstra's search from the pressed triangle's corners), so a part the other side of a thin gap stays masked however near it is in space; shapes follow the mesh's edges a little. The search settles at most 50,000 vertices a frame (about 20 ms on the test VM, release build, 655k-vertex sphere; 650k settled in 0.17 s over 9 frames) and carries on after the release until it has reached the reach set last, so a reach across a dense mesh never stalls a frame; starting one on an object with no mask costs what Mask all does (about 40 ms there), since everything far is masked at once; each gesture is one mask undo step, Esc or a second finger puts the mask back, and a tap only selects. Hidden parts keep their mask (the search crosses them). The gizmo then goes to the new mask's soft edge, so the part pressed poses at once. It works with dynamic topology on. The exit stats give `topo_masks` (gestures that masked) and `topo_reach` (the last one's reach in the object's own units).

Brush: left drag sculpts the object under the cursor, which becomes selected (Shift smooths, Ctrl inverts; the Mask brush then erases). The brush is the one the press chose for the whole stroke: Shift, the rail's Smooth or Mask, or a brush key pressed mid-stroke leave it as it began. Shift (or the rail's Smooth) pressed mid-stroke instead lays the stroke again as a straight line from where it began to the pointer, as 3DCoat's Shift line and ZBrush's Lazy Mouse line do: what the stroke did is put back from the undo copies it keeps (`sculptor_stroke_restore`) and laid again whenever the pointer moves, so it stays one undo step; a line longer than a frame's dabs (64) is laid over the next frames and finished at the release; letting Shift go lays the line to where the pointer last was with it held, then carries on freehand from there, so where a line ends depends only on the pointer's path, never on how fast the frames were, and a replay lays it the same. A straight line ignores the lazy mouse; a viewport resized mid-line leaves it as it was laid. Not with dynamic topology, whose strokes cannot be put back, nor Grab, Snake hook, Cloth or Rectangle strokes. A take recorded before this with Shift pressed mid-stroke replays as such a line; a left drag that starts on empty space turns the view instead, as in ZBrush, and keeps turning it when it crosses the model (Settings > Touch makes the mouse wait for the model instead). Keys 1 to 9 and 0 pick the first ten brushes, X toggles X symmetry, V swaps the main and secondary colours, Shift+C picks the colour under the pointer (the Eyedropper), Alt+M clears the mask and Ctrl+I inverts it. Ctrl + drag from empty space masks a rectangle or lasso (Ctrl+Alt unmasks). Ctrl+Shift + drag runs the chosen gesture tool: hides by rectangle (the default) or lasso (Alt hides the inside), clips along a line (Alt: the other side) or slices along a line; a Ctrl+Shift click shows everything. Ctrl+H shows everything, Ctrl+Shift+H inverts what is hidden. The brush follows every mouse or pen sample, not just where the pointer is once a frame: dabs go along the path the pointer took on screen, a fifth of the radius apart (Spacing under Stroke > More changes that), each where that point meets the surface. Pen pressure scales the strength, and the size too with "Pressure changes size" in the Brush palette's Pen section, through the pressure curve there; a mouse counts as full pressure. Space (hold and let go, or tap) or a right click without dragging opens the brush pie (see Pen and brush pie above). S over the viewport sizes the brush where it is (move away from or toward the circle's middle, then click or press S again; a right click or Escape puts the old size back), and U does the same for the strength; [ and ] step the size, Shift+[ and Shift+] the strength. Each brush keeps its own strength, and Shift-smoothing uses the Smooth brush's. The brush ring takes the colour the reference app gives its cursor for what a press will do: red to sculpt, pink to sculpt inverted, blue to smooth, yellow to paint the mask and white to hide (a selection), from the keys held or the modifier wheel, and more opaque while pressing. Lazy mouse (Stroke > More) makes the brush trail the cursor on a string that many pixels long, for steadier lines. Line (Stroke > More, `stroke_line` in settings.txt) makes every stroke a straight line from its press, redone as the pointer moves, for a pen or finger with no Shift; the Stroke section says so while it is on. Join strokes (Stroke > More, `stroke_join`) carries a stroke on from where the last one ended when it is pressed within 24 pixels of that end, on the same object with the view unchanged: the dab spacing and direction go on as if the pointer had never let go, so a line drawn in pieces has no blob or gap at the joins (each piece is still its own undo step). Replays and recordings start without a stroke to join, and undo, redo or opening a scene forgets the last stroke's end, so a press then starts its own stroke; replaying the last stroke when it joined another lays it unjoined. The exit stats count both as `stroke_lines` and `stroke_joins`. Over the model the brush ring lies on the surface, as in ZBrush: a circle of the brush's size in the plane the surface faces under it (the vertex normals the brush would reach, weighted as a dab weighs them, so it shows the way the brush pushes), seen in perspective, with a short tick out along that normal from its middle; off the model it is a flat circle on the screen (Settings > Interface turns this off). With an alpha (see Brush alphas above) the outline is the alpha's square. R over the viewport replays the recorded strokes (or else the last stroke) starting under the pointer, and Escape stops a replay (see Stroke recorder above).

**The Objects card** (Objects card in Search, or a bar button) is the quick way to the objects, laid out as ZBrush for iPad's Subtools card; the Scene palette's Objects section stays as the other place to reach them, as ZBrush keeps Tool > Subtool. It opens beside the view rail in the palette tray's place: opening the tray (or Settings, Help or Macros) closes it, and opening it closes the tray. Its row of dots on top drags it anywhere (remembered with the other panels); holding the dots puts it back in the tray, which opens on the Scene palette's Objects. Under the title are Add (a menu: Insert puts the picked shape below the selected object, in its folder; Append at the end of the list; then Import mesh), Duplicate, New folder and More (Rename, All low and All high, which show every object at its lowest or highest subdivision level, Copy, Paste, Duplicate, Delete, Delete other and Delete all). Each object has a tall row: a picture of it, its name with its boolean switch under it (on makes it a Subtract in booleans; the row's menu picks Add, Subtract or Intersect), and at the right its polypaint toggle and its eye. A folder's row shows a folder with how many objects are in it, its name, a chevron that folds it and its eye. The pictures are drawn from the front, turned as the object is, in a light clay with its polypaint; one is drawn again once its object has stayed unchanged for 0.4 s, never during a stroke or while the view moves and at most one a frame, so sculpting costs nothing until the stroke ends and then one draw of the object (a full draw of a 10M-vertex object, about 20M triangles, once). A tap on a row selects it (Shift or Ctrl + tap, or Select several, adds it); a long press (or right-click) opens its menu: on the selected object Rename, Copy, Paste, Duplicate, Bring to top, Send to bottom, New folder, Make a ghost, Boolean process (Off, Union, Subtract, Intersect), Delete and Delete other; on another object only Boolean process, which leaves the selection alone. A folder's long press opens the folder menu, which also shows or hides the polypaint of every object in it. To move a row, hold it until its menu opens and then drag it (the menu closes): a line shows where it goes, as in the Scene palette, and near the list's ends it scrolls along; a quick swipe scrolls the list instead. With a mouse a row drags at once. The eye works as in the Scene palette (dragging down the eyes sets each row's), and the polypaint toggle leaves the object's colours out of the drawing without losing them (saved with the scene from scene file version 11, which older versions of the app do not open). The bar under the list sets how tall the list is, for this run. Copy keeps the object as the scene file stores it (for a 10M-vertex object about 370 MB, which the log notes), so Paste (one undo step) works after the object is deleted or in another scene; Bring to top and Send to bottom move it to the ends of its folder, or of the list, as one undo step each; Delete other and Delete all are one undo step that brings every object back, and are refused with a note when the copies undo keeps would pass its 1.5 GB limit. All low and All high are not undo steps, as choosing a level is not. Scripts: `copy_object`, `paste_object`, `object_to_top`, `object_to_bottom`, `delete_other_objects`, `delete_all_objects`, `all_lowest_level`, `all_highest_level`.

**The Objects card's Actions** unfold under its list (folded at first, as ZBrush's card has them) and scroll in the room the list leaves. First is Store visibility, V1 to V8: each keeps which objects and folders are shown. Choosing one keeps the eyes as they are in the one chosen before and brings back its own; one never chosen takes the eyes as they are, so nothing changes until an eye is turned. The selected object always stays in view. They are not undo steps, as eyes are not, and are saved with the scene from scene file version 12, which older versions of the app do not open. Then come these sections, each folded until tapped:
- **Split** makes part of the selected object an object of its own, as one undo step. Split hidden takes its hidden part, Split masked its masked part (each triangle masked at least half at every corner), Groups split each polygroup, and Split to parts each connected piece; each is greyed out while there is nothing of its kind. The object keeps the first part (what is shown, what is unmasked, the first group or piece), and each other part becomes an object below it, in its folder, where it was. As with Slice, the levels, HD detail, mask, hidden parts and strokes go; the polypaint, the polygroups and the shown level's sculpt layers stay. More than 64 parts is refused with a note. Under dynamic topology it is greyed out.
- **Merge**: Merge down makes the selected object and the one below it in the list one object, in the selected one's place, and Merge visible adds one new object made of every shown object at the end of the list, leaving them as they are; the folder menu's Merge folder does the same for a folder's shown objects. Each object merged in keeps polygroups of its own, numbered on from the ones before, so Groups split takes a merge apart again; polypaint comes too. Merge down drops the selected object's levels and sculpt layers and is one undo step with the delete of the object below; Merge visible is refused with a note when the new object would pass the subdivide memory limit, or when the objects have more polygroups between them than one object can keep.
- **Transpose** says how the Move tool's gizmo moves, turns and scales several selected objects together (ZBrush's Transpose Master), and how a mask poses part of one.
- **Boolean**, **Project** and **Extract** are the Object palette's Booleans, Project detail and Extract sections, here too.
- **Align** has a row of three tiles for each axis, X, Y and Z: the low sides, the middles or the high sides line up. With several objects selected they line up with the first selected; with one, it lines up with the bounds of every shown object, as ZBrush aligns a subtool. Only the position along that axis changes.
- **Distribute** spaces objects evenly along an axis by their low sides, middles or high sides: the selected ones when three or more are, or else every shown object. The outermost two stay where they are. Align and Distribute are one undo step each.

Scripts: `visibility_slot v1` to `v8`, `split_object hidden|masked|groups|parts`, `merge_down`, `merge_visible`, `align_objects x_min` (or `x_centre`, `x_max`, then `y_` and `z_`) and `distribute_objects` likewise.

Scene palette, the objects: each row shows the object's name, size and levels ("base" on the object a boolean applies to), a ghost and an eye. A tap on a row selects it; a right-click or long press opens its menu (Focus, Duplicate, Rename, Solo, Make a ghost, Hide object, New folder, Move to a folder, Take out of folder, Delete). **Folders** gather objects one level deep, as ZBrush's SubTool folders: New folder (Ctrl+G) puts the selected object in one; its row's chevron folds it, its eye and ghost apply to everything in it, a tap selects its first object, and its menu renames it, ungroups it (its objects stay, out of any folder) or deletes it with its objects. A folder's objects always sit together in the list, which is the scene's order (the first object no boolean marks is the base). Rename (F2, or the menus) opens a field for a new name. **Dragging** a row by its grip (the dotted handle at its left, with any pointer; a finger on it never scrolls the panel) moves it: a line shows where it will go, an outline when it would go into a folder (let go over the middle of a folder's row). Under a folder's last object the line steps in, keeping the object in that folder, when the pointer is right of the indent. A folder's grip moves it with all its objects, between the rows outside folders. Near the top or bottom of the tray the list scrolls along. **Several objects** can be selected: Shift + click (or Ctrl + click) on rows, Shift + click on objects with the Move tool, the Scene palette's **Select several** switch (then a tap adds or removes, for a finger), or a folder's Select its objects. The last one added carries the gizmo and is the one every other panel and tool works on; the rest are drawn most of the way to the selected colour. The gizmo, the Object palette's Transform values and typed gizmo values move, turn and scale all of them together about the same point, keeping their spacing (a scale stops where any of them reaches its limit); Reset transform puts each back at the middle by itself; Duplicate copies each, keeping their spacing, and Delete takes all of them; each is one undo step. A Shift + click that misses every object keeps the selection. A plain tap, adding an object or anything else that picks one object leaves that one alone selected; a press on one of the selection with the Move tool keeps the rest and drags them all. **Column drag**: a press on an eye or a ghost dragged up or down the column sets every row it crosses the way the pressed one was going (a finger first holds still on it until its tooltip shows, as a quick drag scrolls the panel), so hiding or ghosting a run of objects is one sweep. Folders, moving into and out of them, moving rows, renaming and deleting a folder are undo steps; deleting a folder is one step that brings back the folder and its objects together. The **eye** takes an object out of view and out of reach (H hides the selected one and selects the next; Alt+H, or Show all objects under the list, shows every object again); the **ghost** dims it and lets presses through it, to sculpt another object against it as a reference. **Solo** (Shift+S, or the object menu) shows only the selected object, and whichever is selected next, until Solo is off again; the eyes stay as they were. As ZBrush's SubTool list, the selected object is always one in view and reach: hiding or ghosting it selects the next, and a tap on the row of a hidden or ghosted object brings it back and selects it. These are view state, saved with the scene but not undo steps; an object out of view still takes part in booleans, retopology and Project detail (the Booleans section says so), and Frame all frames only the objects in view. Hidden triangles inside an object are the Visibility section's (Ctrl+H shows them).

Scene palette: pick a shape (Sphere, Cube, Cylinder, Cone, Torus, Plane, or one of the starters below) and a size (41k to 25M vertices) and Add it; the Object palette's Remesh rebuilds the selected object as an even mesh of about the size picked there that keeps its shape (see Voxel remesh above). Geodesic spheres come in steps of 10f² + 2 vertices; the Scene palette shows the exact count it will build. The cube and torus are even grids, so their triangles follow their edges; the cylinder, cone and plane (a flat slab) are voxel remeshed from a smooth outline or a flattened box, which gives even triangles over the caps (their counts land within about a fifth of the one asked). Every shape is closed, so it subdivides and remeshes like a sphere. Flag: `--shape=NAME` starts with that shape instead of spheres. As voxels makes the Add button add a sphere of clay instead, of the resolution whose surface has about the size's vertices (see Voxel sculpting); the Objects card's Add menu has Voxel sphere too.

**Starters** are base meshes to begin a sculpt from, as ZBrush's and Nomad's are: Head, Bust, Figure (A-pose), Hand and Creature (four legs and a tail). They are made in code, so they need no files and carry no licence (`src/geom/starters.odin`): each is a few dozen ellipsoids and tapered tubes, fused into one closed surface by a voxel remesh (as fine as keeps the thinnest part, a finger or the tail, whole) and relaxed so the joins blend, then remeshed again at the size asked and relaxed a little more. Each faces the front (+Z) with +Y up and, all but the hand, is symmetric across X, so X symmetry works on it at once. Added at a size, a starter arrives with subdivision levels: as many as keep its base at 15,000 vertices or more, up to three, so a 1M Head is a 16k base with three levels above it, to step down to for big changes of form and up for detail (the Scene palette says how many levels it builds). A starter is never built under 15,000 vertices. On the test VM a 1M Head takes about 3 s from the tap to sculpting.

**New scene** (Menu > New scene, or the palette's "New scene: Head" and so on) replaces the scene with one Sphere, Head, Bust, Figure, Hand or Creature at the Scene palette's Add size, in the starting view, with an empty History and no file to save to (Save then asks where), as opening a scene does. When the scene has work to lose (any undo step, or more than one object), the first tap only arms that button, which turns red and reads Discard?, with a note; a second tap on it within 4 s goes ahead, and the menu stays open between the two. A palette row needs running twice in the same way.

Orbit, as ZBrush: a left drag on empty space or in the safe zone along the window's edges (see Touch and pen), a right drag anywhere, or Alt + left drag. The view turns about the middle of the last stroke, which stays where it is on screen while everything turns round it, as ZBrush's Local and Blender's Orbit Around Selection do, so the part being worked on stays in view; before any stroke, and after Focus, Frame all or Reset camera, it turns about the selected object's middle, and a point behind the eye is passed over. A small dot marks it while the view turns. Settings > Camera > Turn about can make a drag that starts on the model turn about the spot under it instead (Pointer, as Blender's Auto Depth and Nomad's pivot on camera move; with Off the model, turn about the air on, a drag on empty space turns about the point under it as deep as the middle of the view, as Nomad's Allow air pivot, rather than about the last stroke), every orbit turn about the middle of the selected objects (Selected objects, as Blender's Orbit Around Selection in Object Mode and Nomad's Selection pivot) or of everything shown (Whole scene, as Nomad's Scene pivot), or about the middle of the view (View centre, as Blender with Orbit Around Selection off). Show the pivot dot (on by default) hides the dot, as Nomad can; a double tap's pin still pulses. Hold Shift while turning to snap to the nearest view square to the axes (front, back, sides, top or bottom, upright or at a quarter turn of roll), and a drag far enough jumps to the next. Pan: middle drag, or Alt or Shift + right drag. Zoom: mouse wheel, or Ctrl + right drag (up zooms in). The wheel zooms toward the middle of the view, or with Settings > Camera > Wheel zooms toward the pointer on, toward what is under the pointer, which stays there (Blender's Zoom to Mouse Position), as a pinch keeps what is between the fingers. Settings > Keys can make the buttons work as Blender or Maya has them instead. The orbit goes all the way round both ways, over the top too, with no jump and no flip at the poles: Turntable (the default) turns about the model's up axis, Trackball about the screen's axes, and X axis and Z axis sideways about the world's X or Z axis (Settings > Camera, with the speeds and inverting). P (or keypad 5) switches between perspective and orthographic (parallel) projection, as wide at the orbit centre as the perspective view, so the object keeps its size on screen. Keypad 1, 3 and 7 look from the front, right and top, with Ctrl from the back, left and bottom (also in the Scene palette's Camera section). In orthographic projection the far-chunk simplification while orbiting is off. Camera history: Alt+Left (Previous view) goes back to the view before and Alt+Right (Next view) forward again, as a browser goes back and forward through pages, without touching the work or its undo; they are also in the Camera menu (which stays open to step several), the palette and the gesture map's choices. A view counts once the camera has stayed still 0.4 s with nothing turning it, whatever moved it (a turn, pan, zoom, pinch, Focus, Frame all, an axis view, Reset camera), and the view a scene opens with counts at once; going back from a view that has not rested yet keeps it first, so Next view returns to it, and a new view after going back drops the ones ahead. The last 32 are kept; the projection is not part of a view, and opening a scene or making a new one starts afresh (they are not saved). Script: `ortho_toggle on`, `view_axis front` (or `back`, `right`, `left`, `top`, `bottom`).

## Touch and pen

Fingers and the pen work as in ZBrush for iPad (`src/app/touch.odin`, `src/app/navigate.odin`, with every setting in Settings > Touch):
- One finger on the model sculpts, as the mouse does, once it has been down 90 ms (0.25 s while double taps are on) or moved past the slop, so a second finger landing with it makes a gesture before any clay moves. One finger on empty space turns the view, and keeps turning it over the model. Settings > Touch > On the model > Turns the view leaves sculpting to the pen.
- The pen sculpts on the model with pressure, and on empty space turns the view (or waits for the model). While the pen is near the screen or on it, a single finger does nothing (the hand holding it); the pen coming down cancels a touch in progress. On the iPad, whose system itself ignores a hand resting on the screen while the Pencil is in use, only the Pencil's tip and the fifth of a second after it lifts (when the heel of the hand lands as a stroke ends) make a finger the palm: with the Pencil hovering just above the screen, a finger of the other hand turns the view at once, and the hovering Pencil moves nothing while that finger is down (`pen_holds_hand` in `src/app/touch.odin`; `--system-palms` runs a desktop the same way). Not yet tried on an iPad. The pen leaving takes its hover with it: no name or highlight stays where it left. SDL 3.4 on the iPad never says that the Pencil left (nothing in its UIKit driver sends the leave), so there the app says it itself (`pencil_leave` in `src/app/pencil.odin`): SDL reports a hovering Pencil whenever it moves or turns, which a hand never stops doing for long, so half a second without a report while its tip is up counts as it having gone, and a finger works again from then on (a Pencil held quite still over the screen that long counts as gone until it moves). `--pen-no-leave` runs a desktop the same way, for `scripts/touch_check.py`. Not yet tried on an iPad.
- Two fingers pan with their middle, zoom as they spread or pinch, toward the point between them, and twist to roll the view about that point once they have turned 10 degrees (in Trackball, and in Turntable with Settings > Camera > Twist two fingers to roll the turntable on, `turntable_roll 1` in the settings file). A rolled turntable still turns the model about its up axis, and reads a drag along the rolled screen, so the model follows the finger whatever the roll; its roll holds level within 4 degrees of level, catching up by 8, so twisting back finds level again without a jump. Three fingers drag up and down for the brush size and sideways for its focal shift (400 points across its range), whichever way they go first; strength is a drag up or down on the brush bar's Strength. A quick tap of two fingers undoes, of three redoes (by default: see the gesture map below). Held still (landing together, within 0.15 s), two fingers undo again and again: once at 0.45 s (its own time, not the Hold delay, so fingers resting before a pinch do not undo), with a ring filling round them from 0.15 s on, then every 0.12 s with a count beside the ring, until a finger moves or lifts; three redo the same way. A hold never starts while the pen is near the screen, and once it undoes the touch neither pans nor zooms.
- Put two fingers down and lift one, and the other turns the view once it moves past the slop, about the same pivot an orbit would use, so a view filled by the model can still be turned with one finger. Only after two fingers that stayed still: the finger left rolling off the end of a pinch turns nothing. A two-finger tap still undoes unless the finger left turned the view.
- A double tap of one finger (the second tap within 0.3 s and 48 points of the first) on empty space frames the selected object, or every object when none is selected. On the model it pins the pivot to that point, with a pulse to show where: every orbit turns about it, whatever Turn about says, until the next stroke ends or the view is framed or reset. Neither tap sculpts: while one finger sculpts, a single tap's dab waits out the 0.3 s for a second tap, then lands as it was tapped (same view, brush and modifier buttons), or at once when anything else comes first (a key, the mouse, the pen, a finger elsewhere or a stroke). Taps in the safe zone along the edges never double-tap, nor do the presses that draw mask or hide shapes. A quick tap's dab keeps the modifier buttons latched or held at its press.
- The modifier wheel at the bottom left (`src/app/modifiers.odin`, `src/ui/wheel.odin`) does what Shift, Ctrl and Alt do, for a tablet with no keyboard. Its top sector is Smooth, its left Mask, its bottom Invert and its right opens the brush pie (as Space does), in Sculpt: **each space has its own dial**, as each asks something else of the off hand. Model's is Snap (Shift, the Smooth modifier, which snaps a Model drag to steps), the pie, Alt (Ctrl, the Invert modifier, which gives an action its variant) and the Select action on the left; Render's is Place highlight (the command Place_Light: it turns the Lights section's switch on and opens that section, so the next press on the model moves the picked light's highlight there, and lights its sector while on), the pie, Exposure (a tap opens its slider and its -1, 0 and +1 stop steps) and Pick focus. A space switch lets go of a latch the new dial holds no sector for. Library > Dial (see Arranging) changes the current space's; the settings file (version 6) keeps Sculpt's as the `dial_sector` lines an older build reads as its one dial and the others' as `dial_space <space> <sector> modifier|command <name>` lines, and a file from before version 6, when one dial served every space, gives Model the sectors changed on it and Render the commands among them. Exit stats: `dial_sectors=` (the current space's), `dial_spaces=` (how many differ from how they come), `exposure=` and `light_place=`. In Sculpt Smooth and Mask on together are Hide, as Shift and Ctrl held together are. Smooth smooths (Shift; held down by the other hand while the pen or mouse is mid-stroke, it lays a straight line instead, as Shift does then, and lifting it does not latch it), Invert inverts the brush (Ctrl), Mask paints the mask on the model at the Mask brush's strength and draws the Mask section's rectangle or lasso from empty space (Ctrl from empty space), and Hide (Smooth and Mask together) runs the gesture tool picked in Hide, clip and slice (Ctrl+Shift): a drag hides, clips or slices, a tap on a polygroup isolates it and a tap off one shows everything. Invert with Mask erases the mask and unmasks shapes; with Hide it flips the gesture as Alt does. With Mask on, where a tap lands chooses a mask command, as ZBrush's Ctrl click and Nomad's taps while Mask is held: off the model it inverts the mask, on a masked part it blurs the mask, on an unmasked part it sharpens it (by the Mask section's Steps), each with a toast that offers Undo; a tap paints no dot, so a mask stroke begins once it moves past the slop or is held 0.25 s. With the mouse, Ctrl and a click on empty space invert the mask too (Ctrl on the model still inverts the brush).
